ACF African Competition Forum
4
I am privileged to pen down these opening 
words to present the African Competition Forum’s 
(ACF) study report on trade flow patterns in the 
agriculture and agro-processing sector.  This 
report is the outcome of the collaborative effort of 
the six participating ACF member agencies from 
Angola, Kenya, Mauritius, South Africa, Zambia 
and Zimbabwe. 
Agriculture is a key sector for Africa and high on 
the continental development agenda aimed at 
achieving inclusive and sustainable economic 
development.  Foremost, agriculture provides 
food security and contributes to economic growth. 
The share of agriculture is about one-third of the 
continent’s total output and provides employment 
and thus livelihood to around fifty percent of its 
population of nearly 1.5 billion people. 
The agricultural sector is facing several challenges 
owing to disruptions in global trade due to geo-
political conflicts and adverse climatic conditions, 
amongst others. The sector is also not immune 
to anti-competitive practices, which may be 
compounding the negative impact of the supply 
shocks. All economies around the globe are facing 
the consequences with rising prices for agricultural 
inputs and food in general. Africa is likely to be 
much more affected given its vulnerabilities with 
more than 400 million of the population living in 
extreme poverty. 
In response to the various challenges and to 
achieve the continent goal of an integrated, 
inclusive and sustainable Africa, it is imperative to 
boost agricultural production and foster regional 
trade. In this regard, this ACF cross-country study 
into agricultural trade flows and regional value 
chains attempts to provide critical insights on the 
competition dynamics and regulatory landscape 
for the selected key agricultural products. It 
identifies several common barriers or limiting 
factors which impede the efficiency and growth 
of agricultural trade in the continent. Amongst, 
infrastructure 
deficiencies, 
regulatory 
and 
bureaucratic impediments, restrictive tariff and 
non-tariff measures and market access issues. The 
report also highlights climatic and environmental 
challenges affecting regional trade.
The findings of the study are intended to inform 
the relevant stakeholders on how to enhance 
regional trade in agriculture through pro-
competitive policies. Amongst, the potential for 
developing and expanding smaller intra-African 
trade volumes further within the continent. 
It will also inform future research and policy 
development for the achievement of an inclusive 
and sustainable Africa.
I would like to thank all the contributors from 
the participating competition agencies and in 
particular, the Competition Commission of South 
Africa for the overall coordinating for the conduct 
of the study and publication of this report. 
Mr Deshmuk Kowlessur
Chair of African Competition Forum

ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS

ACF African Competition Forum
6
The ACF analysis of trade flow patterns and 
regional value chains in the agriculture and 
agro-processing sectors aims to deepen the 
understanding of trade dynamics across key 
African countries, addressing the critical role 
of agriculture in fostering regional economic 
resilience and growth. 
Agriculture is a foundational component of 
African economies, contributing significantly 
to food security, employment, and industrial 
development. However, recent global crises, 
including the COVID-19 pandemic and the Russia-
Ukraine conflict, exposed vulnerabilities within 
global food supply chains, causing sustained 
food price inflation that disproportionately affects 
lower-income households. These developments 
underscore the need for improved regional trade 
resilience, competitive markets, and stronger 
intra-African trade to mitigate global disruptions
.
Against this backdrop, this ACF study focuses on 
analysing trade flow patterns in agriculture and 
agro-processing across six countries: Angola, 
Kenya, Mauritius, South Africa, Zambia, and 
Zimbabwe. By examining the import and export 
dynamics in these countries, the study explores 
the economic implications of agricultural policies, 
sectoral dependencies, and the role of key 
commodities within local and regional economies. 
Additionally, it assesses the regulatory frameworks, 
market barriers, and competition challenges 
affecting the sector, providing a comprehensive 
understanding of opportunities and obstacles 
within African agricultural trade.
The study seeks to provide a regional perspective 
on the economic activity within agricultural and 
agro-processing sectors across Africa, using 
trade flow data to identify opportunities for 
strengthening economic integration, competitive 
practices, and regional collaboration. More 
specifically, the study’s objectives are:
•	
Analysing regional value chains: The study 
explores 
the 
dynamics 
of 
agricultural 
and agro-processing value chains within 
ACF member countries, focusing on both 
high-volume 
and 
strategically 
important 
commodities, including maize, sugar, coffee, 
tea, and fertilisers. This analysis extends 
to trade (import and export) flows across 
different 
geographic 
levels—continental, 
regional, and national.
•	
Investigating competition and trade barriers: 
It also examines competition issues within 
value chains, with the aim of identifying 
interventions that could facilitate regional 
trade and improve competitive conditions. The 
study considers factors such as tariff and non-
tariff barriers, as well as market concentration, 
which can hinder trade efficiency.
•	
Highlighting 
opportunities 
for 
regional 
trade: The research also seeks to locate 
regulatory and infrastructural elements that 
either facilitate or impede regional trade 
in agriculture, identifying areas for future 
research to support policy interventions at a 
regional level.
In terms of the methodology employed in the 
research, the study combines the quantitative 
trade flow data of each country’s key agricultural 
products and the qualitative insights, in order 
to map trade patterns and competition issues. 
Data selection focuses on products central to 
the average African household’s consumption 
patterns, and products critical to food security 
and economic resilience, including fertilisers, 
vegetable oils, grains, protein foods, and agro-
processed products. Each product was analysed 
INTRODUCTION

ACF African Competition Forum
7
within its value chain context to identify relevant 
supply chain factors, including pricing volatility 
and dependencies on imported agricultural 
inputs, particularly fertilisers.
Statistical data on these key agricultural products’ 
imports and exports are analysed to assess the 
competitiveness of specific agricultural sectors, 
including critical products such as maize, sugar, 
tea, and poultry. The primary data in the study 
comprises of export and import volumes and 
values, using annual statistics sourced from each 
country’s national statistics databases or trade 
data sources, such as ITC Trade Map. The trade 
data generally spans the 2017-2022 period, 
allowing for an analysis of any trends before, 
during and after the Covid19 pandemic. The 
secondary data for the study includes economic 
reports, market studies, and other publications, 
providing contextual understanding of observed 
trade and price trends.
The participating countries in this analysis represent 
a 
cross-section 
of 
agricultural 
economies, 
each with distinct trade dependencies and 
competitive landscapes. Zimbabwe’s agriculture 
sector, for instance, emphasises value addition, 
especially in maize and soya, while focusing on 
enhancing self-reliance within the regional trade 
framework. Zambia leverages its vast arable land 
for agriculture but remains reliant on imports 
for staple food security, due to logistical and 
infrastructural constraints. Angola is expanding its 
agricultural capacity with a focus on coffee and rice 
exports, while working to reduce dependencies 
on 
imported 
agricultural 
inputs. 
Mauritius, 
although limited in agricultural resources, plays 
a critical role in sugar and processed food trade 
within Africa, using regional imports to support its 
agro-processing industry. South Africa has a well-
developed agro-processing sector and serves as a 
major regional trade hub, but faces concentration 
issues within certain agricultural sectors, impacting 
local prices and market access. Kenya’s diversified 
agricultural sector includes globally competitive 
tea and coffee exports, but also faces challenges 
in overcoming non-tariff barriers within the East 
African Community (EAC) and the rest of Africa.
The report is structured to provide an analysis 
of each country’s agriculture trade landscape. 
Following 
this 
introduction, 
each 
chapter 
presents an examination of the trade patterns 
and competitive dynamics for each country in the 
following order: Angola, Kenya, Mauritius, South 
Africa, Zambia, and Zimbabwe. Each chapter 
explores the specific agricultural commodities 
central to each country’s economy, discussing the 
associated trade patterns, market dynamics, and 
regulatory factors that influence these sectors.
The report concludes with an overarching chapter 
that synthesises key insights from the country 
chapters, 
identifying 
unique 
and 
common 
aspects across the countries, as well as differences 
and trade opportunities. This final section also 
discusses the structural barriers and facilitators 
to agricultural trade, and highlights opportunities 
for enhancing regional trade within the African 
Continental Free Trade Area (AfCFTA). By 
emphasising both the competitive and integrative 
aspects of African agricultural trade, this report 
provides a framework for understanding and 
advancing agricultural trade across the continent.

ACF African Competition Forum
12
1.	
The study on the Value Chain of the Agricultural 
and Agro-Processing Sector is the result of an 
initiative by the African Competition Forum 
(ACF). 
2.	
It should be noted that the study is pertinent 
insofar as food shortages have become 
a reality at global level, as a result of the 
negative effects of the constant increase in 
the world’s population. Studies predict that 
the world’s population will exceed 9 billion by 
2050, a growth that will lead to an increase in 
demand for food by more than 70%.1
3.	
Against 
this 
backdrop, Africa 
has 
the 
opportunity to become the main source of 
food supply globally, due to the favourable 
natural conditions and the strong potential for 
agri-food production on this continent.
4.	
The continent boasts a growth rate of 5 per 
cent a year, which could result in a tripling 
of its Gross Domestic Product (GDP) by 
2030.2 At this rate, Africa’s GDP could surpass 
Asia’s by 2050. With direct links to the rest 
of the economy, agribusiness and the agro-
processing industry could become the main 
catalysts for boosting Africa’s economic 
growth to the desired levels.
5.	
What’s more, the PricewaterhouseCoopers 
(PwC) report on Agribusiness in Angola states 
that this branch of business contributes 65% 
of employment and 75% of domestic trade in 
Africa.
1	
PricewaterhouseCoopers Angola (2020). Leadership in a global future, Agribusiness in Angola. Available at: https://
www.pwc.com/ao/pt/assets/img/pwc-angola-agronegocio.pdf . Consulted on October 25, 2023.
2	
Ditto.
3	
World Bank (2022). Towards better water and food security: Angola’s potential as a future agricultural power in Africa. 
Available at: https://blogs.worldbank.org/africacan/towards-improved-water-and-food-security-angolas-potential-
future-agriculture-powerhouse . Consulted on October 25, 2023.
4	
IMF (2021). Food Inflation in Sub-Saharan Africa. Available at: https://www.imf.org/en/Blogs/Articles/2021/12/06/food-
inflation-in-sub-saharan-africa . Consulted on October 25, 2023.
6.	
If it continues to grow at this rate, Africa’s GDP 
could surpass Asia’s by 2050. With direct links 
to the rest of the economy, agribusiness and 
the agro-processing industry could prove to 
be the catalysts needed to drive that level of 
growth on the African continent.
7.	
In this sense, agriculture and agribusiness 
are becoming increasingly relevant, as food 
security and job creation become increasingly 
relevant topics. Companies in the agribusiness 
sector in Africa need to be prepared for 
the contours of the industry in which they 
operate, namely the external factors that have 
a determining impact, and the dynamic “rules 
of the game,” in order to become leaders and 
true players of industry reference.
8.	
Angola has the potential to become one of 
the leading agribusiness countries in Africa, 
due to its fertile and rich soils and favourable 
natural 
conditions, 
suitable 
for 
animal 
husbandry and the planting of varied crops. 
However, currently only 10% of the 35 mllion 
hectares of land available for agricultural 
production are being cultivated. 3
9.	
Furthermore, 
in 
the 
current 
context, 
characterised by an environment of high 
food 
inflation,4 
competition 
and 
the 
effective application of Competition Law 
are fundamental to keeping prices low for 
consumers.
1
INTRODUCTION

ACF African Competition Forum
13
2.1.	 Objectives of the Study
10.	 The general objective of this study is to 
understand the existing regional economic 
activity in the agriculture and agro-processing 
sector. In specific terms, the study aims to 
achieve the following objectives:
•	
Understand the dynamics of regional value 
chains in the agriculture and agro-processing 
sectors;
•	
Understand 
competition 
issues 
along 
regional value chains, especially cross-border 
issues and what interventions are needed at a 
regional level;
•	
Understand the competition issues that 
can arise due to the impact of restrictions 
such as tariff and non-tariff barriers, weak 
infrastructure, demanding quality standards, 
as well as local suppliers developing weakly, 
and how this hinders inclusion in regional 
value chains.
11.	 To ensure alignment with the other members 
involved in preparing the respective study, it 
will be limited, essentially, to the analysis of 
some products contained in the following 
categories: agricultural inputs, vegetable 
oils, grains and legumes, protein foods, fruits, 
drinks and other merchandise.
2.2.	 Study Methodology
12.	 In 
preparing 
the 
study, 
the 
following 
methodological 
instruments 
were 
used: 
a bibliographical review; surveys of some 
companies and associations; documentary 
and statistical analysis; as well as research 
on the main websites, namely Ministry of 
Agriculture and Forestry (MINAGRIF), Ministry 
of Industry and Commerce (MINDCOM), 
National Statistics Institute (INE), General Tax 
Administration (AGT), Ministry of Finance 
(MINFIN), National Bank of Angola (BNA), 
World Bank (BM), United Nations Food 
Support Fund (FAO), French Development 
Agency (AFD) and Business Associations in 
the sector.
13.	 The quantitative data, whether primary or 
secondary, presented in graphs and tables/
figures refers to the periods from 2017 to 
2022.
2.3.	 Study Structure
14.	 To achieve the research objectives, the 
study in question is segmented into eight 
parts, including the introductory note, which 
constitutes the first part, and the overview of 
the study, which is considered the second 
part.
15.	 In the third part, a characterisation of the 
agricultural and agro-processing sector in 
Angola is carried out, with emphasis on 
identifying the commercial flow of agriculture; 
in the fourth section, the commercial flow of 
the agriculture and agro-processing sector in 
Angola is presented.
16.	 In the fifth part, an analysis of the prices 
of agricultural products is carried out at 
national level, while in the sixth section, the 
commercial flow of Angola’s agriculture and 
agro-processing sector at an African and 
global level is identified.
17.	 In the seventh section, an assessment of 
competition issues in the agriculture and 
agro-processing sector is made; and finally, in 
the eighth part, some final considerations are 
presented.

STUDY
OVERVIEW

ACF African Competition Forum

18.	 The 
agricultural 
sector 
in 
Angola 
is 
characterised as having a vast area of arable 
land, of about 35 million hectares favourable 
for farming, and is considered to hold the 
largest agricultural potential in Southern 
Africa. There are fertile, rich soils and 
favourable natural conditions, but according 
to the French Development Agency (AFD), 
only 17% of hectares is actually cultivated.5

19.	 Furthermore, the country has rivers with flow 
superiority among the largest in the world, 
with 77 river basins and 43 hydrological 
basins, in addition to having millions of 
family farmers who play a fundamental 
role in food production, and sustainable 
management of natural resources. These 
farmers, predominantly linked to family 
farming, contribute significantly to supplying 
agriculture produce to the domestic market.6

between its exploration potential and the

with idleness in the order of 84%.

a prevalence of exploitation of the primary

not characterised by market competitive

23.	 As far as business holdings are concerned, 
more than half of the different livestock and 
aquaculture holdings have a tractor as their 
main agricultural equipment. The equipment 
that follows the tractor is less than half as 
representative, namely (plough, motor pump 
and backpack sprayers). Around 41.3% of the 
business holdings have a warehouse for their 
products, 23% have breeding stables/courts, 
and 12% have vaccination pens. It should also 
be noted that, from a market point of view, 
only 11% of holdings are entrepreneurial, 
indicating concentrated markets and low 
levels of investment.

Angola are cassava, corn, beans, reindeer and 
sweet potatoes, soybeans, bananas, coffee,

agricultural production and private sector 
involvement, such as the National Medium-
Term Development Plan for the Agricultural 
Sector (PDMPSA).

from the National Statistics Institute (INE) 
from the fourth quarter of 2022 indicates 
that around 50% of the employed Angolan 
population is engaged in agriculture, animal 
production, hunting, forestry and fishing, 
which is illustrated in figure 4 below.7

34.	 This section takes a detailed look at the supply 
chain segments of Angola’s agricultural and 
agro-processing sector. Starting with the 
overall supply structure, the supply chain of 
the agricultural sector in Angola is segmented 
as follows: inputs, production and/or import, 
transformation and storage, distribution, 
marketing and consumption, with each of 
these activities presenting a different market 
structure.11
Figure 10 - Agricultural Sector Supply Chain
Source: PwC Angola (2021), ARC (2024)
4.1.	 Supply of Inputs 
35.	 Due to its characteristic as the initial basis 
for production, the input supply constitutes 
the first stage of the agricultural production 
chain. Inputs are made up of various elements 
used in production, that is, everything that is 
provided to make production possible namely 
– seeds, soil improvers, fertilisers, agricultural 
pesticides, animal feed, veterinary medicines, 
tools and equipment, agricultural machinery 
and implements, and technology.
36.	 Angola imports a large part of its agricultural 
inputs, 
spending 
on 
average, 
around 
US$26,10 million annually on imports.12 This 
segment faces several challenges, including 
difficult access to more modern, efficient seed 
varieties that guarantee greater food security 
and reduce soil contamination.
4
COMMERCIAL FLOW OF THE 
AGRICULTURE AND AGRO-
PROCESSING SECTOR IN ANGOLA

ACF African Competition Forum
21
37.	 Furthermore, the high costs of importing 
fertilisers, seeds, agrochemicals and parts for 
the main machines that operate in the field are 
highlighted as the main factors contributing 
to the weak development of the agrarian 
sector in Angola.13
4.2.	 Production
38.	 Agricultural production includes the entire 
range of products and benefits generated 
by agricultural activity. It is the basis for 
maintaining the world economy. After all, it is 
from agriculture that food and raw materials 
for industries are produced.14

41.	 Data from the Angolan Embassy in Portugal 
shows that in the 2019/2020 agricultural 
campaign, 3,071,085 tons of the five types 
most consumed cereals in the country were 
produced (including corn, pennisetum gray, 
sorghum, rice and wheat), representing 
a growth of 5.6% (171,980 tons more) 
compared to the previous period.16
42.	 Most of this production was carried out in the 
province of Huambo, with 27.8%, followed by 
Cuanza Sul (22.9%), Bié (16.1%), Benguela 
(11.1%) and Huíla (8.7%), in an area of 
2,903,111 hectares (3.6% more), where the 
average harvest was around 1,058 kg per 
hectare.

ACF African Competition Forum
22
43.	 Of the total production, corn led the top 
5 in the cereal sector, reaching 2,972,177 
tons, an increase of 5.4 percent (160,497 
more) compared to the previous harvest, 
which resulted in the cultivation of around 
1,367,200 tons. Of the total corn production, 
2,375,653 tons of corn were produced by 
Family Agricultural Companies (FAC), while 
Corporate 
Explorations 
(CAE) 
harvested 
596,524 tons, on an average of 1,143 kg per 
hectare.
44.	 Rice production amounted to 10,567 (4.6%) 
tons, distributed in terms of production by 
5,336 tons for FAC and 5,231 tons for CAE, 
resulting in around 1,337 kg per hectare. 
Wheat totalled 9,368 (2.1%) tons, with 9,172 
tons from FAC and 196 tons from CAE, with an 
average harvest of 715 kg per hectare.
45.	 Despite also registering an increase in 
production, legumes and oilseeds reached 
only 606,698 tons (+5.5%), cultivated on 
1,037,644 hectares (+2.9%), with an average 
harvest of around 585 kg per hectare (+2.5%), 
in the provinces of Huambo (19.5%), Bié 
(17.9%), Cuanza Sul (16%), Uíge (11,3%) and 
Malanje (7.1%).
46.	 In this group, the production of beans, one of 
the most consumed foods in Angola, topped 
the list of three food goods, having totalled 
344,762 tons (+5.8%), 304,533 of which were 
from FAC and 40,229 from CAE, with a record 
average harvest of around 512 kg per hectare.
47.	 Soybean production also increased by 1.6% 
to 37,961 tons, 25,698 for the FAC and 
12,263 for the CAE, 1,064 kg per hectare. The 
agricultural sector recorded the production 
of 12,100 tons of coffee and 6.050 tons of

48.	 However, the agricultural production sector 
experienced some difficulties and challenges, 
such as: poor protection against pests and

precarious 
irrigation 
systems 
(with a high dependence on rain); lack of 
maintenance and replacement services for 
machines; as well as a low level of training of 
staff.17
4.3.	 Transformation and Storage 
49.	 In addition to production, the sustainability 
of the agricultural sector is also based on 
the ability to transform products via agro-
processing, as well as their conservation by 
way of appropriate storage. 
50.	 In 
Angola, 
the 
transformation 
process 
includes, for example: 
•	
Corn Processing: transformation into corn 
flour, starch and other products. 
•	
Coffee Processing: Coffee processing involves 
drying, peeling and classifying the beans. 
•	
Cassava Processing: Transformed into flour, 
starch and other products. 
•	
Palm Oil Production: Palm oil is extracted 
from palm trees and is used in cooking and 
industry. 
•	
Tropical Fruit Processing: Fruits such as 
bananas, pineapples and mangoes are 
processed into juices, pulps and jams. 
51.	 Table 5 below  lists the main agricultural 
products 
which 
undergo 
transformation 
and are processed in Angola, separated by 
product origin (either of plant origin or animal 
origin).

52.	 The main stored agricultural products include 
cereals (such as corn and rice), legumes, roots 
(such as cassava) and fruits. Storage facilities 
range from traditional barns to modern 
structures such as silos and cold rooms. 
53.	 In Angola, the transformation (processing) and 
storage segment has faced several challenges, 
including high fuel and electricity costs; 
costs of industrial material for transformation 
and processing; lack of adequate storage 
facilities; as well as low level of staff training.19

4.4.	 Distribution and Marketing of Agricultural 
Products (Food)
55.	 The 
distribution 
phase 
is 
responsible 
for getting agricultural products to the 
intermediate and/or final consumer. At 
this stage, there are two groups operating: 
wholesalers and retailers.
56.	 The Angolan market has many distributors 
of agricultural and processed products, the 
main companies being: Angoalissar, Grupo 
Carrinho, 
Mercadoro 
Angola, 
Customs 
Warehouse and Sodosa.
57.	 Likewise, 
the 
following 
main 
retailers 
(supermarkets) in Angola stand out: Maxi, 
Shoprite, Arreiou, Jumbo, Kibabo, Kero, 
AngoMart, Alimenta Angola, Deskontão, 
Fresmart and Bem Barato, as well as some 
large 
informal 
markets 
distributed 
by 
provinces, namely the markets of Km 30 and 
Catinton (Luanda), 4 de Abril (Benguela), 
Germany 
(Huambo), 
Mutundo 
(Huila), 
São Pedro (Cabinda), 5 de Abril (Namibe), 
Chawandi (Malanje), Tchissindo (Bié) and 
Chingo (Cuanza Sul).
58.	 As PwC points out, the main challenges of the 
food distribution and marketing markets in 
Angola include the low quality of roads and 
lack of integration of transport networks, the 
low cost of imported products compared to 
national products, and the high incidence of 
the informal market.21

4.5.	 Consumption
59.	 Despite Angola having a vast amount of 
arable and/or fertile land, a large part of the 
food consumed in the country is imported.
60.	 However, there is a greater demand in coastal 
areas. Furthermore, a significant portion 
of national production, mainly agricultural, 
comes from the interior of the country, which, 
in turn, faces low demand for the respective 
products.
61.	 This 
situation 
is 
aggravated 
by 
the 
existence of poor logistical infrastructure 
and limited traffic routes. These factors 
result in difficulties and restrictions in the 
connection between production areas and 
large consumption centres. Consequently, 
the efficient functioning of markets and the 
competitiveness of national production are 
harmed, which indirectly encourages the 
importation of goods.
62.	 Data from the General Tax Administration 
(AGT) indicates that from 2017 to 2022 the 
country imported various agricultural and 
agro-industrial goods worth 5.76 billion 
dollars,22 as shown in the table below.23 The 
table also shows that the total imports of 
agricultural and agro-industrial goods has 
decreased at a compound annual rate of 
around 18%, during the period from 2017 to 
2022.

63.	 Also, it should be noted that, according to the 
BNA, between 2017 and 2022 Angola spent 
around 16,11 billion dollars on importing 
food.24
64.	 The importation of food goods is carried out 
by complying with measures established in 
Presidential Decree No. 213/23, of October 
30, which approves the Legal Regime for 
Incentives to National Production. Among the 
measures, the following stand out:
•	
the supply of national products enjoys priority 
over imports;
•	
the import process must be preceded by 
consultations with the national market, in 
order to determine the existence of the goods 
to be imported;
•	
authorisation is subject to demonstration of 
the prior conclusion of purchase contracts 
for 
national 
production, 
the 
existence 
of initiatives aimed at direct or indirect 
investment, or other forms of promoting 
national production, as well as the effective 
settlement of purchases made from producer 
nationals, or the existence of a guarantee of 
their future settlement;
•	
contracting 
public 
entities 
and 
their 
contracted 
suppliers 
must, 
preferably, 
purchase goods produced in Angola, and 
they will only be purchased from importers 
after all possibilities of purchasing from 
national producers have been exhausted. This 
means that the State must resort to importing 
products only when there is no availability in 
the national market.

ACF African Competition Forum
26
25	
The monitored price regime arises from the need to correct distortions in the formation of prices for certain goods or 
services, because of price changes not justified by normal market conditions.
26	
MINFIN (2022). Monthly Price Report Products under Regulated Price Regime. Available at: https://www.ucm.minfin.gov.
ao/cs/groups/public/documents/document/aw4z/ndix/~edisp/minfin3421790.pdf. Consulted on April 27, 2023.
65.	 Most of the food consumed in Angola is 
subject to the Monitored Price Regime. This 
regime is established by Presidential Decree 
no. 206/11, of 29 July, which defines the 
General Bases for the Organisation of the 
National Price System, with Article 9(2) having 
been reworded by Presidential Decree no. 
113/16, of 30 May, in which the monitored 
price now takes the form of a reference price, 
determined on the basis of the cost structure 
of the respective good or service25.
66.	 The list of goods and services subject to 
the monitored price regime was published 
through Executive Decree no. 256/20, of 30 
October.26 Among the goods, the following 
stand out: sugar, rice, wheat flour, beans, 
cornmeal, pasta.
67.	 However, the “rules and procedures for setting 
and changing prices” practised in the different 
regimes, and the mechanisms for carrying out 
price control and inspection activities, are 
set out in Executive Decree no. 77/16, of 25 
March.
68.	 In Angola, trade data shows that there is 
a steep rise in food prices from 2018 to 
2022, because of rising exchange rates. 
Furthermore, according to the Association 
of Modern Commerce and Distribution 
Companies 
of 
Angola 
(ECODIMA), 
commercial establishments, supermarkets, as 
well as industrial units in various parts of the 
country operate with electrical generators, 
which increases companies’ costs, reflecting 
in increasing prices. This trend of overall 
increased prices over time is seen in the table 
below, showing average prices of food goods 
from 2018 to 2022.

69.	 In the last 6 years, Angola has seen high 
levels of food inflation, reaching a historic 
maximum of 27% in 2021, and a minimum of 
approximately 14% in 2022. As can be seen 
in figure 9 below, food prices have almost 
doubled since the start of the Covid-19 
pandemic in the country.

The homologous inflation rate is the price of the whole basket in a certain month compared to its prices in the same 
month a year before.

most important components in the Consumer 
Price Index (CPI) are food and non-alcoholic 
drinks, which in 2022 represented 54% of the 
total weight. This means that the cost of these 
items has a significant impact on the country’s 
overall inflation.27

72.	 An important element for the sustainability of 
Angola’s agricultural development process 
lies in its relationship with the outside world, 
and the competitive insertion of the economy 
in the international context.
73.	 Angola is part of the international trade 
system,30 which is why it consumes products 
from different countries around the world, 
thus giving the country’s inhabitants the 
possibility of satisfying their consumption 
needs for goods and services that the country 
does not produce itself.
74.	 Within the framework of bilateral partnerships, 
Angola develops an extensive cooperation 
activity with all countries that, strategically, 
correspond to the country’s political, social 
and economic challenges. This is aimed 
at the country´s development through the 
establishment of agreements of a different 
nature and public investment projects and 
private investment.
75.	 According to AGT statistical data, the 
Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is one 
of Angola’s main export partners. However, 
between 2020 and 2022, there was a 
reduction in this commercial partnership. In 
2017, trade between Angola and the DRC 
totalled to more than 13 million dollars,31 
which will fall to 1.14 million dollars in 2022.

77.	 As can be seen in tables 7 and 8 above, 
the country registered more imports than 
exports, which indicates a deficit in its trade 
balance and the domestic production of 
these agricultural goods.
6.1.	 Analysis of Angola’s trade flows at African 
and global level by product
78.	 The tables presented above show exports and 
imports by product category in a generic way. 
However, this subchapter presents trade flows, 
by product, between Angola and the rest of 
Africa, and the world. Due to data limitations, 
only a few products will be analysed, namely 
fertilisers, seeds, rice, wheat, corn, coffee, tea 
and sugar.

ACF African Competition Forum
31
6.1.1.	 Fertilizers
79.	 Among the essential factors for the success 
of agriculture, fertilisers and agrominerals 
emerge as key factors that drive the 
productivity and growth of the Angolan 
agricultural sector. 
80.	 Between 2020 and 2022 Angola exported 
around 2,313 tonnes (equivalent to US$ 2.43 
million) to the world, of which 2,204 tonnes 
(equivalent to US$ 2.40 million) was exported 
to the rest of Africa. As can be seen from the 
figures below, Angola purchases most of its 
fertilisers outside the African continent, and 
is a net importer of fertiliser in terms of its 
fertiliser trade with the rest of the world.

imported from Africa.

6.1.4.	 Rice
93.	 “Rice is one of the most consumed foods in the 
world. Rice production in Angola continues to 
be below national consumption needs, a fact 
that leads to the import of this product on 
the international market”.32 Despite this, and 
given its comparative advantages, Angola has 
exported part of its internal rice production to 
Africa and to the world.
94.	 Data from the International Trade Centre 
(ITC) indicate that in the period from 2020 
to 2022, Angola exported around 820 tons 
of rice to the world (the equivalent of 249 
thousand US dollars), of which around 796 
tons (the equivalent of 220 thousand dollars) 
are exported to Africa. This shows that most 
of the rice exported by Angola was destined 
for Africa.

121.	 Overall, after analysing Angola’s trade flow 
at two levels, continental and global, for the 
products mentioned above, it is observed 
that trade exchanges in the period under 
study between Angola and the rest of the 
world presented values below the agricultural 
potential that the country presents, as was 
noted in the introduction.
122.	 On the other hand, the data presented 
shows that Angola has more trade relations 
with countries outside the African continent. 
Furthermore, there was an unbalanced trade 
balance, that is, during the period under 
analysis, Angola’s imports were greater 
than exports, showing the country to have 
a net importer status in relation to the key 
agricultural products considered.
123.	 This situation is due to the fact that there 
are some factors that limit the development 
of the agricultural and agro-processing 
sector, as well as the export of the respective 
products, namely:

ACF African Competition Forum
45
•	
For all agricultural sectors, WTO sanitary 
and phytosanitary (SPS) requirements are 
generally very demanding, especially in 
developed country markets. Furthermore, 
there are trade barriers in terms of quality 
standard requirements and certification 
requirements;35
•	
For any consumer goods, meeting quality 
and safety standards are challenging, as 
well as certification requirements, packaging 
and labelling regulations, especially in 
developed country markets, particularly due 
to the poor infrastructure (in terms of quality) 
in Angola.
35	
African Development Bank (2021). Study on Diversification of Angolan Exports. Available at: https://www.afdb.org/sites/
default/files/2022/02/17/export_diversification_study_2019-03-20_pt.pdf . Consulted on March 15, 2024.

ACF African Competition Forum
46
124.	 In recent years, the Angolan State has faced 
challenges in the food subsector, due to 
the national and international economic 
situation. The Production Support, Export 
Diversification, 
and 
Import 
Substitution 
Program 
(PRODESI) 
aims 
to 
improve 
families’ access to food by stabilising prices, 
promoting production and the food industry 
in general, and creating an efficient model 
for importing and exporting basic products.
125.	 Furthermore, ensuring healthy competition in 
the subsector constitutes a crucial challenge 
for the Angolan economy, and it is necessary 
to make the market more competitive and 
attractive for different companies and, 
consequently, for private investment.
126.	 In this context, with a view to supporting 
family agricultural businesses, plus micro 
and small industries, the Angolan State 
provides incentives, through Presidential 
Decree No. 213/23, of 30 October, which 
has materialised initiatives to facilitate and 
promote access to credit.
127.	 In point 3 of article 3 of the aforementioned 
Decree, the State grants incentives to facilitate 
and promote access to credit for national 
producers, transporters, industrialists and 
traders who establish alliances among 
themselves, in the form of consortia of 
various types, cooperatives, as well as other 
forms of cooperation in the development of 
productive activity.
128.	 Despite the underlying public interest, 
paragraph 3 of article 3 of the respective 
Presidential Decree establishes measures 
restricting competition, by encouraging 
national production and, thus, favouring 
the Angolan business community to the 
detriment of other market operators.
129.	 This standard has the potential to distort 
competition in the food market, as it creates 
inequality 
of 
circumstances 
between 
competitors, a condition contrary to article 
3 of the Competition Law (LdC), which 
establishes that in the competition process 
companies must compete on equal terms in 
order to attract their respective customers.
130.	 Article 4 of the above-mentioned Decree 
establishes that the import process must be 
preceded by consultations with the national 
market on the existence of the goods to be 
imported.
131.	 Furthermore, 
the 
State 
conditions 
authorisation of imports on demonstration 
of the prior conclusion of contracts for 
the purchase of national production, the 
existence of initiatives aimed at direct or 
indirect investment, or other mechanisms to 
promote national production. Additionally, 
the effective settlement of purchases made 
from national producers or, exceptionally, the 
guarantee of future settlement is required.
132.	 From a competition point of view, these 
measures create obstacles for companies 
wishing to import food, as they can be 
considered as excessive requirements that 
make it difficult for operators to enter this 
area of economic activity.
133.	 The import flow becomes slower because 
the signing of purchase contracts for 
national production does not depend solely 
on the company that wants to import. The 
investment initiative is also linked to financial 
capacity, something that companies are 
7
COMPETITIVE
ANALYSIS

ACF African Competition Forum
47
generally able to meet. Furthermore, the 
RJIPN does not establish a specific deadline 
for the Ministerial Department responsible 
for trade to issue the binding opinion.
134.	 Furthermore, article 5(1) of the same 
provision, 
establishes 
that 
companies 
must submit annually, until the 15th of 
August, to MINDCOM information relating 
to the next 12 months on, “input needs, by 
national producers and purchase intentions 
of consumer goods by importers,” for 
publication purposes.
135.	 By 
making 
this 
information 
available, 
companies have the opportunity to share 
data and, consequently, coordinate practices 
to fend off their competitors. This can result 
in higher prices and a reduction in product 
variety, creating a business environment 
that is unfavourable to competition between 
companies.
136.	 Another 
consequence 
of 
exchanging 
information between companies is that it 
reduces uncertainty regarding competitors’ 
strategies, 
and 
affects 
commercial 
independence between them.
137.	 Furthermore, article 6(1) of the same statute 
establishes that national producers must 
enter their price information, as well as the 
quantities and quality of their production, 
on MINDCOM’s portal, while Article 6(3) 
states that the data provided is subject to 
registration and subsequent dissemination.
138.	 This measure may constitute a practice that 
restricts competition, as it may facilitate 
the conclusion of prohibited collective 
agreements between producers, risks of 
coordination and price increases, as well as 
a decrease in production and a reduction 
in the variety of products, which ultimately 
disadvantages end consumers.
139.	 In 
turn, 
the 
Strategic 
Food 
Reserve 
Regulation (RREA), approved by Joint 
Executive Decree no. 208/19, of 9 August, 
determines in its article 4 that the Strategic 
Food Reserve (REA) must be managed by 
an entity contracted for this purpose, whose 
terms of service provision must be agreed to 
by the State, represented by the respective 
oversight bodies.
140.	 However, with regard to the management 
instruments, Article 12(1) and (2) of the 
RREA requires operators to register annually 
with the REA Management Entity (EGREA), 
that is, Entreposto Aduaneiro E.P., in the 
following categories: seller, buyer or reseller 
of designated products, in order to allow it 
to maintain a database made up of national 
and foreign companies involved in the 
formal circuit of the food subsector, namely 
in the production, processing, distribution 
and commercialisation of the products listed 
in the annual purchasing plan.
141.	 The standard gives EGREA the power to 
control the main market operators, due to 
the size and strategic importance of REA 
in the food market, which can attract many 
companies operating at different stages of 
the value chain, making them, in a certain 
way, dependent on REA.
142.	 It should be noted that paragraph 3 of the 
same article requires registered operators 
to provide EGREA with data on prices and 
stocks of the goods they produce, process 
or store.
143.	 It is important to highlight that the EP Bonded 
Warehouse – a vertically integrated public 
company, has a broad corporate purpose 
related to the food sector, namely general 
trade, storage, wholesale distribution of 
food, and management of large warehouses, 
among others.
144.	 In this context, the power granted to EGREA 
can become a competition problem in the 
context of relationships between economic 
agents present at different levels of the value 
chain.

ACF African Competition Forum
48
145.	 Therefore, it is important to examine the 
clauses of the contract signed between the 
parties, to verify whether it guarantees a 
structural separation of EGREA’s actions, 
avoiding activities throughout the value 
chain and, consequently, distortions of 
competition.
146.	 As part of the survey carried out, the 
Association 
of 
Modern 
Commerce 
and Distribution Companies of Angola 
(ECODIMA) presented some competition 
concerns, namely:
i.	
the lack of a legal regime for the 
movement of goods;
ii.	
excesses 
in 
inspections 
and 
the 
application of heavy fines in commercial 
activities, with a product costing U$D 
500.00 being penalised with fines 
that reach around U$D 45,000.00, 
increasingly aggravating the economic 
and financial situation of companies;
iii.	 the existence of national producers/
wholesalers who do not observe the 
commercial chain and the rules of 
commercial activities, selling directly 
to consumers at the same price that 
they supply to distributors and/or 
retailers, becoming competitors of their 
customers;
iv.	 the discrepancy in opportunities, such 
as greater ease of access to credit for 
foreign companies, to the detriment of 
national companies;
v.	
restriction of access, by companies 
associated with ECODIMA (around 75 
companies), to REA food products, for 
reasons unknown to this entity, which 
constitutes discriminatory treatment;
vi.	 Propagation of lobbies and price fixing, 
as well as the promotion of competitive 
distortions, through the allocation of 
certain products to the informal market.
36	
National Radio of Angola (2024). Angola and DRC will standardize customs fees at customs posts. Available at: https://
rna.ao/rna.ao/2024/02/13/angola-e-rdc-vao-uniformizar-taxa-aduaneira-nos-postos-fronteiricos/ . Consulted on March 
15, 2024.
147.	 With regard to concentration operations, 
in the period between 2020 and 2023, the 
Angolan Competition Regulatory Authority 
(ARC) analysed seven notifications of mergers 
of companies in the agricultural sector, 
namely: Rovic & Lonrho Mês, Fazenda Kikuxi, 
Sociedade 
Agro-Pecuária 
do 
Bailundo, 
Grupo Edson Drov’s, Telegest & Matadouro 
M. do Porto Amboim, DPI & Grupo Solevo 
and IEP & Fazendas (Quizenga, Quimba and 
Longa). The respective operations consisted 
of the merger and acquisition (vertical and 
horizontal integration) of companies.
148.	 The above companies are of national and 
foreign origin (Mauritius, United Kingdom, 
France) and carry out production, distribution 
and marketing activities of fertilisers, seeds, 
agricultural 
products 
and 
equipment, 
instruments and parts.
149.	 However, from the analysis carried out, it 
was concluded that the operations do not 
present potential harm to effective or future 
competition, nor do they create or reinforce 
a dominant position that would allow them 
to generate unilateral or coordinated effects, 
resulting in a non-opposition deliberation or 
operation.
150.	 As for regional trade relations, there is a 
discrepancy in terms of customs fees. It 
is noteworthy, for example, that “Angolan 
trucks pay 4,000 dollars (around 3,734 euros) 
at the customs post in the DRC compared to 
100 dollars (93 euros) for the Congolese at 
the Angolan border.”36
151.	 Although customs tariffs are used to protect 
national interests, their excessive application 
can have negative competitive and economic 
impacts, as it limits access to foreign products 
and restricts consumer choice, in addition to 
jeopardising commercial relations between 
these countries.

ACF African Competition Forum
49
152.	 Agriculture has a directly proportional 
relationship with the economic growth of any 
region. It serves as a source of raw materials 
for the manufacturing industry, absorbs 
labour (even the least qualified), provides a 
source of food for the population, as well as 
a source of foreign exchange for the country.
153.	 Undoubtedly, the promotion of agriculture 
makes it possible to improve the current 
inflationary situation that most African 
countries face. The war in Ukraine has 
shown that Africa, despite its agricultural 
potential, is still very dependent on the West, 
especially when it comes to the production 
and purchase of agricultural products, 
which jeopardises the continent’s food and 
nutritional security.
154.	 Angola’s agricultural sector is the largest 
employer in the economy, with around 
50% of the workforce. Family farming is 
predominant and uses 96% of the entire 
area occupied for exploration. Business 
exploration presents a large gap between its 
exploration potential and the area actually 
used, with idleness in the order of 84%. 
155.	 Furthermore, the Angolan agricultural sector 
faces several difficulties and/or challenges, 
namely: insufficient agricultural inputs and 
fertilisers, poor protection against pests 
and diseases, precarious irrigation systems 
(high dependence on rain), lack of quality 
infrastructure for flow, transformation and 
storage of products, lack of maintenance 
and replacement assistance for machines, 
low level of staff training, restrictions in the 
connection between production areas, and 
large consumption centres.
156.	 This 
situation 
contributes 
to 
the 
underdevelopment 
of 
the 
agricultural 
sector in Angola, resulting in a low supply 
of national products in the domestic market 
and, 
consequently, 
a 
dependence 
on 
imported products.
157.	 From the analysis carried out, it is clear that 
Angola’s imports of agricultural and agro-
processed products are higher than its 
exports, and that the country has few trade 
relations with other African countries, since 
the majority of imported products come 
from outside the continent.
158.	 With the exception of the sixth chapter, 
in which some barriers to accessing the 
international market were highlighted, the 
data obtained did not allow for a competitive 
analysis by product, limiting the study to 
the levels/phases of the value chain. This 
situation meant that competition concerns 
were analysed in a specific chapter, and not 
highlighted in each section of the study.
159.	 The challenges that the Angolan State 
has faced in the agricultural sector and, 
specifically, in the food subsector, due to 
the national and international economic 
situation, have led to the adoption of 
strategies and the approval of laws and 
regulations to improve the current situation.
160.	 However, 
such 
measures 
can 
create 
restrictions on competition in the market, 
limiting the efficient operation of companies 
and, therefore, negatively affecting the quality 
of life of the population, making it necessary 
to observe and respect competition law 
in tandem with the required policies and 
regulations needed for economic and 
agricultural development in Angola.

CONSIDERATIONS

1.	
The agriculture sector in Kenya plays a vital 
role in the economy. It contributes 20% 
of the Gross Domestic Product (GDP) and 
indirectly contributes 27% of the same 
through its linkages with other sectors. The 
sector employs more than 40% of the total 
population, and more than 70% of Kenya’s 
rural population.1 The agricultural sector 
productivity in Kenya is the second highest 
in East Africa. Agricultural products represent 
65% of Kenya’s total exports. The country is 
the third-largest tea exporter globally, and the 
largest exporter of horticulture in East Africa. 
Kenya’s agricultural exports include traditional 
agricultural exports (tea and coffee) and 
non-traditional agricultural exports such as 
vegetables and cut flowers.2
2.	
Besides being the backbone of the economy, 
agriculture is the main driver of non-
agricultural sectors including manufacturing, 
and provides inputs and markets for non-
agricultural operations such as building/
construction, 
transportation, 
tourism, 
education, and other social services. Kenya’s 
Vision 2030 identifies agriculture as one of the 
six key economic sectors expected to drive the 
economy. This goal will be realised through 
the promotion of innovative, commercially-
oriented and modern agriculture.
3.	
Given the importance of the sector in the 
economy, 
strengthening 
and 
improving 
the performance of the sector is therefore a 
necessary condition for achieving recovery 
and growth of the national economy. 
Hence, the Government’s vision to achieve 
agricultural 
transformation 
and 
inclusive 
growth is outlined in the Agricultural Sector 
Transformation Strategy and the Bottom-up 
Economic Transformation Agenda (BETA).3
4.	
The BETA proposes to transform 2 million poor 
farmers into surplus producers, through input 
finance and intensive agricultural extension 
support; to enhance the productivity of key 
value chains; and to reduce the dependence 
on basic food imports by 30%, through 
supporting local production and revitalisation 
of export crops.       
5.	
The development of agriculture is equally 
important for poverty reduction, since most 
of the vulnerable groups like pastoralists, the 
landless, and subsistence farmers, depend on 
agriculture as their main source of livelihood. 
Growth in the sector is, therefore, expected to 
have a greater impact on a larger section of 
the population. The development of the sector 
is therefore important for the development of 
the economy as a whole.
1.1.	 Overview of Imports and Exports of 
Agriculture and Agro-processing 
6.	
The African Competition Forum (ACF) initiated 
a cross-country study of food value chains 
in agricultural and agro-processing, and the 
analysis of trade data at different regional 
levels. The study aimed to understand 
concentration and competition issues within 
regional and continental value chains in 
agriculture and agro-processing. The focus 
on agricultural and agro-processing value 
chains stems from their importance for all 
1
INTRODUCTION

ACF African Competition Forum
54
African economies, and the current high food 
inflation environment that continues to place 
pressure on consumers. 
7.	
Kenya’s major agricultural exports are tea, 
coffee, cut flowers,4 and vegetables. The 
country is a net importer of agricultural 
products. Agricultural exports to Kenya 
reached $3.26 billion in 2021. The country 
faces supply deficits in most of its food sectors, 
and it uses instruments under the Common 
Market for Eastern and Southern Africa 
(COMESA) and East African Community (EAC) 
agreements, which both provide for high 
non-member tariffs on sensitive commodities 
including meat, dairy poultry, maize, rice, 
wheat, and beans to import food products. 
8.	
According to the Kenya Economic Survey 
2023, imports from Africa rose from USD 1.76 
billion in 2021 to USD 2.10 billion in 2022, 
4	
 The main cut flowers grown in Kenya are roses, carnations, and Alstroemeria
occasioned by an increase in imports from 
Mozambique, which more than doubled, 
South Africa (38.5%), Uganda (16.9%) and 
Zambia (33.7%). Imports from the EAC 
accounted for 40.1% of total imports from 
Africa having risen by 64.5% to USD 703. 42 
million in 2021. 
9.	
Based on the foregoing, Kenya focuses on 
coffee and tea, which are the country’s main 
export products, and maize and sugar, whose 
importation rose recently to cushion the 
country from the high cost of living and to 
ease shortages. Some of the countries that 
Kenya exports coffee to are the Democratic 
Republic of Congo (DRC) and Mauritius, while 
we export tea to South Africa, Mauritius, and 
Zambia. The country also imports maize from 
Tanzania, Zambia and Uganda.

Kenyans, and are the most widely consumed. 
Tea and coffee are among Kenya’s most 
important export commodities, and major 
contributors to the nation’s economy.

1.2.	 Study Objectives
11.	 The study’s general objective is to improve 
opportunities for investment, growth, and 
price competitiveness within the context of 
 
continental and regional integration initiatives. 
The specific objectives of the study are:
i.	 	 To understand the dynamics of regional 
value chains in ACF member countries’ 
agriculture and agro-processing sectors. 
The focus is to explore the trade (export 
and import) flows of countries within the 
region, identify what food value chains 
are being traded currently, and at what 
price(s) along the value chain;
ii.		 To understand the competition issues 
along regional value chains, particularly 
cross-border issues and what interventions 
are needed at a regional level; and
iii.	 To understand competition issues that 
may arise because of the impact of 
constraints such as tariffs and non-tariff 
barriers, weak infrastructure, demanding 
quality standards, and weakly developed 
local suppliers, and how that hinders 
inclusivity into regional value chains. 
1.3.	 Scope of the Study
12.	 The study explores the Kenyan trade (export 
and import) flows to countries within the 
region and at a continental level, and identifies 
what food value chains are being traded 
currently, and at what price(s) along the value 
chain. Kenyan main crops of focus are coffee, 
tea, maize, and sugar, and export and import 
of the same within the African continent.

ACF African Competition Forum
56
13.	 The study adopted quantitative research 
methods, and used both primary and 
secondary data. Primary data was obtained 
from various government agencies, including 
the Ministry of Agriculture, the Kenya National 
Bureau of Statistics (KNBS), the Ministry of 
Investment, Trade and Industry (MITI), and 
the Agriculture and Food Authority (AFA). 
Secondary data was sourced from various 
Kenyan economic surveys, publications, and 
reports. Data has been analysed descriptively 
using MS Excel, and presented in tables and 
graphs.
14.	 The agriculture sector in Kenya is regulated 
by the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock 
and Fisheries, which develops and enforces 
policies 
and 
legislation. 
Some 
of 
the 
functions of the ministry are, inter alia: 
formulation, implementation, and monitoring 
of agricultural legislation, regulations, and 
policies; development, implementation, and 
coordination of programmes; and regulating 
and quality control of inputs, produce, and 
products. The legislations are highlighted 
below: 
a.	
Agriculture and Food Authority Act No. 13 of 
2013 – The purpose of this Act is to guide the 
promotion, development, and regulation of 
food crops. It provides for the establishment 
of the Agriculture and Food Authority, which 
is charged with the regulation of all food 
crops in Kenya.
b.	 The Crops Act No. 16 of 2013 – The main 
aim of the Act is to accelerate the growth 
and development of agriculture, enhance 
the productivity and incomes of farmers and 
the rural population, improve the investment 
climate and efficiency of agribusiness, and 
develop crops as export crops.
c.	
Crops (Food Crops) Regulations, 2019 – The 
principal objective of the Regulations is to 
promote the development and regulation 
of food crops and food produce, and to 
specifically provide for the registration of 
growers  and growers’ associations; certify 
processing plants, warehouses, collection 
centres, and markets; the issuance of export 
clearance permits, ensuring the safety and 
quality of food crops and food produce; the 
marketing of food crops and food produce; 
the promotion of best practices in production, 
2

57
transportation, 
storage 
and 
processing 
of food crops and food produce; and the 
collection and maintenance of a database 
on the production, prices, and trade of food 
crops and food produce.
d.	 Pest Control Products Board Act – This Act 
regulates 
the 
importation, 
exportation, 
manufacture, distribution, and use of products 
used for the control of pests and the organic 
function of plants and animals and connected 
purposes, and for that purpose it establishes 
the Pest Control Products Board.
e.	
Kenya Agricultural and Livestock Research 
Act, 2013 – The Act provides an administrative 
framework 
for 
agricultural 
research 
in 
Kenya, and provides for the promotion 
and coordination of agricultural research 
activities in the country. It establishes the 
Kenya Agricultural and Livestock Research 
Organisation (KALRO).
f.	
Kenya Plant Health Inspectorate Service 
Act of 2012 – It is an Act that ensures the 
protection of plants, seeds and plant varieties, 
and agricultural produce. It further regulates 
the quality of agricultural inputs and produce, 
to prevent adverse impacts on the economy, 
the environment, and human health.
g.	 Standards Act – The Act  makes provision 
for the promotion of standards in Kenya 
established by the Kenya Bureau of Standards 
(KEBs) which is mandated to ensure that the 
standards development process is aligned 
with the technological, economic, and social 
priorities of the Kenyan Nation and the 
provisions of the Standards Act. All imported 
products 
must 
satisfy 
Kenyan 
import 
requirements before export, and it is KEBs 
that issue the certificate of conformity.
h.	
Competition Act No. 12 of 2010 – The 
Act regulates market conduct and market 
structure. It contains provisions on restrictive 
trade practices, regulation of mergers, abuse 
of a dominant position, and consumer welfare. 
15.	 The country’s regulatory regime in the 
agricultural 
sector 
does 
not 
impede 
competition in agricultural markets, but rather 
accommodates and encourages competition 
in Kenya’s agricultural and agro-processing 
industries. Further, the regulatory environment 
promotes the exportation of agricultural 
produce including coffee, tea, and fruits and 
vegetables, among others.

4.1.	 Trade flow within the Agriculture and Agro-
processing Sector in Kenya 
17.	 Agricultural Trade plays an important role 
in providing a living for farmers and people 
employed along the sector’s supply chains. It 
also contributes to  reducing food insecurity 
in the country, and provides greater choice in 
consumer goods.5
Figure 4.1: Agriculture growth rate in Kenya from 2018 to 2022
Source: KNBS Economic Survey 2023
18.	 According to Figure 4.1, the agriculture 
growth rate is seen to have been fluctuating 
in the last five years. The rate declined from 
6.1% in 2018 to 2.8% in 2019. The agricultural 
sector in Kenya did not grow in 2021 and 2022, 
despite significant growth in 2020 during the 
COVID-19 pandemic period, during which 
the sector expanded by 5.2%. The decline 
was largely occasioned by drought in most 
parts of the country, which severely affected 
agricultural production.6

world demand for Kenyan tea, coffee, fruits, 
and vegetables.10 Kenya has seven major

23.	 Historically, Kenya has had significant trade 
relations with countries in the EAC. The 
EAC is an intergovernmental organisation 
composed of seven partner states, namely: 
Kenya, Tanzania, Uganda, Rwanda, Burundi, 
South Sudan, and the Democratic Republic of 
the Congo. Kenya’s developed infrastructure 
and membership in both the EAC and 
Common Market for Eastern and Southern 
Africa (COMESA) make it the ultimate trade 
and investment destination. The country’s 
strategic location provides easy access to the 
EAC market. Kenya’s trade with the rest of the 
region has been facilitated by factors such as 
trade agreements.12 
24.	 Kenya’s 
imports 
from 
African 
countries 
account for 11% of all its imported goods, 
and are worth US$ 2 082 billion. The most 
important supplying markets are Tanzania 
(24%), Egypt (21%), South Africa (19%) and 
Uganda (15%). Among the products imported 
from these countries are cereals and sugars.13

27.	 This section discusses coffee, tea, sugar, 
and maize sub-sectors in Kenya, including 
the production, import, and export of these 
products to countries in Africa. 
5.1.	 Coffee Sub-sector in Kenya
28.	 The coffee industry plays a key role in the 
Kenyan economy; it contributes about 1% 
to the GDP, 8% of the total agricultural 
export earnings, and up to 30% of the total 
labour force employed in the agriculture 
sector. The sector’s key role is recognised 
in 
the 
Government’s 
efforts 
to 
fight 
poverty, and is targeted as a sector that can 
contribute towards the realisation of Kenya’s 
development blueprint, Vision 2030.14 This 
blueprint aims to transform Kenya into a 
newly industrialising “middle-income country 
providing a high-quality life to all its citizens 
by the year 2030.” 
5.1.1.	 Coffee Production in Kenya
29.	 Coffee-growing areas are located within the 
Western, Rift Valley, Central Kenya, and Mount 
Kenya regions. Kenyan coffee is produced 
under two systems, comprising smallholder 
farmers (co-operative societies) and coffee 
estates, which are individually managed 
coffee plantations. Coffee in Kenya is grown by 
over 800,00015 smallholder farmers clustered 
under 543 cooperative societies, and a total of 
about 3,000 coffee estates16 (categorised as 
small, medium, and large) involved in coffee 
production. The total annual production has 
been fluctuating wildly due to climate change. 
In 2021, Kenya experienced its worst drought 
in over 40 years.  Equally, due to climate 
change, harvest times and crop quality have 
become nearly impossible to control and 
predict.  As of 2022, coffee production stood 
at 51,900 tonnes.
Figure 5.1: Coffee production in Kenya (‘000 tonnes)
Source: KNBS Economic Survey 2023

30.	 Over the last five years, the production of 
coffee declined from 45 metric tonnes in 
2019 to 34.5 metric tonnes in 2021 (Table 
5.1 further below). According to KNBS,17 the 
decline is attributed to multiple factors, such 
as poor weather conditions in the coffee-
growing areas, inadequate application of farm 
inputs by smallholder farmers, and the shift in 
land use from coffee farming to real estate, 
among others. The production then increased 
by 50.4%, from 34.5 metric tonnes in 2021 to 
51.9 metric tonnes in 2022.  The increase was 
attributed to an increase in yields, coupled 
with improved crop husbandry practices.18

5.1.2.	 Marketing of Coffee
31.	 Coffee in Kenya is sold through two marketing 
agents: the Central Auction, who offer their 
services purely for commercial purposes, and 
Direct Sales, who are growers marketing their 
coffee. The central auction is administered by 
the Nairobi Coffee Exchange Management 
Committee, which is drawn from industry 
stakeholders.19
32.	 Direct sales are where the farmers sell their 
coffee directly to locals or overseas. However, 
in cases where farmers cannot market their 
products directly, commercial marketing 
agents facilitate the process by drawing 
up sales agreements between producers 
and buyers, and handling other marketing 
logistics.20
Figure 5.2: Earnings from Coffee exports in Kenya in USD million
Source: KNBS Economic Survey 2023
33.	 From Figure 5.2 above, earnings from coffee 
exports increased from USD 175.63 million 
in 2018 to USD 282.37 million in 2022, an 
increase of 60%. This was due to an increase 
in production partly attributed to conducive 
weather conditions in the coffee-growing 
areas, and improved crop husbandry.21

inconsistent over the last five years due to 
varying production quantities, which was 
attributed to unfavorable climate changes.

dividing the export values in USD by the 
export volumes in Kg, have been fluctuating 
over the last five years for different countries, 
as shown in Table 5.4 above. For instance, one 
kilogram of coffee was sold at USD 4.44 to 
South Africa in 2017, and it increased by 9% 
to USD 4.86 in 2022.
5.1.4.	 Coffee Sub-Sector Value Chain
38.	 The coffee value chain begins with production 
by farmers, who engage in land preparation, 
fertilising, spraying, tools maintenance, and 
harvesting. Once harvested, the coffee cherries 
are transported to factories for processing 
(dry or wet processing) and subsequently to 
commercial millers for milling and grading. In 
the processing stage, the cherry fruit or pulp 
is removed, leaving the seed or bean, which is 
then dried. The method used determines the 
flavours of roasted and brewed coffee. Once 
the pulp has been removed the bean is then 
left surrounded by two layers, the silver skin 
and the parchment. 
39.	 The parchment is then ready for sun drying on 
tables, where it is regularly turned – to obtain 
the bluish color for which Kenyan coffee is 

ACF African Competition Forum
66
famous. Drying the coffee is the last process 
on the farm. The fully dried coffee is bagged 
and ready to be sent to the mills. Grinding is 
then done on the beans depending on their 
size, location, and altitude at which they were 
grown, how they were prepared and packed, 
and how they tasted, or their cup quality.22 
40.	 At the mills, the parchment skin surrounding 
each bean is removed, followed by mechanical 
grading of the coffee into seven (7) separate 
grades according to the size, weight, and 
shape of the bean. It is then graded by the 
number of imperfections (defective beans, 
pebbles, and sticks) per sample.  Following 
this, a secondary processing of the coffee 
takes place. 
41.	 The graded beans are passed to the coffee 
marketing agents, who market them through 
an auction at the Nairobi Coffee Exchange 
(NCE).  Dealers consequently purchase from 
22	
The Coffee Industry in Kenya. 2023.  https://www.kenyarep-jp.com/en/business/coffee/#:~:text=Almost%20all%20
Kenyan%20coffee%20is,to%20remove%20the%20outer%20skin
the auction and sell to the exporters (Dada, 
2007). The Auction happens every Tuesday 
of each month annually, and the data on the 
prices per grade offered by various dealers 
who participate in the Auction is published 
online. 
42.	 At the Auction, the coffee marketing agents 
are allowed to have price negotiations on 
the coffee presented as per the reserve 
price, and these are normally listed as ‘noted’ 
transactions, and if they agree on a price, they 
report the same to the NCE – or if they do 
not agree the marketer can present the same 
coffee to the auction the following Tuesday. In 
instances where the dealer and the marketer 
agree on a price on the floor, the sale is 
confirmed. 
43.	 The processes of the value chain and the 
market players involved in the processes are 
summarised in Figure.
Figure 5.5: Coffee Sector Value Chain and Players in charge of the process

ACF African Competition Forum
67
44.	 The coffee subsector has been central to the 
economy of Kenya since independence. At 
one time it was a leading foreign exchange 
earner for the country, a scenario that has since 
changed, relegating it to the number four 
(4) foreign exchange earner. Subsequently, 
the government embarked on a reform 
journey to restore the sub-sector to its lost 
position.23 The purpose of the renewed effort 
by the government is to ensure that farmers 
benefit from their crops, and that the country 
increases its offering to the global markets.
45.	 The reforms, which started in June 2023, 
included having dealers purchase coffee 
through the Nairobi Coffee Exchange auction, 
as the government moved to revoke the 
licenses  of cartels who had infiltrated the 
sector. These reforms are geared at making 
the Kenyan coffee sector more competitive, 
by eliminating market failures and improving 
coffee production.
5.2.	 Tea Sub-Sector in Kenya
46.	 Kenya is the third-largest exporter of tea 
globally, after China and Sri Lanka, and the 
largest exporter of black tea and the third largest 
producer, after China and India.24 The top 
exporters of tea in 2021 were China ($1.97B), Sri 
Lanka ($1.34B), Kenya ($1.2B), India ($709M), 
and  the United Arab Emirates  ($316M)25. 
23	
AFA Coffee directorate. 2023. https://coffee.agricultureauthority.go.ke/index.php/resource-center/licensed-coffee-
buyers?download=56:licensed-coffee-buyers

The 
tea 
industry 
makes 
an 
important 
contribution to the Kenyan economy. It is 
among the leading foreign exchange earners, 
contributing about 23% of total foreign 
exchange earnings, and 2% of the Agricultural 
GDP. Annually, the country produces over 450 
million Kg of tea, which earns the country over 
USD 912.55 million in export earnings, and 
USD 167.30 million in local sales. The industry 
supports about 5 million people directly and 
indirectly, while an estimated 650,000 tea 
growers depend on tea, making the industry 
one of the leading sources of livelihood in the 
country.26
5.2.1.	  Tea Production in Kenya 
47.	 According to Figure 5.6 below, Kenyan 
tea production has been fluctuating over 
the years. It increased by 24.1% from 
458.8 thousand tonnes in 2019 to 569.5 
thousand tonnes in 2020, on account of 
adequate rainfall in tea-growing areas27. 
The production then declined to 537.8 
thousand tonnes in 2021 and to 535 
thousand tonnes in 2022 (Figure 5.7). The 
lower production in 2022 was attributed to 
depressed and poorly distributed rainfall 
during the year, where rainfall amounts 
received were below the expected amount 
for the respective months.28

5.2.2.	  Tea Value Chain in Kenya
48.	 This section outlines the key players in the tea 
sector, and their interrelations. It attempts to 
show the role of each sector player in terms of 
how tea marketing is organised and operated, 
along the value chain from the producer to tea 
buyer/exporter – including through auction at 
the port of Mombasa.
5.2.2.1.	 Producers
49.	 Tea is produced by farmers who are categorised 
into small-holder or estate farmers. The small-
holder farmers are organised into factories 
where they are individual shareholders, and 
the factories are corporate shareholders of 
the Kenya Tea Development Authority (KTDA). 
The role of KTDA is to ensure that farm 
agronomics, processing, handling storage, 
and marketing of tea in Kenya can give the 
farmer adequate returns commensurate with 
their expanded efforts.29
50.	 The producers of tea do not participate in the 
Mombasa tea auction. They however enter 
into a contract with a broker who represents 
 
them at the auction, using a sample of tea 
from the farmer. The broker charges a fee 
for this service. The Kenya Tea Growers 
Association (KTGA) represents the farmers, 
with the mandate of promoting the common 
interests of the members in the cultivation 
and manufacture of tea, and inculcating good 
industrial relations and sound wage policies 
for the workers.30 KTGA members represent 
40% of total tea production, while KTDA 
represents 60% of the tea produced by small-
scale farmers in Kenya. 
29	
KTDA. 2023. https://ktdateas.com/
30	
KTGA. 2023. https://ktga.or.ke/background-information/
31	
EATTA. Tea Brokers. 2023. https://eatta.co.ke/eatta-membership/eatta-member-functions/149-tea-
brokers#:~:text=Collectively%2C%20the%20brokers%20form%20the,a%20proposer%20and%20a%20seconder.
32	
EATTA. Mombasa Tea Auction Process. 2023.  https://eatta.co.ke/the-mombasa-tea-auction  
5.2.2.2.	 Brokers/Agents 
51.	 The small-holder farmers sell their tea through 
brokers/agents at the public auction. They 
prepare catalogues for use at the auction. 
The farmers are members of the East Africa 
Tea Trader Association (EATTA), which is 
responsible for organising the Mombasa 
Tea Auction. The players are guided by the 
rules of EATTA, and they have a Tea Brokers 
Association.31
5.2.2.3.	 Buyers
52.	 The buyers are companies registered in Kenya 
and licensed by the Tea Directorate under 
AFA, to undertake the business of buying tea 
either at the auction, by private sale for export, 
or by sale to local tea packers.32
5.2.2.4.	 Warehouses
53.	 The warehouses are responsible for holding 
the tea on behalf of producers and buyers, as 
they bid at the auction. These are members 
of EATTA, and also have a warehousing 
association. The cost of warehousing is also 
partly borne by the tea farmers (producers).
5.2.2.5.	 Packers
54.	 These players blend and pack tea for the 
domestic market, and some for export. The 
packers source their tea mainly from buyers, 
after sourcing from the auction or brokers, and 
their target markets are normally domestic 
consumers. Figure 5.7 shows the players in the 
tea value chain.

5.2.3.	  Marketing of tea
55.	 Once the tea is produced by farmers, it is 
delivered to collection centres, transported to 
individual factories where it is processed, and 
then traded through:
I.	
Factory sales in normally small consumer packets;
II.	
Direct Bulk sales, from the factory through an 
agent or broker to packers and exporters; or
III.	 Auction sales from factories to buyers through 
agents/ brokers, who represent producers at 
the Mombasa Auction. 
56.	 A large proportion of tea trading takes place 
via the auction through the Mombasa Port.33 
All sale contracts are registered with the Tea 
Directorate within thirty days of finalisation of 
the contract.34 The auction operates under 
the rules of EATTA, which ensure fair play.35
57.	 Most of the tea produced in Kenya supplies 
the international market; 22%36 of the 
country’s

production 
is

key 
destinations 
include

5.3.	 Maize Sub-sector in Kenya
60.	 Maize is the most important staple food in 
Kenya. Farming is one of the most important 
agricultural 
activities 
in 
the 
country, 
contributing significantly to food security. 
Maize production has been on the decline 
over the past few years.38 
5.3.1.	 Maize Production and Importation
61.	 Smallholder farmers account for 70% of the 
country’s maize production.39 Production 
fluctuates, according to the Economic Survey 
of 2023. It declined from 44.6 million bags40 in 
2018 to 34.3 million bags in 2022, translating 
to a 23.1% decrease in total production due 
to climate-related factors such as unreliable 
rainfall and an increase in temperatures, 
drought, and related events. Figure 5.9 below 
illustrates the production of maize for the past 
5 years.

Figure 5.10: Maize Value Chain in Kenya42

ACF African Competition Forum
74
63.	 The maize value chain encompasses various 
stages, from production to consumption, 
involving different stakeholders. Figure 5.10 
illustrates an overview of the maize value 
chain in Kenya.  
5.3.2.1.	 Input suppliers
64.	 The farmers need inputs like seeds and 
fertilisers to enhance the crop yield and 
protect it from pests and diseases. They get 
those inputs from different sources, including 
the government.
5.3.2.2.	 Farmers
65.	 Farmers are made up of small-scale farmers 
and large-scale farmers. Largely, maize 
farming is carried out in Rift Valley, Nyanza, 
and parts of the western and eastern regions. 
The small-scale farmers operate in low-input 
and low-yield systems, but produce about 
70% of the national maize output. Large-scale 
farmers cultivate for commercial purposes 
and are heavy users of commercial inputs 
such as fertiliser, improved seeds, chemicals, 
and machinery. The large-scale farmers 
produce large volumes on more than 30 acres 
of land, and sell their grain to the National 
Cereals and Produce Board (NCPB) and large 
commercial millers.43
43	
International Livestock Research Institute (ILRI). Food safety landscape analysis: The maize value chain in Kenya. 2023.

of%20the%20maize%20value,make%20the%20sub%2Dsector%20sustainable

5.3.2.3.	 Marketers 
66.	 Maize 
marketing 
in 
Kenya 
consists 
of 
thousands of small assemblers, brokers, 
medium-scale wholesalers, large wholesalers, 
transporters, and retailers.
5.3.2.3.1.	
Assemblers 
67.	 Assemblers 
are 
the 
first 
commercial 
purchasers of maize from the field. They buy 
maize directly from farmers and then sell 
it to brokers. In some cases, they also act 
as purchasing agents of large commercial 
millers. They account for about 55% of sales 
by farmers.44
5.3.2.3.2.	
Wholesale traders 
68.	 Wholesale traders buy maize from assemblers 
in bulk, store and fumigate it, and then sell it 
to retailers or millers. They also sell it to the 
National Cereal and Produce Board (NCPB). 
The NCPB is a cereal purchasing, marketing, 
and price regulatory agency that ensures a 
year-round supply of cereals for the nation.
5.3.2.3.3.	
Millers 
69.	 Milling of maize is the main form of its value 
addition. Maize is milled to produce various 
products such as maize flour, maize meal, 
and other derivatives. Millers buy maize from 
farmers and traders to meet their consumption 
demands, but if it is insufficient, they turn to 
the NCPB.

ACF African Competition Forum
