5.4.	 Sugar Sub-Sector in Kenya
73.	 Sugar is among the top commercial crops in 
Kenya, alongside tea and coffee, currently. The 
sugar industry contributes 4.1% to the value 
of agricultural marketed production, and 5.9% 
of all crops’ marketed value. The industry 
supports the livelihoods of about eight million 
Kenyans directly or indirectly, and employs 
500,000 workers along the entire value chain.46 
5.4.1.	 Sugar Production and Importation in 
Kenya
74.	 Commercial sugarcane production in Kenya 
is concentrated in the western, Nyanza, Rift 
Valley, and coastal regions. Over 300,000 
farmers supply sugarcane to the millers. 
Over 94% of the cane supply is by out-
growers, the difference being supplied by the 
Nucleus Estates owned by the various milling 
companies.47 Sugarcane is the raw material 

ACF African Competition Forum
77
used for commercial sugar production in 
Kenya, with a range of varieties that mature 
between nine (9) and twenty-four (24) months, 
depending on altitude for the coastal and 
western regions, respectively.48
75.	 Currently, sugarcane is grown in 14 counties 
by an estimated 270,000 growers – in 
Bungoma, Kakamega, Busia, Trans Nzoia, 
Uasin Gishu, Nandi, Kericho, Kisumu, Siaya, 
Homabay, Migori, Narok, Kisii, and Kwale. 
These 
sugarcane 
growing-counties 
are 
broadly categorised into four zones: Nyando, 
South Nyanza, Western, and Coast. 
48	
Ibid 41.
49	
Agricultural Sciences Vol.10 No.11, November 2019. Overview of Sugar Industry in Kenya and Prospects for Production 
at the Coast. 2023https://www.scirp.org/journal/paperinformation.aspx?paperid=96541 
50	
KAM. Sugar Subsector Profile. 2023.  https://kam.co.ke/wp-content/uploads/2021/04/sugar-sub-sector-profile-2020.pdf 
76.	 Sugar production in Kenya has declined over 
the years, while the country’s sugar imports 
have been increasing. The annual domestic 
demand is over 900,000 tonnes,49 which 
makes the country a net importer of sugar. 
Kenya does, however, also export small 
quantities of sugar, mostly to South Sudan and 
Somalia. Most of the produced sugar is for 
domestic consumption.  Figure 5.12 indicates

from 458.6 thousand tonnes in 2019 to 320.7

78.	 A total of 796,554 MT of sugar was produced 
in 2022, the highest ever recorded in the 
sector. This was 14% higher than the 700,241 
MT realised in 2021. The increase is attributed 
to improved sugarcane supply in all sugar 
zones, and the coming into operation of the 
Naitiri and Mumias sugar mills. As earlier 
mentioned, the country is a net importer 
of sugar to meet its sugar requirements. 
COMESA countries are the main source of 
imported sugar in Kenya, and accounted for 
67% of total imports in the country in 2019.50

79.	 Kenya sources the bulk of its sugar imports 
from COMESA countries, as these countries53 
enjoy duty-free access to Kenya’s market 
up to an authorized quota.54 Kenya applies 
a 100% tariff on raw and refined sugar from 
outside the COMESA region. Some of the 
COMESA countries that Kenya imports sugar 
from are Uganda, Mauritius, Zimbabwe, and 
Egypt. Besides these COMESA countries, 
the country also imports sugar from South 
Africa and Eswatini. The countries from which 
Kenya imports most of its sugar are Egypt and 
Mauritius (See Figure 5.14 and Table 5.5).
5.4.2.	 Sugar Value Chain 
80.	 The key stages in sugarcane production 
involve land selection and farmer recruitment; 
land preparation; seed cane selection and 
planting; 
crop 
maintenance; 
harvesting; 
transportation; and cane weighing. Cane 
production is the first stage in the sugar value 
chain, and it has strong bearings on costs and 
the availability of sugar in subsequent stages 
in the sugar value chain. Sugar is produced in 
a sugarcane mill, when cane juice is extracted 
from sugarcane through the cane-crushing 
process to produce raw sugar (which at this 
stage is not fit for human consumption). 
81.	 Following this, impurities (and most of the 
molasses) and foreign particles are removed 
and the remaining juice is then crystallised 
into granular brown sugar, which is further 
refined to produce refined (white) sugar. 
Generally, brown and white sugars are 
used by both household consumers (direct 
consumption) and manufacturers such as 
those in the baking, confectionary, beverage, 
and food processing industries (industrial 
consumption).55

ACF African Competition Forum
80
5.4.2.1.	 Cane Harvesting, Transportation and Buying
Figure 5.14: Sugar Value Chain
56	
Ibid 41
57	
Ibid 41  
82.	  Cane Harvesting – Cane harvesting is mainly 
done manually by contractors assigned by 
various millers. Manually harvested cane is 
either piled, heaped, stacked, or windrowed. 
Mechanical harvesting has been adopted by 
Kwale International Sugar Company Limited 
(KISCOL). It is common to have cane of 
different maturities harvested together. This 
compromises the optimal yield at the farm, 
and sucrose content. The cane harvesting 
program is frequently affected by various 
factors, which include unscheduled mill stops, 
cane fires, and cane on small land sizes.56
83.	  Cane 
Transportation 
– 
Transportation 
is 
currently carried out largely by contracted 
parties, but the day-to-day programming and 
allocation of transport tasks is managed by the 
various transport departments of respective 
millers, whose key task is to match transport 
resources with cane demand. A few factories 
have their own transport fleets. In most mill 
areas the cost of transportation is based 
on 4 Km or 10 Km distance bands from the 
respective mills. These rates are reviewed 
from time to time by millers, transporters, and 
farmers. Transportation costs generally average 
between 11% and 29% of the total cost of 
production per tonne, and vary from one cane 
zone to another. In general, the average cost 
of transportation is unfavorable where low 
payload vehicles are used for haulage from 
far-flung areas, or where cane is 8 transported 
from small land sizes. Transportation of cane 
during wet weather is extremely difficult, more 
so in low-lying terrains and in areas with poorly 
maintained roads, where tractors require 
intensive winching.57

ACF African Competition Forum
81
84.	  Cane buying centers – Buying off the sugarcane 
is done at the weighbridges at factory gates. 
However, several sugar mills have installed 
off-site weighbridges at cane-buying centres. 
The setting up of the cane buying centres is 
currently unregulated, encouraging cane side 
selling outside farming contracts. The weighing 
process is often preceded by cane quality 
checks focusing on the extraneous matter, 
maturity, and general assessment of the cane 
for compliance with the appropriate quality 
threshold. With the collapse of the out-grower 
institutions, the lack of farmers’ representation 
at the weighbridges has led to suspicion and 
complaints about recorded weights.58
85.	 Figure 5.14 shows that at the upstream 
level, there can be two types of growers – 
the nucleus (or miller-cum-planter) estates, 
and out-growers. A nucleus estate is where 
the miller farms their land and produces 
their sugarcane, whereas out-growers are 
independent farmers who produce sugarcane 
and sell it to millers. The ratio of out-growers 
to nucleus farming is an important factor that 
influences the entire value chain for sugar 
production.59 
5.4.3.	 Sugar Sub-Sector Intervention
86.	 In 2018, the Authority initiated investigations 
regarding allegations from the public that 
market players were hoarding sugar – to 
create artificial shortages that would drive the 
price upwards.60 The Authority interrogated 
the sugar value chain for cartel-like conduct 
and any impediments to competition in the 
sector. The investigations established that 
high sugar prices could have been catalysed 
by the inefficiencies of the sugar millers, 
the current importation regime, and the 
sharing of strategic information through their 
association.61 
58	
Ibid 41  
59	
Ibid 47

CAK Annual report 2016-2017 https://www.cak.go.ke/sites/default/files/annual-reports/FY%202016-2017%20CAK%20
Annual%20Report.pdf 
61	
Ibid 52 
62	
Competition Act No. 12 of 2010
63	
Ibid 47 
87.	 Further, it was established that the sharing of 
information was a result of a policy directive by 
the sector regulator. The sharing of information 
could have led to dampening of competition 
in Kenya’s sugar market. The Competition 
Authority of Kenya, under Section 9 of the Act,62 
advised the Agriculture and Food Authority 
to employ strategies aimed at increasing 
production and minimising the sharing of 
disaggregated and historical information.
5.4.4.	 ACF Sugar Study
88.	 The Authority, under the African Competition 
Forum (ACF); a forum that seeks to promote 
the adoption of competition principles 
among African countries to alleviate poverty, 
conducted a study in the sugar industry 
alongside other member countries; South 
Africa, Tanzania, and Zambia. The research 
aimed at appreciating the factors that affected 
cross-border trade in the industry, and trade 
in-flows within the region.
89.	 The study found that, in the Kenyan sugar 
sector, there were a large number of sugar 
producers. However, this did not translate 
into lower consumer prices, as the sugar 
prices were exceptionally high. It was 
discovered that the sugar factories were 
inefficient in production, enjoyed strong 
protection 
against 
imports, 
and 
faced 
unreliable and insufficient sugarcane supply, 
as well as structural constraints to growth in 
productivity. The study also found that there 
was a lack of competitiveness in the sector, 
caused by the comparatively high levels of 
state intervention.63 
5.4.5.	 Leasing of five state-owned sugar mills
90.	 In 2020, the Authority in exercising its advisory 
mandate under section 9 of the Act, and to 

ACF African Competition Forum
82
deepen competition in the sugar sector, gave 
an advisory opinion regarding the Government 
initiative to revive the sugar sector, by leasing 
five State-owned sugar mills, and the Authority 
advised and recommended the following:  
I.	
The Lessor(s) must ensure that contracts 
with farmers and suppliers are explicit, to 
eliminate instances of abuse of buyer power. 
It was recommended that the contracts at 
a minimum provide for the following: the 
terms of payment, payment date, interest 
rate payable on late payment, conditions 
for termination, and variation of contract, 
with reasonable notice and mechanisms for 
resolution of disputes; 
II.	
Cross 
directorships 
among 
competitors 
should be avoided: this is to promote 
competition in the sector for the benefit of the 
farmers and consumers; 
III.	 Approvals have to be sought from the Authority: 
this is because the leasing process amounts 
to notifiable merger(s). The Authority shall 
expedite the review and determination process;
IV.	 Terms of Reference of the Leasing Tender 
should include the following: the winning 
bidder(s) must demonstrate measures of 
increasing (a) efficiency and (b) output to 
cater to the growing demand. These will not 
only be measured by the level of technology 
employed, but also by proposed rates to 
farmers per delivery. In addition, the winning 
bidder should (c) highlight its capacity to 
progressively increase its uptake of raw sugar. 
V.	
Abuse of Dominance: Taking into account 
section 23 (2)(a) of the Act64 the lessor must 
not have cumulatively more than 50% of the 
market share, as this amounts to dominance, 
capable of exerting market power to the 
detriment of the farmers and consumers. It 
was thus recommended that each factory 
be leased to a different bidder, to promote 
competition and reduce the possibility of the 
creation of dominance within a region.
64	
Competition Act No. 12 of 2010
65	
COMESA concentration, and market outcomes in fertilizer markets in East and southern Africa 2024. https://
comesacompetition.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/04/CCC_AMO-Fertiliser-Working-Paper.pdf 
5.5.	 Fertilizer Sub-sector in Kenya 
5.5.1.	 Market Overview65
91.	 Kenya has an annual demand for fertiliser of 
between 500 and 800th Mt per annum, with 
the main products being urea, AN, NPK, and 
DAP, and these are mostly imported from the 
Russian Federation, Norway, Romania, and the 
Middle East through the Mombasa port. The 
import prices in 2022 were US$842/t for DAP, 
US$753/t for Urea and NPK, and US$727/t 
for Ammonium Nitrate, more than double 
that of 2020. However, prices at agro-dealers 
were much higher again, at around $1200/t in 
2022 for urea, and remaining above $800/t in 
May 2023, even while international prices had 
fallen to $330.
92.	 Imports are mainly in bulk form, which are 
then bagged close to the port, while some 
imports are already bagged and imported in 
containers, although these tend to be more 
expensive. Yara has its bagging facility in 
Mombasa, while the Kenyan Ports Authority 
also provides bagging services (Nduati, 2015). 
Bagging services have cost around $5 to $10 
per tonne. Yara East Africa is headquartered 
in Kenya and has three distribution centres in 
the country – Nairobi, Mombasa, and Eldoret.
93.	 The 
fertiliser 
market’s 
leading 
players 
include 
importers, 
blenders, 
and 
the 
Kenyan government, which runs a fertiliser 
subsidy program. There are different tiers of 
companies trading and supplying fertiliser. 
Large importers can arrange to source 
internationally, and land in bulk with their 
bagging facilities at the port. The two leaders, 
Yara and ETG, are estimated to have around a 
40% market share each, with a third company, 
OCP, having around 20%. These companies 
import for Kenya as part of their planning to 
supply the whole of East Africa. The imports 
mainly come from the Middle East and 

ACF African Competition Forum
83
Norway, reflecting ETG links with SABIC, and 
Yara’s production, as well as being imported 
from Russia.
94.	 In 
the 
second 
tier 
of 
medium-sized 
companies, there are around four to six of 
the substantial scale who could arrange 
imports themselves, however, this requires 
sourcing and shipping internationally, and 
the substantial financial commitment this 
entails. These companies instead buy from 
the tier-one importers, mainly from the port 
in bagged form, and sell through networks 
of agro-dealers across the country. They are 
therefore not well positioned to effectively 
compete, as they are dependent on the major 
importers for bulk supplies.
95.	 The Fertiliser Association of Kenya (FAK) was 
established in 2009 and in 2023 it had 13 
members, including all the major suppliers 
and companies in related activities such as 
consulting services. According to FAK, there 
are seven blending plants, one granulation 
plant (Mea), and one plant manufacturing 
single superphosphate. We understand that 
Yara has two blending plants, and ETG, Toyota 
Tyusho (CFAO), and Mea each have one. 
Minjingu Mines Tanzania mines phosphate 
and produces phosphate fertilisers, and 
has a Kenyan business, Minjingu Mines and 
Fertiliser Kenya Ltd.
5.5.2.	 Kenya Fertilizer Market Inquiry66
96.	 The inquiry on the fertiliser market in Kenya 
was conducted for the Authority by the Centre 
for Competition, Regulation and Economic 
Development (CCRED) at the University of 
Johannesburg in 2015. The main objective of 
the inquiry was to identify any anticompetitive 
conduct, 
competition 
constraints, 
and 
consumer protection issues within the market 
66 	 Kenya Fertiliser Market Inquiry 2015. https://www.cak.go.ke/sites/default/files/Kenya%20Fertiliser%20Market%20Inquiry.
pdf
67	
Ibid 66
68	
Ibid 66
69	
Ibid 52
70	
Ibid 69 
for fertiliser. The inquiry also assessed the 
Government of Kenya’s (GoK) fertiliser subsidy 
programme and its effect on competitive 
outcomes in the market as a whole.
97.	 The inquiry found that there were strong 
indications 
of 
anti-competitive 
conduct 
which has caused substantial harm to farmers 
purchasing 
fertiliser 
in 
the 
commercial 
market.67 This includes coordination around 
pricing benchmarks, which appear to have 
served to increase and maintain prices 
substantially above competitive levels. In 
addition, it also found indications of market 
allocation. The inquiry recommended an 
investigation be initiated concerning possible 
anti-competitive conduct, including but not 
limited to restrictive practices that distort, limit 
or prevent competition.68
5.5.3.	 Fertiliser Sub-Sector Intervention69
98.	 The Authority conducted an investigation into 
the fertiliser sector concerning alleged market 
allocation and price fixing in the sector by 
fertiliser importers. The investigations focused 
on collusion in price, market, and output 
by market players, which are prohibited 
under section 21(3) of the Act. The Authority 
established that there were no price-fixing 
arrangements between members of the 
association, and that the sharing of the data 
was pursuant to the Ministry of Agriculture’s 
directive. The parties were ordered to 
individually forward the information required 
to the Government through AFA.70

ACF African Competition Forum
84
71	
Tea Board of Kenya guides the development, promotion, and regulation of the tea industry for the benefit of the tea 
growers and other stakeholders
6.1.	 Agricultural Export Requirements
99.	 The export of coffee is regulated by AFA under 
the Coffee Directorate, while tea is regulated 
by the Tea Board of Kenya71 (TBK). The coffee 
in Kenya is sold through two marketing 
agents, and that is through Central Auction, 
who offer their services purely for commercial 
purposes, and Direct Sales, which are growers 
marketing their coffee. The central auction 
is administered by Nairobi Coffee Exchange 
Management Committee, which is drawn 
from industry stakeholders.
100.	 Direct sales are where the farmers sell 
their coffee directly to locals, or overseas. 
However, in cases where farmers cannot 
market their products directly, commercial 
marketing agents facilitate the process by 
drawing up sales agreements between 
producers and buyers, and handling other 
marketing logistics.
101.	 Traders of tea are required to register with 
the East African Tea Trade Association 
(EATTA). EATTA is an  organisation  that 
brings together tea producers, exporters, 
brokers, packers, and warehousemen to 
interact commercially at the tea auction, and 
to promote the best interests of the tea trade 
in Africa. The auction system has four main 
 
 
categories: producers, brokers, exporters, 
and warehousemen.
102.	 The Traders are required to be trained and 
then registered on the Trade Facilitation 
Platform (TFP) as users of the system. TFP 
is an electronic system that facilitates the 
online application, processing, approvals, 
and issuance of export and import permits, 
and declaration of goods.  The TFP is 
managed by the Kenya Trade Network 
Agency (KenTrade).
103.	 A food hygiene license is required for 
all facilities that process and package 
products that are for human consumption, 
e.g. pack houses. The license is issued by 
Port Health Services Kenya (PHS)  or by 
Public Health Officers, from the respective 
County Governments where the premises 
are located. The license is valid for one (1) 
calendar year.
104.	 Most of the small-scale holders in the 
tea sector are members of the Kenya Tea 
Development Agency (KTDA). KTDA is a 
private company owned by about 600,000 
smallholder tea farmers, spread across 16 
tea-growing counties in Kenya. The farmers 
are shareholders in 54 tea companies that 
own KTDA (H) and its 8 subsidiary companies.

ACF African Competition Forum
85
It provides services such as transportation, 
processing, supply of inputs (fertiliser), and 
marketing of tea for the farmers.72
6.2.	 Import Requirements 73
105.	 Import of food and agricultural products 
to Kenya is regulated by five key agencies, 
which are linked to a single online export/
import approval system. Those agencies are 
the Kenya Plant Health Inspectorate Service 
(KEPHIS), the Directorate of Veterinary 
Services (DVS), the Kenya Dairy Board (KDB), 
the Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS), the 
National Biosafety Authority (NBA), and 
the Department of Public Health (DPH). All 
certification requests are submitted through 
the Kenya Electronic Import Export System 
(KESWS).  
106.	 In addition to product-specific certifications, 
a Certificate of Conformity (CoC) issued 
after pre-export certification by an agent 
of the Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS) is 
mandatory for all exports to Kenya.  For a 
trader to obtain a CoC, an imported product 
must satisfy Kenyan import requirements 
before export. This evaluation is conducted 
by pre-export verification of conformity 
(PVoC) agents appointed by GOK. Once a 
PVoC agent has issued a CoC, the importer 
may present the CoC to KEBS for clearance 
of the goods and apply for the Import 
Standardisation Mark (ISM), a stick-on-label 
to be affixed to each retail container. Below is 
the description of ten (10) import procedures 
after a CoC has been acquired;
i.	
The importer will notify a clearing agent (CA) 
of the arrival date of a shipment.
ii.	
The CA notifies the Kenya Revenue Authority 
(KRA) via its online clearing system. The 
importer must use a KRA-registered CA. 
72	
Kenya Tea Development Agency. https://ktdateas.com/about-us/ 
73	
Food Crop Directorate. Export/Import Guidelines. https://food.agricultureauthority.go.ke/index.php/export-import-
guidelines 
74	
World Trade Organization (WTO). Understanding the WTO Agreement on Sanitary and Phytosanitary Measures. 2023.
https://www.wto.org/english/tratop_e/sps_e/spsund_e.htm#:~:text=The%20Agreement%20on%20Sanitary%20
and,the%20purpose%20of%20trade%20protection. 
iii.	 The CA enters the arrival date and manifest 
number into the KRA system. 
iv.	 The KRA posts the manifest number to the 
specified Kenyan bank.
v.	
The CA pays the relevant taxes using HS 
Codes and VAT rates where applicable.
vi.	 The KRA agent clears and confirms the entry 
of the cargo to the CA.
vii.	 The CA uses the KRA confirmation to pay 
the various port charges at the Kenya Ports 
Authority (KPA). 
viii.	 The CA uses the KPA documents, and any 
related import permits to request clearance 
from KEPHIS, KEBS, Port Health, and the local 
police. 
ix.	 The KPA conducts a final physical verification 
of the cargo before releasing it into the 
domestic market.
107.	 Customs and trade regulations play an 
important role in ensuring that products 
adhere to safety and quality standards. 
These procedures and regulations should 
therefore not be viewed as a barrier to 
trade, especially the requirement to obtain 
a Certificate of Conformity from a Kenya 
Bureau of Standards appointed pre-export 
verification of conformity (PVoC) partner, 
and the obligation to obtain an Import 
Standards Mark (ISM) for a list of sensitive 
products 
imported 
into 
Kenya. 
These 
regulations have therefore bolstered trade in 
agricultural products amongst countries on 
the continent.
6.2.1.	 Sanitary and Phyto-sanitary Requirements
108.	 The General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade 
(GATT) indicates that members of the World 
Trade Organisation (WTO)74 have the right 
to take Sanitary and Phytosanitary (SPS) 
measures necessary for the protection of 

ACF African Competition Forum
86
human, animal, or plant life or health. Kenya 
being a signatory to the WTO complied with 
the requirement  and set up Kenya Plant 
Health Inspectorate Service (KEPHIS) as the 
regulatory body that is mandated to  carry 
out Phyto-sanitary inspections, and analysis 
of fresh produce samples and farm input 
quality. 
109.	 Traders are required to obtain a copy of 
a Plant Import Permit and Phyto-sanitary 
certificate for each consignment imported 
into Kenya. The certificate verifies that 
a competent authority in the exporting 
country examined the plant material for 
pests and diseases before they left the 
country, and that the plant materials meet 
Kenya’s Phyto-sanitary requirements. The 
Phyto-sanitary certificate is also issued to all 
export consignments that meet the quality 
standards. To this extent, this requirement 
has promoted trade in agricultural export 
and import markets within the continent, by 
creating trust and dependability on these 
products.
6.3.	 Regional Organisations Agreement 
110.	 Kenya is a member of the EAC, the COMESA, 
and the Intergovernmental Authority on 
Development (IGAD). Kenya was among the 
first countries to ratify the African Continental 
Free Trade Area Agreement (AfCFTA). 
Kenya views these Regional Blocs as market 
opportunities to expand their export markets 
including for agricultural products.
6.3.1.	 East African Community (EAC)
111.	 The EAC is an intergovernmental organisation 
composed of seven member states, namely 
the three founding members of Kenya, 
Tanzania, and Uganda, as well as Rwanda, 
Burundi, South Sudan, and the Democratic 
Republic of the Congo. In 2010, The member 
states signed the East African Community 
Customs Union and the Common Market 
75	
COMESA in brief. 2023. https://www.comesa.int/wp-content/uploads/2020/05/COMESA-in-brief-FINAL-_web.pdf 
Protocol. These agreements aim to promote 
free 
trade 
and 
economic 
integration 
among 
member 
states. 
Consequently, 
Kenya has enjoyed certain benefits in terms 
of reduced trade barriers, harmonised 
customs procedures, and increased market 
access within the EAC region. However, it is 
important to note that Kenya’s trade relations 
are not limited to EAC countries alone. 
6.3.2.	 	Common Market for Eastern and 
Southern Africa (COMESA)
112.	 COMESA Member States established a 
Free Trade Area (FTA) on 31 October 2000, 
when nine of the Member States, namely: 
Djibouti, 
Kenya, 
Madagascar, 
Malawi, 
Mauritius, Sudan, Zambia, and Zimbabwe 
eliminated 
their 
tariffs 
on 
COMESA-
originating products, in accordance with the 
tariff reduction schedule adopted in 1992.75 
The establishment of FTA was intended to 
promote free trade, economic integration, 
and elimination of trade barriers among 
member states. Kenya has exploited this 
agreement, especially in the importation of 
Sugar from COMESA countries. 
113.	 In conclusion, these regulations have worked 
to the benefit of Kenyan Agricultural value 
chain markets, not only at regional markets 
but also at continental levels. Through 
the implementation of the regulations, 
agricultural export and import markets 
have been expanded, and agricultural food 
product safety and quality guaranteed.
6.4.	 Access to Finance and Other Agricultural 
Essentials 
114.	 The use of farm inputs such as improved 
seeds and fertilisers has the potential to 
increase the level of output and reduce food 
insecurity for the masses, whose backbone 
is agriculture. It is part of the government’s 
plans to reduce the cost of food production 
in the country, thus reducing the cost of 

ACF African Competition Forum
87
living. Given this, the government-initiated 
fertiliser subsidy program makes it easier for 
farmers to access affordable fertilisers. 
115.	 Access to timely and affordable financing 
remains a major challenge for many farmers 
in Kenya.  One of the most common 
agricultural financing options in Kenya 
is agricultural lines of credit. Financial 
institutions such as banks and microfinance 
institutions offer agricultural lines of credit to 
farmers at competitive interest rates.
116.	 In addition to that, Kenyan farmers can also 
access 
government-sponsored 
financing 
programs which are aimed at providing 
affordable financing solutions to small-
scale farmers. An example of such is the 
funding offered by the Agricultural Finance 
Corporation (AFC) to farmers engaged 
in crop and livestock production, agro-
processing, and value addition.
6.5.	 Entry Barriers
117.	 The Kenyan agricultural trade market has 
been stifled by entry barriers for more 
productive 
investors 
and 
specialisation 
in commodities that have low demand 
growth. Some of the challenges are poor 
infrastructure76 and other non-tariff barriers 
in Kenya, like the requirement to obtain a 
Certificate of Conformity, the obligation 
to obtain an Import Standards Mark (ISM) 
for a list of sensitive products imported 
into Kenya, and cumbersome procedures 
in obtaining the requirements from the 
regulatory agencies77 and other NTBs in 
trading partners.78 The requirements of SPS 
measures imposed by trading partners are 
also a challenge, as several Kenyan agro-food 
products were affected for violating the SPS 
76	
Africa’s Investment Gateway. How Taveta-Holili chokepoint is fueling Kenya-Tanzania trade war. 2023. https://
theexchange.africa/countries/how-taveta-holili-chokepoint-is-fueling-kenya-tanzania-trade-war/
77	
Kenya - Trade Barriers. 2023. https://www.export.gov/apex/article2?id=Kenya-trade-barriers 
78	
KPSA & TPSF. Expanding Business Opportunities Beyond Borders: Bridging The Trade Gap Between Kenya and 
Tanzania. 2023. https://www.tralac.org/images/docs/11983/expanding-business-opportunities-beyond-borders-
bridging-the-trade-gap-between-kenya-and-tanzania-kepsa-tpsf-august-2017.pdf 
79	
KIPPRA. Sanitary and Phytosanitary Standards Constraints in Kenya. 2023 https://kippra.or.ke/sanitary-and-phytosanitary-
standards-constraints-in-kenya/ 
regulations and market access restrictions. 
Agro-food products are among the products 
that have been adversely affected by these 
measures.79
6.6.	 Non-tariff barriers
118.	 Non-tariff barriers (NTB) are a bigger and 
more hidden menace to intra-African trade, 
particularly in the agricultural sector. Most of 
these NTBs stem from regulations imposed 
by one Partner State over one or more of the 
others. The NTB challenges Kenyan traders 
face include the cumbersome import and 
export procedures, like the requirement to 
obtain a Certificate of Conformity from a 
Kenya Bureau of Standards appointed pre-
export verification of conformity (PVoC) 
partner, and the obligation to obtain an 
Import Standards Mark (ISM) for a list of 
sensitive products imported into Kenya.

ACF African Competition Forum
88
119.	 The main coffee export destinations within 
the African continent are Egypt, South Sudan, 
South Africa, Tunisia, and Morocco. On the 
other hand, Kenyan tea is mainly exported 
to Egypt and Sudan, and tea exports to 
other countries within the continent remain 
relatively low in value.
120.	 Maize is the main staple food in Kenya. The 
quantity of the country’s imported maize has 
been on the rise for the last three years in 
a row, due to the drop in maize production 
– to meet the deficit created by the low 
production which was occasioned by climate 
change. South Africa and Uganda have been 
the consistent exporters of maize to Kenya in 
the last five years, up to 2023.
121.	 Kenya is a net importer of sugar, and sources 
the bulk of its sugar imports from the 
COMESA countries. Some of the COMESA 
countries that Kenya imports sugar from are 
Uganda, Mauritius, Zimbabwe, and Egypt. 
Besides these COMESA countries, the 
country also imports sugar from South Africa, 
Madagascar, and Eswatini.
122.	 The Kenyan traders are facing significant 
challenges to trade smoothly with the EAC 
and the Rest of Africa (ROA), because of non-
tariff barriers (NTBs) such as the requirement 
for SPS measures, which greatly affected 
the 
agricultural 
products; 
inadequate 
infrastructure to support the free movement 
of goods and services; and cumbersome 
procedures in meeting the import and export 
requirements.
123.	 The Competition Authority of Kenya has taken 
decisions on several cases in the agricultural 
sector. In addition, it has conducted market 
inquiries in the agricultural sector, and 
provided advisories to government agencies 
in the sector. Agricultural and food markets 
in Kenya are susceptible to market failures, 
especially farmers, who are mainly price 
takers and are subjected to broker prices, 
consequently not earning the commensurate 
revenues from their products.
124.	 The 
Competition 
Authority 
of 
Kenya 
continues to work in agricultural markets 
and trade. In this regard, it highlights the 
following insights going forward:
i.	
Ease of doing business: The government 
should simplify the process of getting the 
documents required for the importation 
or exportation of products, to minimise 
the cumbersome procedure through one-
stop shops, computerisation, and extensive 
digitisation of the processes;
ii.	
Collaboration in competition enforcement: 
Regional 
institutions 
and 
regional-level 
enforcement agencies are key to building a 
better holistic response to anti-competitive 
conduct. Collaboration among the regional 
competition 
agencies 
is 
imperative 
in 
tackling regional anti-competitive behaviour, 
such as cross-border cartels in food markets.
iii.	
Co-operation: The Competition Authority 
of Kenya will continue to work together 
with national and other sector regulators 
in the agricultural sector, to deepen the 
culture of competition by way of advocacy 
initiatives, and to eliminate market failures in 
agricultural food value chains.
iv.	
Access to financing: The choice of the farmers 
to access capital is determined by the ease 
7

of accessing it; improving digital finance 
services can help address the challenge of 
access to finance, credit, and savings by the 
smallholder farmers in rural areas, especially 
where traditional finance models have not 
adequately addressed the demands in rural 
agricultural production markets. 
v.	
African countries need to promote regional 
and continental trade in agricultural food 
products, by facilitating the implementation 
of the Tripartite FTA80 Agreement provisions 
through 
harmonisation 
and 
consistent 
implementation of customs procedures, 
transit procedures, tariff liberalisation, and 
Rules of Origin.

1	
The Trade to GDP ratio has been computed for 2022 figures from the National Account Estimates, accessed  at https://
statsmauritius.govmu.org/Pages/Statistics/ESI/National_Accounts/NA/NAE_Mar24.aspx
2	
Indicative figures computed from the Digest of agricultural statistics 2022 which can be found at https://statsmauritius.
govmu.org/Pages/Statistics/By_Subject/Agriculture/Agri.aspx
1.	
Mauritius is a small island of 2,040 Km2, 
open to international trade, as reflected in 
the trade to GDP ratio of 118%1 for the year 
2022. It has evolved from a mono-crop, 
sugar export-based economy in the 1960s 
to a well-diversified one, with several pillars 
such as tourism, financial services, textiles 
and construction.  Agriculture, nonetheless, 
remains an important sector for the Mauritian 
economy, contributing around 4.1% of the 
Gross-Value-Added (GVA); and 5.6% to 
total employment for the year 2022.  The 
agricultural sector is also one of the main 
sources 
of 
foreign 
exchange 
earnings, 
accounting for around 31%2 of the country’s 
total merchandise export earnings in 2022.      
2.	
Agriculture and the agro-processing industry 
are of utmost importance in ensuring food 
security. In this regard, it is worth highlighting 
that Mauritius is largely self-sufficient in food 
commodities like sugar, tea, vegetables and 
fresh chicken. At the same time, however, 
it relies heavily on importation for several 
staple food items such as wheat, rice, pulses 
and dairy products. The main sources of 
importation for these commodities have 
traditionally been countries like France, India, 
Australia, and Madagascar (mostly for pulses). 
For exports of its agricultural products, in 
particular sugar, the European market has 
for a long time been its primary destination. 
This is explained by the colonial ties that 
Mauritius has had with Great Britain, and the 
trade preferential agreements from which it 
benefitted with the European Union (EU) in 
the post-independence era after 1968.
3.	
Mauritius is no longer benefitting from 
preferential treatment in terms of guaranteed 
volume and price for the export of its sugar to 
the EU (under the Lome Convention). It now 
has to be competitive on the international 
market for trade in agricultural commodities, 
and for other products.  Being a member 
of 
the 
Southern 
African 
Development 
Community (SADC), the Common Market for 
Eastern and Southern Africa (COMESA) and 
recently the African Continental Free Trade 
Area (AfCFTA), Mauritius must tap into trade 
opportunities within Africa. An understanding 
of the current Mauritian trade flows into 
agricultural products could be useful in 
devising and implementing strategies that 
can boost regional intra-trade for the country, 
and promoting economic integration within 
Africa.
1.1.	 MOTIVATION AND SCOPE 
4.	
This study is part of the collaborative African 
Competition Forum (ACF) research into 
regional trade flow patterns related to the 
agriculture and agro-processing sectors. There 
are six participating ACF member countries, 
namely, Mauritius, Angola, Kenya, South Africa, 
Zambia and Zimbabwe. The study provides 
a regional focus on important, highly-traded 
food products and agricultural inputs.  
5.	
This chapter of the ACF study provides an 
assessment of the trade flows for selected 
agriculture and agro-processing products for 
Mauritius, with a focus on African continental 
and regional community levels. The selected 
agricultural inputs and food products are 
grouped in the following categories: 
1
INTRODUCTION

ACF African Competition Forum
96
a.	
Agricultural inputs: fertilisers, seeds for 
sowing and oilcake.
b.	
Vegetable oils: sunflower oil, soybean oil and 
palm oil.
c.	
Beverages and other commodities: tea, coffee 
and sugar.
d.	
Grains and legumes: wheat and rice.
e.	
Protein foods: poultry.
f.	
Vegetables: onions and tomatoes. 
g.	
Citrus fruit: lemons, mandarins, oranges and 
other citrus fruits.
1.2.	 METHODOLOGY 
6.	
The study analyses the volume of trade in 
the above-mentioned selected agri-food 
commodities at global, continental and 
regional levels for the period 2018 to 2022. 
7.	
For benchmarking purposes, the study 
compares the weighted average prices 
of exports and imports across the partner 
countries, and at regional level. To the extent 
possible, price comparison with the World 
Bank’s commodity price has been undertaken. 
For sugar and tea, two main crops for 
Mauritius, the average domestic prices from 
Statistics Mauritius have been compared with 
import and export prices.   
8.	
Both CIF and FOB values have been used to 
analyse average export and import prices, 
and also to assess changes in freight and 
insurance components. 
9.	
The value of exports and imports, reported 
in Mauritian rupees in the source data, have 
been converted to US dollars (US$), using

2.1.	 AGRICULTURAL INPUTS 
2.1.1.	 Fertilisers 
13.	 Mauritius relies on imports in the supply of 
fertilisers for vegetable growing, especially for 
sugarcane plantations.  It is to be highlighted 
that sugarcane is the primary cash crop for 
Mauritius, which accounted for 83%9 of the 
total cultivated land area of 47,064 hectares in 
2022.  Fertilisation is a key driver of increased 
sugarcane production, which accounts for 
around 25%10 of the total cost.      
14.	 Mauritius 
does 
not 
produce 
inorganic 
(chemical) fertilisers as the country is not 
endowed with natural resources such as 
phosphate rock, potash, and natural gas. 
Mauritius relies heavily on the importation 
of fertilisers, although there is one local 
company, namely, Ingenia (previously known 
as Mauritius Chemical & Fertiliser Industry 
Limited), which processes imported raw 
materials and supplies blended and NPK 
complex fertilisers. There are also private 
importers engaged in the importation and 
distribution of chemical fertilisers (inorganic), 
whether straight or complex. There is a 
local company, Island Renewable Fertilisers 
 
 
Ltd,11 which produces and supplies organic 
fertilisers, albeit in a lower volume compared 
to inorganic fertilisers.   
15.	 Mauritius imports chemical fertilisers, which 
are classified into straight or complex 
fertilisers. Straight fertilisers are nitrogenous 
fertilisers, phosphatic fertilisers and potassic 
fertilisers. 
Complex 
fertilisers 
refer 
to 
fertilisers containing nitrogen phosphorus 
with potassium, also known as NPK fertilisers. 
16.	 The average annual fertiliser consumption in 
Mauritius has been in decline, from 35,000 
tonnes in 2017 to around 26,000 tonnes in 
2022.12 This is mainly explained by a reduction 
in land cultivation area for sugarcane, and 
decreases in imports of processed fertilisers.  
17.	 As highlighted above, the country depends 
mostly on importation of inorganic fertilisers. 
The  section below analyses fertiliser trade for 
the period under review. 
Imports of fertilisers
18.	 Figure 1 below depicts the total value and 
volume of chemical fertilisers imported by 
Mauritius during the period 2018 to 2022. It is 
observed that imports of fertilisers have been 
on a declining trend, from 43,881 tonnes in 
2018 to 26,459 tonnes in 2022; except for 
2021.13 
2

AGRICULTURAL COMMODITIES

19.	 The decline in fertiliser imports can, to a large 
extent, be attributed to the reduction in land 
cultivation area for sugarcane, from 47,678 
hectares in 2018 to 39,199 hectares in 2022. 
Another factor explaining the reduction in 
fertiliser imports is the decrease in volume of 
processed fertilisers exported from Mauritius 
to regional countries. Also, the adoption of 
sustainable agricultural practices, through 
the use of organic fertilisers, explains to some 
extent the lower volume of chemical fertiliser 
imported into the country.

23.	 Between 2018 to 2022, Mauritius has largely 
imported nitrogenous fertiliser (urea-based) 
(49%) and NPK fertiliser (35%) accounting for 
around 84% of total imports. Potassic fertiliser 
accounted for 15%, and phosphatic fertiliser 
in low volume (averaging less than 1% for the 
period under review).
24.	 Nitrogenous fertiliser, the most imported 
form of fertilisers in Mauritius, was primarily 
sourced from Belgium and China. It was also 
observed that the import volume from South 
Africa rose from 181 tonnes in 2018 to 3,848 
tonnes in 2022, representing a growth of 25%. 
25.	 With regard to phosphatic fertiliser, the major 
percentage was sourced from the largest 
producing country globally, China.16 It was 
observed that, post Covid-19, Mauritius started 
importing from France and Turkey, albeit in 
a smaller volume. This could potentially be 
explained by restrictive measures adopted 
by China in 2022, when the country imposed 
export quotas17 to curb its rising domestic 
prices of phosphatic fertilisers.     
26.	 With regard to potassic fertilisers, Mauritius 
has imported from mostly European and 
Asian countries. Imports from Belarus, in 2022, 
halted as a result of restrictions on the use and 
supply of Belarusian potash fertiliser by the 
EU, which affected the country’s exports due 
to its status as a landlocked country.18   
27.	 Mauritius sourced NPK fertilisers from the 
major producing countries, namely China and 
Vietnam, and to a lesser extent from Morrocco, 
for the period under review. 
28.	 When assessing regional trade, it can be 
observed that Africa’s share in Mauritius’ 
importation of fertilisers has grown from 1,686 
tonnes (4%) in 2018 to 7,939 tonnes (30%) 
in 2022. South Africa has become a major 
provider of nitrogenous fertilisers. Mauritius 
imported 36% of its potassic fertilisers from 
South Africa (775 tonnes) and Morocco (410 
tonnes) in 2022. With regard to NPK fertilisers, 
Mauritius imported 23%, around 1,816 
tonnes, from Morocco and Egypt.  
29.	 A further analysis of the average import prices 
from African countries, compared to countries 
from other continents, was undertaken and 
presented below.

30.	 Table 3 presents the import prices (in US$/kg) 
for the different types of chemical fertilisers 
imported by Mauritius, from 2018 to 2022. 
For nitrogenous fertilisers, the import prices 
from Africa were 46% higher on average than 
from other countries, indicating that Mauritius 
sourced nitrogenous fertilisers at better prices 
from outside Africa. 
31.	 Regarding phosphatic fertilisers, Mauritius 
imported mostly outside Africa (mainly from 
China and France, the largest-phosphatic 
fertiliser producing countries). It is to be 
highlighted that year 2021 was an exception 
when 
Mauritius 
imported 
phosphatic 
fertilisers from Africa. However, the average 
import price from Africa was US$ 0.57, 
significantly higher than from other sources. 
32.	 From 2018 to 2021, Mauritius imported 
potassic fertilisers mostly outside Africa. In the 
year 2022, Mauritius imported a significant 
amount of potassic fertilisers from Africa 
(South Africa and Morocco) at a price of US$ 
0.84 per kg, 18% lower than prices from non-
African countries. 
33.	 From 2018 to 2020, import prices for NPK 
fertilisers from Africa were 69% higher than 
from other countries. It is to be highlighted 
that in 2022, Mauritius sourced a significant 
amount 
of 
NPK 
fertilisers 
from 
Africa 
(principally Morocco) at US$ 1.1 per kg, 20% 
lower than prices from non-African countries 
that year. This suggests that Africa could 
potentially be a cheaper source of imports for 
NPK fertilisers for Mauritius. 
34.	 Urea-based nitrogenous fertilisers are the 
most imported form of fertiliser in Mauritius. 
A benchmarking analysis between Mauritius’ 
import prices and the world prices of urea-
based nitrogenous fertilisers is presented in 
Figure 2 below.

102

35.	 Mauritius imported urea-based nitrogenous 
fertilisers at US$ 327 per tonne in 2018, 
increasing significantly to US$ 981 per tonne 
in 2022, partly explained by an increase in 
prices of freight and insurance costs, from 
US$ 35 per tonne in 2018 to US$ 131 per 
tonne in 2022. 
36.	 The world price of urea-based nitrogenous 
fertilisers revolved around US$ 250 per tonne 
to US$ 700 per tonne. When benchmarked 
against the world price, it can be observed 
that the price differential (between world 
price and import price) has risen significantly, 
from US$ 77 per tonne in 2018 to US$ 281 
per tonne in 2022, which can be explained by 
rising prices in source countries.

fertiliser exports. Other exports included 32 
tonnes of potassic fertilisers (17%), 9 tonnes 
of nitrogenous fertilisers (4%), and 1 tonne 
of phosphatic fertilisers. These exports were 
mostly destined to regional islands.

40.	 In 2018, 908 tonnes of fertilisers were exported 
to Africa, comprising 402 tonnes from 
Rwanda, 389 tonnes from Madagascar, and

the years, Africa’s portion increased to 90% 
in 2022; Madagascar (76 tonnes) and the 
Seychelles (96 tonnes).

from 2018 to 2022, an analysis of price has 
been deemed unnecessary.  
2.1.2.	 Seeds for sowing 
42.	 Mauritius relies heavily on seeds for sowing, 
particularly for the production of food 
crops: vegetables and fruits. Food crops are 
cultivated on around 7,864 hectares19 (17% 
 

ACF African Competition Forum
105
of total land cultivation) in Mauritius. These 
are grown in open field (hydroponic and non-
hydroponic). 
43.	 Mauritius’ annual importation of seeds is in 
the range of 50,000 kg to 80,000 kg. Mauritius 
imports different types of seeds, namely seeds 
for vegetables, fruits and spores, herbaceous 
20	
Figures computed from Digest of Agricultural statistics 2022 (table 28) available at https://statsmauritius.govmu.org/
Pages/Statistics/By_Subject/Agriculture/Agri.aspx
plants, forage plants and lucerne plants. 
The bulk of seeds imported into the country 
remains vegetable seeds. 
44.	 Figure 4 below provides Mauritius’ total 
volume and value of importation of seeds for 
sowing for the period 2018 to 2022.
Figure 4: Imports of seeds for sowing by Mauritius, 2018-2022
Source: MCCI, statistics
45.	 Mauritius imported 58,503 kg of seeds at US$ 
33 per kg in 2018. The import volume rose 
significantly to 96,792 tonnes in 2019, and 
then fell during the Covid period 2020-21 
to around 76,000 tonnes. In 2022, imports of 
seed rose to 89,407 kg, costing around US$ 
28 per kg. 
46.	 The increase in importation of seeds for 
sowing, from 2018 to 2022, can partly be 
explained by an increase in land under 
cultivation for food crops, from 7,646 hectares 
in 2018 to 7,865 hectares in 2022. Also, the 
adoption of soilless agriculture practices, 
under protective cultures, explains to an extent 
the increase in the volume of seeds imported 
into the country. For instance, production of 
food crops rose from 96,847 tonnes in 2018 to 
117,115 tonnes in 2022; out of which 107,879 
tonnes20 came through open fields and 9,237 
tonnes through covered cultures.

2.1.3.	 Oilcake 
55.	 Mauritius imports oilcake, including solid 
residues from soybean oil extraction, primarily 
from South America. These serve as crucial 
inputs for animal feed factories.21 There are 

ACF African Competition Forum
109
at present two manufacturers in the animal 
feed sector in Mauritius, namely, Livestock 
Feed Ltd (“LFL”)22 and Meaders Feeds Ltd 
(“MFL”)23. Both manufacturers cater for local 
and regional markets.   
56.	 LFL (part of Eclosia group) operates three 
feed plants, in namely Mauritius, Madagascar 
and Rwanda. The company has a regional 
production capacity of 242,000 tonnes24  per 
year: Mauritius (140,000 tonnes), Madagascar 
(90,000 tonnes) and Rwanda (12,000 tonnes). 
LFL supplies animal feed locally and to

2.2.	 VEGETABLE OILS
67.	 This section relates to trade of the main 
edible oil for Mauritius, namely soybean 
oil, sunflower oil and palm oil.  The analysis 
relates to the period 2018 to 2022. 
2.2.1.	 Supply of edible oil in Mauritius
68.	 Edible oil is regarded as an essential product 
for households. It is sourced from plants and 
can only be consumed in refined form. The 
process of refining crude edible oil involves 
the extraction of impurities from the solvent 
extraction plant, and entails making the oil 
more palatable and stable against rancidity 
upon storage. It also includes degumming, 
deacidification, 
decolorising, 
deodorising 
and dewaxing. 
Supply chain
69.	 In 
Mauritius, 
pre-packed 
edible 
oil 
is 
imported, as well as crude oil for refining and 
distribution. The supply chain for edible oil is 
shown below. 
70.	 As illustrated in Figure 8, local refineries and 
wholesale import distributors operate in the 
market for wholesale supply of refined edible 
oil in Mauritius. Their main customer groups 
are (i) supermarkets and other retailers (ii) 
hotels and restaurants and (iii) industrial food 
processors. Local refineries and importers 
supply their products directly to hotels and 
restaurants. The latter can also procure edible 
oil from supermarkets and other retailers. 
71.	 The two long-standing edible oil refineries 
in Mauritius, Mauritius Oil Refineries Limited 
(Moroil)  and Ramdenee Edible Oil Products 
Ltd (REOP), are the ones supplying most of 
the blended vegetable oil to the domestic 
market. They import crude soya bean oil and 
sunflower oil, which are refined and blended 
with imported refined palm oil. REOP imports 
crude oil in flexi bags, whereas Moroil imports 
crude oil in tanker ships and containers, 
refining the products and supplying refined 
blended edible oil in pouches, bottles, 
gallons, and tanks. Moroil also imports and 
distributes bottled refined edible oil, such as 
extra virgin olive oil.28

Policy measures for edible oil
72.	 With regards to trade policy measures 
applicable for edible oil, there is currently no 
tariff imposed on the importation of crude 
oil in Mauritius. However, for refined oil, a 
10% tariff rate is generally applicable for 
imports of refined soybean oil and refined 
sunflower oil, from different countries, except 
for COMESA Free Trade (FTA) member states 
(Group 1), Indian Ocean Commission (IOC), 
and the SADC member states. A 1% tariff is 
also applicable to non-FTA COMESA member 
states (Group 2). There is currently no tariff 
rate applicable for refined palm oil, olive oil, 
crude soybean oil and crude sunflower oil.29 
Import volumes of crude and refined oil and 
consumption of edible oil

and refined edible oil for the period 2018 to 
2022.

imports of crude soybean oil have increased 
steadily, from 13,370 tonnes in 2018 to

edible oil imports, which have decreased 
from 11,161 tonnes in 2018 to 5,602 tonnes 
in 2022.

Our history – Mauritius Sugar Syndicate

Our Savoir Faire – Mauritius Sugar Syndicate
82.	 The comparative analysis shows that the 
import price from African countries was more 
or less similar to non-African countries for 
crude soybean oil. For crude sunflower oil, 
the import price from African countries was 
lower than that of other sources by 89%. With 
regard to refined sunflower oil, the import 
price from African countries was on average 
45% lower than that from other countries. For 
refined palm oil, the import price from African 
countries was 6% less than from other sources, 
for the year 2022. For refined soybean oil, 
the import price from African countries was 
14% less expensive than from non-African 
countries, on average.  
83.	 The study does not lay emphasis on exports of 
vegetable oils, as Mauritius is not a producer of 
vegetable oils, and only re-exports vegetable 
oils in low volumes.  
2.3.	 BEVERAGES AND OTHER COMMODITIES
2.3.1.	 Sugar 
84.	 Cane sugar has traditionally been the most 
important agricultural sector in the Mauritian 
economy. Sugarcane was first introduced 
in Mauritius by the Dutch colonisers around 
1638. The sugar industry witnessed a boom 
under British colonisation in 1810, and 
continued to be the main crop generating 
revenue for the economy for more than 100 
years after its introduction.30 By 1858 there 
were 288 factories, the largest number of 
factories to have operated on the island. The 
increased production and export of sugar led 
to significant development in the country.31 

ACF African Competition Forum
118
85.	 In 1936, the entire community of sugar 
planters joined the syndicate for sugar, the 
Mauritius Sugar Syndicate, which became the 
sole organisation in charge of local sale and 
exports of sugars produced on the island. As 
from 1975, sugar began to be exported and 
traded under the Sugar Protocol (SP).32 Under 
the Sugar Protocol, the European Union has 
been the main export market for a very long 
time, owing to trade preferential areas.33 
86.	 In 1978, specialty sugars such as demerara and 
golden granulated were first produced and 
exported. In 2009 and up to 2015, an exclusive 
partnership agreement was established with 
Sudzucker, the largest beet sugar company 
in the European Union, for the marketing of 
Mauritius white refined sugar, equivalent to 
the EU quality, across the European continent, 
while the market base for special sugars was 
diversified to attain the annual sales target of 
120,000 tonnes.34 In 2012, 71% of Mauritius’ 
cane sugar was destined for the EU. 
87.	 In 2017 the Sugar Protocol ended, and exports 
of sugar fell significantly below the average 
of US$ 229 m. Producers were compelled to 
review their production mix, and diversified 
into other types of sugar for exports. New 
markets for exports of sugar were identified.35
88.	 As a result, Mauritius now produces and 
exports special sugars which are used for 
diverse household and industrial usage, 
sweetening beverages, confectionery, baking, 
winemaking, and haute cuisine. Examples of 
such sugars include coffee crystals, extra light 
muscovado, light muscovado, dark brown 
soft, dark muscovado, molasses sugar, dry 
demerara, clear demerara, fine demerara, 
standard demerara, dark demerara, dark 
32	
Ibid
33	
Export Insights Report-December 2023-Mauritius Chamber of Commerce and Industry (MCCI).
34

standard demerara, golden granulated, fine 
caster, golden caster, special raw, brown cane 
sugar, and Mauritius Industrial cane sugar.36  
89.	 In 2022, the exports of cane sugar amounted 
to US$ 197 m; of which 46% were exported 
to the EU. The share of exports to Africa was 
32%.37 This represents a significant increase 
when compared to the share of export to 
Africa of only 1% in 201238. Kenya was the 
primary market in Africa for Mauritian export 
of sugar. In 2022, Mauritius exported around 
66,000 tonnes of sugar, or 23% of total 
exports, to Kenya. 
90.	 In addition to the European Union and 
Eastern Africa (where preferential access in 
the form of duty-free exports is secured under 
the COMESA treaty)39, the Mauritius Sugar 
Syndicate presently exports special sugars to 
over 30 destinations worldwide. The Mauritius 
Sugar Syndicate have set a target to increase 
sales of special sugars to 180,000 tonnes by 
2026.40
91.	 There is also the Free Trade Agreement 
between China and Mauritius, in force since 
January 2021, whereby Mauritius benefits 
from a tariff quota of 50,000 tonnes of sugar, 
which will be implemented over a course 
of 8 years, with an initial quantity of 15,000 
tonnes.41 Additionally, as of 1 January 2021, 
Mauritius is trading with the UK under the UK-
Eastern and Southern Africa (ESA) economic 
partnership agreement (EPA). The agreement 
is a “continuity” agreement that replicates the 
current trade agreement with the EU, and 
consequently maintains preferential access 
on the UK market for Mauritian products, 
including sugar42. 

ACF African Competition Forum
119
2.3.2.	 Local sugar industry
92.	 The 
Mauritius 
Cane 
Industry 
Authority, 
established in 2011 under the Mauritius 
Cane Industry Authority Act 2011, aims 
to promote the sustainable development 
of the cane sector. Its objectives include 
coordinating industry activities, supporting 
sustainable 
development, 
formulating 
policies, overseeing operations, resolving 
disputes, promoting co-products, facilitating 
modern agricultural practices, and enhancing 
competitiveness.43
93.	 The Mauritius Sugar Syndicate (MSS) serves 
as the commercial entity for Mauritius’ sugar 
sector, and is responsible for overseeing 
the marketing and export of all locally 
produced sugar. It operates independently 
and is governed by its members, comprising 
corporate 
and 
independent 
sugarcane 
growers as well as millers. Its departments 
focus on marketing, sales, logistics, finance, 
producer assistance, and ensuring sugar 
quality and food safety.44  
94.	 The sugar industry is vertically integrated, as 
it consists of three private millers who engage 
in production of sugarcane, as well as milling 
and refining of sugar. In 2022, the private 
millers cultivated around 20,814 hectares of 
sugarcane. There is a small number of planters 
who grow sugarcane on a small scale. In 2022, 
small scale planters occupied 21,339 hectares 
for sugarcane cultivation.  
95.	 The millers are namely: Alteo Ltd, Omnicane 
Ltd and Terra Milling Ltd.45 Alteo Ltd is the 
leading sugar producer in Mauritius, and 
produces added value special sugars. It is 
also a leader in the African sugar industry, 
operating two sugar factories in Kenya and

Tanzania.46 Alteo Ltd in particular has 17,700 
hectares of land under sugar cultivation in 
Mauritius and Tanzania; produces 284,000 
tonnes of sugar in Mauritius, Kenya and 
Tanzania per year, and produced 47,000 
tonnes of premium sugars in Mauritius in one 
year.47
96.	 Omnicane Ltd Incorporated, established 
in 1926, is engaged in the cultivation of 
sugarcane and the production of refined 
sugar, 
bioethanol, 
thermal 
energy 
and 
electricity.48 The company has 14,500 hectares 
of land under cane cultivation in the south of 
Mauritius. It produced around 250,000 tonnes 
of refined white sugar produces per year and 
crushes about 1.3 million tonnes of cane per 
year49. The company has also expanded its 
operations in Eastern Africa and the United 
Kingdom.50
97.	 Terra Milling Ltd used to be an essentially 
sugar-oriented company and has diversified 
its activities to become involved in energy 
production, 
manufacturing 
of 
alcoholic 
beverages, 
commercial 
distribution 
and 
property development. Terra Milling Ltd 
operates a sugar mill in Cote D’Ivoire. The 
company has 12,000 hectares of land under 
sugar cane cultivation; 875,000 tonnes of 
sugarcane processed annually from its factory 
area; 93,000 tonnes of sugar produced per 
year; and 85,000 tonnes of specialty sugars, 
such as golden granulated and demerara, are 
sold to international markets per year.

sugars namely, light, extra light or dark 
muscovado, dark brown soft, molasses sugar,

2.3.6.	 Tea 
119.	 Mauritius has a rich history of tea plantation. 
Mauritius was the first member of the Small 
Islands Developing States to develop a 
commercial tea industry.54 The tea plant, also 
known as Camellia Sinensis, was introduced 
in 1760 in Mauritius. In 1817, commercial tea 
cultivation was encouraged by the British 
colonisers. In 1955, thousands of small 
planters benefited from a plot of land for 
tea cultivation, through the Tea Smallholding 
Scheme and the Project Planters Scheme.55   
120.	 In 1960, the Tea Control Board was set up 
to regulate and control the activities of the 
expanding tea sector. In 1970-1971, the 
government brought in the tea intensification 
programme, known as the Mauritius Tea 
Development Authority, following which 
Mauritius became a major player in the 
global tea market. The tea industry grew as 
one of the pillars of the Mauritian economy, 
providing employment to many Mauritians.56 
121.	 In the mid-1980s, the Mauritian tea industry 
faced serious difficulties due to uncertainties 
caused by declining global prices, high costs 
of production, climatic conditions and an 
ageing labour force, amongst others.57  In 
54	
Reigniting the engine of growth A forward-looking business model for the Mauritian tea industry at https://
openknowledge.fao.org/server/api/core/bitstreams/b433dade-a9fa-4b51-b50d-dbac0d0feb3b/content
55	
Organisation des Nations Unies pour alimentation et l’Agriculture at https://www.fao.org/madagascar/actualites/detail-
events/fr/c/1417109/
56	
Organisation des Nations Unies pour alimentation et l’Agriculture at https://www.fao.org/madagascar/actualites/detail-
events/fr/c/1417109/
57	
https://openknowledge.fao.org/server/api/core/bitstreams/b433dade-a9fa-4b51-b50d-dbac0d0feb3b/content
58	
https://www.fao.org/madagascar/actualites/detail-events/en/c/1417109/
59	
https://www.elibrary.imf.org/view/journals/002/1997/030/article-A001-en.xml

Regulatory%20Office/National-Agricultural-Products-Regulatory-Office-%28NAPRO%29.aspx
1994, Mauritius diversified away from tea 
to sugarcane cultivation, where some 2500 
hectares of tea lands were converted for 
sugarcane plantation.58 This was done to fulfil 
the higher-yielding additional quota under 
the SPS Agreement.59 Thus, tea production 
fell by 36%, from 30,300 tonnes in 1993 to 
19,500 tonnes in 1995.60 Mauritius then 
entered a phase of producing tea mainly to 
satisfy local needs.61 
122.	 Eventually, government-led tea factories 
closed and only three private factories 
survived, which included Bois Cheri Estate, 
Corson Tea Estate Co. Ltd and La Chartreuse 
Tea Manufacturing Co. Ltd. In 2017, Mauristea 
Investment Co. Ltd joined the tea sector.  
2.3.7.	 Regulatory body of the tea sector
123.	 Established in 2013, the National Agricultural 
Products Regulatory Office (NAPRO), is 
mandated by the National Agricultural 
Products Regulatory Office Act 2013. 62 
It is a division within the Ministry of Agro 
Industry and Food Security which merges 
the functions of the former Tea Control 
Board and the regulatory functions of the 
ex-Tobacco Board, and the Mauritius Meat 
Authority under the NAPRO Act.  
124.	 It is empowered to: 
•	
control and regulate the import, export, 
production, and sale of regulated products 
(i.e. tea and tea products, tobacco and 
tobacco products, and meat and meat 
products); 
•	
control 
and 
regulate 
the 
preparation, 

ACF African Competition Forum
129
processing, packing, and manufacturing of 
regulated products. 
•	
determine the producer prices for green tea 
leaves and leaf tobacco. 
125.	 Through the NAPRO Act, the government 
implements a policy which allows for the 
importation of black tea for blending 
purposes only.63  
63	
See NAPRO Act 2013 at https://www.mcci.org/media/35835/national-agricultural-products-regulatory-office-act-2013.pdf

2.3.8.	 Local tea industry
126.	 Tea is normally cultivated by factory estates, 
metayers (a person who, under a tea contract 
with a cultivator, cultivates green leaf on 
the land of the cultivator, and sell to the 
cultivator at prices determined by NAPRO),64 
free planters and planters in co-operative 
societies.

128.	 In 2022, 94% of green tea leaves were 
harvested by free planters (45%), metayers 
(27%), and planters in co-operative societies 
(22%).  Disruptions brought on by the 
Covid-19 pandemic led to a fall in the 
production of green leaf tea by 39% in 2020 
and 1.39% in 2021, when compared to the 
previous years (2019 and 2020 respectively). 
129.	 Production of green leaf grew by 26%, 
from 5,034 tonnes in 2021 to 6,351 tonnes 
in 2022. The slight increase in production 
can partly be attributed to the introduction 
of the winter allowance by the government 
in 2018 for small planters.65  The allowance 
aimed at boosting production of tea during 
winter, through which small planters could 
earn a monthly income support of around 
50-75 cents per kg of harvested tea leaves 
during winter. The amount was later revised 
to Rs 2.00 per kg in 2022, to encourage more 
production.66  
2.3.9.	 Tea factories 
130.	 Tea factories process green tea leaves into 
tea. There are currently four privately owned 
tea factories in Mauritius: La Chartreuse Tea 
Manufacturing Co. Ltd, Société Usinière 
de Bois Chéri, Corson Tea Estate Ltd and 
Mauristea Investment Co. Ltd. La Chartreuse 
is considered the market leader in the tea 
market, with 51% market share, followed by 
Bois Cheri at 32% and Corson at 16%.67 
131.	 La Chartreuse Tea Manufacturing Co. Ltd was 
established on 13 January 1999, and started 

ACF African Competition Forum
130
commercial operations in January 2000. The 
factory employs around 200 employees.68 
The tea factory produced more than 50% 
of the local black tea, around 481 tonnes, in 
202269 and earned around US$ 6.1m from 
the local tea market in 2022.70  
132.	 La Chartreuse Tea Manufacturing Co. Ltd 
is also part of Chartreuse Group Ltd.71 
Chartreuse group diversified in the fast-
moving consumer goods field, and is vertically 
integrated in retail and distribution.72  It 
occupies SaveMax as a retail business, 
which operates more than 28 convenience 
stores across the island.73 Chartrade Co. 
Ltd operates as an import, packaging and 
distribution business amongst others, also 
ensuring supply of food commodities in the 
SaveMax retail outlets. La Chartreuse Tea 
partnered with Royal Herbs,74  through which

2.3.12.	 Coffee 
154.	 Coffee is one of the highly consumed 
beverages in Mauritius, with a per capita 
consumption of around 1.05 kg in 2022. 
However, Mauritius only produces annually 
around 10 tonnes of coffee.80 The bulk of the 
local demand is met through importation. 
The import of coffee ranges from around 400 
tonnes to 700 tonnes per annum. Mauritius 
has largely sourced coffee from Switzerland, 
France, Italy, South Africa, Belgium and, to a 
lesser extent, from Brazil, Poland, Germany, 
Guatemala and the United Kingdom.

2.4.	 GRAINS AND LEGUMES 
2.4.1.	 Wheat 
167.	 Wheat is a crucial ingredient in various 
food products, including bread, pasta, and 
pastries. Mauritius has a tropical climate, 
which is not conducive to large-scale wheat 
cultivation. Since there is no domestic 
cultivation of wheat grains, Mauritius relies 
on importation of raw wheat, which is then 
milled into wheat flour.
168.	 Wheat is imported by the only flour mill in the 
country, Les Moulins de La Concorde Ltee 
(“LMLC”), which was established in 1987 and 
is a partnership between Eclosia Group81 (a 
conglomerate which is engaged in poultry 
production, production of livestock feed, 
production of dairy products, and imports of 

ACF African Competition Forum
139
ready-to-eat foods and vegetables, amongst 
other activities), and Namib Mills82, which 
operates several flour mills in Namibia and 
Southern Africa. The main shareholders of 
LMLC83 are Livestock Feed Ltd (29.13%), 
Custos NMI (Pty) Ltd (28.49%), Management 
and Development Company Ltd84 (18.29%) 
and Mauritius Ports Authority85 (8.33%).
169.	 Since 2008, LMLC has concluded an 
agreement with Soufflet, a French group,86 
which is part of Invivo, a leading European 
agricultural group operating across the 
entire food value chain,87 and Office 
National Interprofessionnel des Grandes 
Cultures (ONIGC) pertaining to a guaranteed 
supply of 130,000 tonnes of wheat in case 
of reduced supply of wheat on the world 
market.
170.	 Around 160,000 tonnes of wheat are 
imported annually by LMLC and stored in its 
sixteen (16) silos, to be processed into flour. 
82

LMLC also produces wheat bran for animal 
feed. The silos have a total storage capacity 
of 40,000 tonnes of wheat. LMLC is able to 
produce a wide variety of flour for various 
uses.88 LMLC produced 113,000 metric tons 
of flour and 29,000 metric tons of wheat 
bran, which was used for animal feed in the 
period 2021/2022.89 
171.	 The majority of the flour produced is sold 
to the State Trading Corporation, through 
international tenders, which then redistributes 
it to local suppliers at competitive prices after 
applying government subsidies.  The State 
Trading Corporation is the trading arm of 
the Government and supplies the domestic 
market with wheat flour, basmati and non-
basmati rice.90 This is done to ensure that the 
population can afford flour and bread.91

2.4.2.	 Rice
176.	 Rice is an important dietary staple in 
Mauritius, which is imported to meet 
local consumption requirements. The two 
categories of rice consumed in Mauritius, are 
ration rice, commonly known as Long Grain 
White Rice,92 and luxurious rice, known as 
Basmati Rice.  
177.	 The luxurious “Basmati” rice market is 
primarily imported by private enterprises. 
The STC,93 the commercial arm of the 
Government, 
also 
imports 
luxury 
rice, 
following concerns raised on persistent price 
increases and adulteration in basmati rice. 
Whereas ration rice is imported solely by 
STC, for food security purposes. The ration 
rice is sold at subsidised prices, as it targets 
poorer households in Mauritius.94 
178.	 Figure 27 below illustrates the consumption 
of rice in Mauritius: Luxurious (basmati rice) 
and ration rice over the period 2018 to 2022.

ACF African Competition Forum
141
Figure 27: Trends in consumption of rice, 2018-2022 
Source: Statistics Mauritius, Digest of Agricultural Statistics
179.	 The consumption of basmati rice was around 
61,957 tonnes, reflecting a 71% share in total 
rice consumed, for 2022. The consumption 
of ration rice was around 25,072 tonnes in 
2022. It can also be observed that the volume 
consumed of both types of rice has increased 
over the years. However, luxurious rice 
remained the most consumed rice in Mauritius.

exported

(92%), Seychelles (3%), Pakistan (1%) and 
Taiwan (1%), earning the country a revenue 
of US$283,255. Considering the low volume, 
an export price analysis has been deemed 
unnecessary.  
2.5.	 PROTEIN FOODS
2.5.1.	 Poultry 
183.	 Poultry is an important source of protein in 
Mauritius. The country is self-sufficient in poultry 
meat to an extent, albeit some form of poultry 
is imported to satisfy local needs. The annual 
consumption of chicken meat in Mauritius has 
been increasing, from around 50,000 tonnes in 
2018 to around 56,000 in 2022. 
184.	 Figure 30 below provides the trends in 
domestic production and consumption in 
chicken meat in Mauritius for the period 
2018 to 2022.

ACF African Competition Forum

Figure 30: Domestic production and consumption of chicken meat, 2018-2022
Source: Statistics Mauritius, Agricultural and fish production digest

185.	 A significant increase in consumption and 
production volumes of chicken meat can be 
observed for the period under review. It is 
to be highlighted that production volumes 
decreased to 47,500 tonnes in 2020, mainly 
due to disruptions brought on by the 
Covid-19 pandemic. 
2.5.2.	 Imports of poultry 
186.	 Importation of poultry and poultry products 
(frozen, fresh or chilled meat) into Mauritius 
are subject to an import permit,96 which is 
based on the Animal Diseases Act (1925) 
and Food Regulation introduced in 1999. 
Veterinary approval and inspection of the 
cold/chilled room is mandatory for the issue 
of the import permit. Livestock and Veterinary 
Division97 is the regulatory body responsible 
for the issue of import permit and verification 
of cold storages at the registered importer’s 
premises.
187.	 Poultry is imported in the form of turkey 
meat, gallus domesticus fowls’ meat, duck 
meat and poultry meat, amongst others. 
Mauritius imported poultry products at low 
volumes, around 500 to 900 tonnes, for 
the period under consideration. Mauritius 
sourced poultry primarily from Brazil, France, 
Malaysia, Netherlands and Poland, and to 
a lesser extent from Portugal, Seychelles, 
Spain and Taiwan.

decreased from 750 tonnes in 2018 to 513

in 2018 to 55,700 tonnes in 2022.

2.6.1.	 Onions
198.	 Mauritius relies on both production and 
importation of onion to meet the local 
demand. The importation of onions is 
regulated by the Agricultural Marketing 
Board (AMB), which is itself a major player in 
the market. The AMB imports between 50% 
- 60% of the total import requirement, and 
issues import permits to private enterprises 
for importation and supply of the remaining 
local requirement.98
199.	 Onions are regarded as an essential 
commodity, as it is commonly used in the 
local Mauritian cuisine. The current local 
consumption of onions ranges from around 
17,000 - 18,000 tonnes per annum. Local 
production of onions merely meets around 
20%-40% of the national requirement. As 
such, Mauritius relies heavily on imports from 
India, the Netherlands and Egypt to satisfy 
local demand. 
Figure 33: Trends in consumption, production and imports of onions, 2018 - 2022 
Source: Statistics Mauritius, Agricultural and fish production digest
200.	 Figure 33 above depicts the trends in 
consumption, production and importation 
of onions for the period 2018 to 2022. As 
observed, consumption of onions has ranged 
around 17,000 to 18,000 tonnes for the 
period under consideration. Between 2018 
and 2020, local production varied around 
3,500 tonnes per annum. Local production 
surged significantly to 5,590 tonnes in 2021 
and 7,443 tonnes in 2022. This can partly 
be explained through subsidies provided 
to small planters on purchase of seeds sold 
by the Agricultural Marketing Board (AMB) 
and on fertilisers, resulting in an increase in 
production of onions by small planters.99 
201.	 Figure 34 below provides the imported 
volume and value of onions in Mauritius for 
the period 2018 to 2022.      

ACF African Competition Forum

Source: MCCI, Trade statistics

of onions at US$ 498 per tonne, which 
decreased to 10,696 tonnes of onions at 
US$ 646 per tonne in 2022. This decrease 
can partially be explained by an increase in 
the local production of onions, from 3,440 
tonnes in 2018 to 7,443 tonnes in 2022.

208.	 Fresh tomatoes were no longer imported 
as from 2020, following a ban on their 
importation.101 As a result, the country has 
had to become self-sufficient in tomato 
production. In the year 2022, tomatoes were 
cultivated over a total area of 536 hectares, 
with an average annual production of 14,269 
tonnes,102 compared to a total cultivation 
area of 655 hectares with an average annual 
production of 13,832 tonnes in the year 
2021.103 The yield of tomatoes increased 
despite a reduction in the area of cultivation. 
209.	 The local tomato production industry is 
estimated to be worth $ 14 million.104 
Farmers are provided with seeds by the Food 
and Agricultural Research and Extension 
Institute (FAREI), and the following varieties 
of tomatoes are planted and harvested in 
Mauritius: Summer King, Summer Star, and 
Rising Star105. Additionally, production of 
tomatoes in greenhouses106 has increased, 
with the following varieties of tomatoes 
being harvested: San Marzano, Beef Heart 
and Cherry tomatoes. 
2.7.	 CITRUS FRUITS
210.	 Citrus fruits belong to the Rutaceae family 
and are one of the most widely grown 
perennial fruit crops in the world. Citrus 
fruits constitute a crucial source of vitamin C. 
Among the most cultivated types, oranges 
account for more than half of world citrus 
production. In 2022, the global production 
of oranges amounted to 76m107 tonnes.
211.	 In Mauritius, the average production of citrus 
fruits averages 1,550 tonnes per annum. 
The production comprises 800 tonnes of 
mandarins, 500 tonnes of lemons and 250 
tonnes of other citrus fruits annually.   
212.	 Mauritius’ consumption of citrus fruits 
revolves around 11,000 to 13,000 tonnes per 
annum. Orange remains the most preferred 
citrus fruit, with its consumption ranging 
from 4,500 to 5,500 tonnes per year. This is 
followed by mandarins (3,000-4,000 tonnes) 
and lemons (1,000-1,800 tonnes).  
213.	 The country imports citrus fruits in the form 
of oranges, mandarins, lemons, grapefruits, 
clementines and other citrus hybrids. The 
imports of citrus fruits ranges from 9,000 to 
11,000 tonnes per annum. The country has 
traditionally sourced citrus fruits from South 
Africa, Egypt, Morrocco, China and, to a 
lesser extent, from Pakistan and Spain.   
214.	 Figure 
36 
below 
provides 
the 
total 
consumption and production of citrus fruits 
in Mauritius for the period 2018 to 2022.  

ACF African Competition Forum

amounted to 12,365 tonnes per annum. 
Local production of citrus fruits met around 
13% of the total consumption, while 87% was 
provided through importation.
