imported mandarins from South Africa, 
Egypt and, to a lesser extent, from Pakistan 
and Morocco. It can be observed that, as 
from 2020, imports of mandarins declined 
significantly from Pakistan, which was subject 
to changing weather conditions, while citrus 
diseases like Citrus Canker and fruit flies 
affected Pakistan’s citrus production.110 As a 
result, Mauritius diverted towards alternative 
markets such as Morocco.   
220.	 Mauritius has traditionally imported more 
than 90% of its lemons requirements from 
South Africa and Egypt; the top producers of 
lemons in Africa.111   
221.	 In 2018, Mauritius imported clementines 
primarily from South Africa, and to a lesser 
extent from Italy. It was observed that 
clementines were not imported from South 
Africa in 2020 and 2022. This was largely 
110	 See page 17 of https://fpcci.org.pk/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/Exploring-Value-Addition-Opportunities-and-Export-
Potential-A-Case-of-Citrus-Industry.pdf
111	 See https://ourworldindata.org/agricultural-production
112	 See https://www.citrusresourcewarehouse.org.za/home/document-home/news-articles/south-african-fruit-journal-safj/
sa-fruit-journal-2020/6763-sa-fruit-journal-aug-sept-2020-overview-of-clementines/file
on account of adverse weather patterns 
affecting production volumes in the Western 
Cape province, which accounts for around 
60%112 of clementine land cultivation in 
South Africa. Mauritius imported clementines 
entirely from Spain in 2022.  
222.	 Between 2018 and 2022, Mauritius has 
imported grapefruits primarily from South 
Africa, China and Egypt, and to a lesser 
extent from India, Israel and Turkey. Other 
citrus hybrids were imported in low volumes, 
mostly from Pakistan.

224.	 Table 47 above shows the evolution of 
Mauritius’ import prices of citrus fruits from 
Africa and other territories for the period 
2018 to 2022. It can be observed that, prior 
to 2020, the price of oranges from Africa was 
declining. As from 2020, the prices surged 
to US$ 0.75 per kg, which can partially be 
explained by an increase in prices from 
source countries due to the knock-on effect 
of the Covid-19 pandemic, resulting in 
global demand worldwide for products rich 
in Vitamin C113.   
225.	 The import prices of mandarins from African 
countries varied between US$ 1.10 per kg 
to US$ 1.27 per kg, between 2018 to 2022. 
Import prices from other countries varied 
between US$ 0.63 per kg to US$ 0.94 per 
kg.  It was observed that average import 
prices from other countries were 65% lower 
on average than from African countries. 
Amongst other countries, Mauritius imported 
mandarins mainly from Pakistan, which sells 
mandarins at lower prices globally. 
226.	 The import prices of lemons from African 
countries ranged between US$ 0.95 per 
kg to US$ 1.12 per kg for the period under 
consideration. Import prices from other 
countries varied between US$ 1.17 per kg to 
US$ 2.88 per kg.  It was seen that the average 
prices from African countries were 32% lower 
on average than from non-African sources.  
227.	 As far as clementines are concerned, 
Mauritius has imported clementines in the 
price range of US$ 0.71 per kg to US$ 1.31 
per kg from African countries. The import 
prices from other countries varied between 
US$ 1.20 per kg to US$ 6.20 per kg. In 2022, 
Mauritius imported clementines from Spain 
at US$ 6.20 per kg, which has been subject 
to low citrus supply and persistent inflation 
(rising from 3.09% in 2021 to 8.32% in 
2022).114 
228.	 Mauritius imported grapefruits from African 
countries at prices ranging from US$ 0.88 
per kg to US$ 0.92 per kg, whilst import 
prices from other countries at US$ 0.85 per 
kg to US$ 1.05 per kg. It was observed that, 
on average, Mauritius imported grapefruits 
at prices lower by 13% from Africa, than from 
other sources.  
229.	 For other citrus hybrids fruits, the import 
prices from African countries varied from 
US$ 1.03 per kg to US$ 3.08 per kg. Import 
prices of other countries ranged between 
US$ 0.58 per kg and US$ 1.30 per kg.

230.	 Import (CIF) prices ranged from $0.62 to 
$0.75 per kg. Mauritius imported oranges at 
$0.62 per kg in 2018 rising to $0.72 per kg 
in 2022, mainly due to an increase in freight 
and insurance costs, from $0.13 per kg in 
2018 to $0.20 per kg in 2022. 
231.	 The world price of oranges ranged from 
US$ 0.79 per kg to US$ 0.92 per kg. When 
benchmarked against the world prices, it 
can be observed that for 2018 and 2022 the 
average import price of oranges was lower 
than the world price. Whereas, between 
2019 to 2021, the import prices of orange 
were higher than world price.

115	 The COMESA is a regional bloc of 21 member states: Burundi, Comoros, Democratic Republic of Congo, Djibouti, 
Egypt, Eritrea, Eswatini, Ethiopia, Kenya, Libya, Madagascar, Malawi, Mauritius, Rwanda, Seychelles, Somalia, Sudan, 
Tunisia, Uganda, Zambia and Zimbabwe.
116	 SADC constitutes of 16 member states, namely: Angola, Botswana, Comoros, Democratic Republic of Congo, Eswatini, 
Lesotho, Madagascar, Malawi, Mauritius, Mozambique, Namibia, Seychelles, South Africa, United Republic of Tanzania, 
Zambia and Zimbabwe.  
237.	 This chapter analyses the regional trade 
dynamics at the level of COMESA115 and 
SADC,116 Mauritius being part of these 
regional economic communities, for the 
selected 
agricultural 
inputs 
and 
food 
products for the period 2018 to 2022.  
238.	 It is to be highlighted that there are eight 
member states which have overlapping 
membership with SADC and COMESA. 
These are Comoros, Democratic Republic 
of Congo, Eswatini, Madagascar, Mauritius, 
Seychelles, Zambia and Zimbabwe. 
239.	 For the purpose of the analysis of Mauritius’ 
trade within SADC and COMESA, the same 
figures for trade with the overlapping 
members have been reported for each bloc. 
3.1.	 FERTILISER 
240.	 Mauritius has largely sourced fertilisers 
outside Africa from the biggest producers: 
China, Belgium and Vietnam, for the period 
2018 to 2022.    
241.	 A comparative analysis of Mauritius’ import 
volume of fertilisers from Africa and non-
African countries is provided in Figure 40 
below.

ACF African Competition Forum
160
242.	 In 2018, Mauritius imported 43,514 tonnes 
of fertilisers from non-African countries, 
representing 96% of the total fertilisers’ 
import volume. In 2022, this volume fell to 
18,520 tonnes, representing 70% of the 
import volume. Simultaneously, a significant 
increase in the import volume of fertilisers 
from Africa was observed during the five-
year period under consideration. Imports 
from Africa rose from 1,686 tonnes in 2018 
to 7,939 tonnes in 2022.
117	 In the illustrations, reference is made to imports from COMESA (Egypt), SADC (South Africa), COMESA/SADC 
(Madagascar) and other African Countries (Morocco and Sierra Leone)
243.	 The majority of Mauritius’ intra-Africa trade, 
regarding imports of fertilisers, comes from 
the COMESA and SADC regions, which 
benefit from duty-free access. 
244.	 A breakdown of imports of fertilisers from 
SADC and COMESA for the period under 
review is provided in Figure 41 below.117   
 
Figure 41: Mauritius’ fertiliser imports from Africa, COMESA and SADC, 2018-2022
Source: MCCI, trade statistics
245.	 In 2022, Mauritius imported 30% of its fertiliser 
requirements from Africa; which principally 
came from South Africa (18.2%), Morocco 
(6.1%), Egypt (3.5%) and Madagascar (2.3%). 
246.	 It is to be highlighted that nitrogenous 
fertilisers were the most imported form of 
fertiliser in 2022, which were around 15,207 
tonnes out of 26,409 tonnes. Out of 15,207 
tonnes, Mauritius imported 25% (3,848 
tonnes) of its requirements from South Africa, 
which forms part of SADC.

ACF African Competition Forum
161
247.	 The second most imported form of fertilisers, 
in 2022, were NPK fertilisers totalling around 
7,728 tonnes, which accounted for 29% of 
total import volume of fertilisers. The country 
imported 1,193 tonnes and 622 tonnes 
(representing each around 15% and 8% of 
its NPK fertiliser requirements) from Egypt, a 
COMESA member state, and Morocco.
248.	 The country also imported 3,278 tonnes of 
potassic fertilisers in 2022, representing 
around 12% of the total fertiliser import 
volume. Mauritius sourced 774 tonnes 
and 410 tonnes from South Africa, a SADC 
member state, and Morocco respectively, 
which represented around 36% of the 
potassic fertiliser’s import volume.

China and Vietnam.

3.2.	 SUGAR
253.	 Africa has become an important market for 
Mauritian sugar exports, rising from 99,905 
tonnes in 2018 (out of 279,018 tonnes) to 
116,483 tonnes in 2022 (out of 331,427 
tonnes).

rose from 76,398 in 2018 to  89,906 tonnes 
in 2022, representing an increase of 18%. 
Of the total export of white sugar to Africa 
in 2022, 53% was destined for Kenya and 
Madagascar, both being part of COMESA. 
White cane sugar exports to non-African 
countries 
significantly 
decreased, 
from 
99,389 tonnes in 2018 to 81,315 tonnes in 
2022.

in white cane sugar has risen from 62,666

(53%) in 2022, while exports to SADC have

to 19,307 tonnes in 2022 (15%). It is to be

3.3.	 CITRUS FRUITS
266.	 Africa has grown into an important citrus 
producer over the years. Egypt (COMESA 
member state), Morocco and South Africa 
(SADC member state) are the world’s largest 
exporters. These countries feature strongly 
in almost all categories of citrus exports, 
including oranges, mandarins, lemons and 

ACF African Competition Forum
166
grapefruits.118 Mauritius has largely relied on 
these top African producers for importation 
of citrus fruits. Mauritius’ high intra-Africa 
trade pertaining to importation of citrus 
fruits is largely driven by the duty-free access 
under COMESA and SADC regional blocs. 
118	 See https://www.howwemadeitinafrica.com/africas-citrus-industry-buoyant-despite-global-shocks/145058/

from 10,017 tonnes (out of 10,301 tonnes) 
in 2018 to 11,505 tonnes (out of 11,906 
tonnes) in 2022. The import share of non-
African countries increased from 284 tonnes 
in 2018 to 401 tonnes in 2022.

countries from 2018 to 2022, indicating that 
Mauritius sourced lemons at better prices 
from these regional trading blocs compared 
to other countries.

applicable in respect of refined oil imports

for COMESA non- FTA member states.

imports, particularly from Madagascar (12%).
276.	 The comparative analysis of the import price 
(USD/Kg) for the different types of edible oil 
imported showed that the import price from 
African countries was lower than that of other 
sources. For instance, for crude sunflower oil, 
the import price from African countries was 
lower by 89%; for refined sunflower oil, the 
import price from African countries was on 
average 45% lower; for refined palm oil, the 
import price from African countries was 6% 
lower; for refined soybean oil, the import 
price from African countries was lower by 
14.8%. The analysis of import prices indicates 
that imports from the COMESA/SADC region 
are beneficial to Mauritius in terms of price 
competitiveness. 
277.	 For exports of crude soybean oil and refined 
soybean oil in 2022, the COMESA/SADC 
region once more was the main destination, 
principally to Madagascar. 
3.5.	 	POULTRY
278.	 With regard to importation of chicken 
meat, Mauritius is self-sufficient. However, it 
imports other forms of poultry meat in lower 
volumes. While looking at regional trade, 
importation of meat from Africa accounted 
for less than 1% in 2022, where the import 
volume of poultry, namely, gallus domesticus 
meat, was from the Seychelles (0.6 tonnes), 
which is a member of both COMESA and 
SADC.  

ACF African Competition Forum
170
279.	 Export destinations of cuts and offal of frozen 
fowls and edible meat and offal of ducks, 
albeit of an insignificant volume, was to the 
Seychelles. 
3.6.	 	TEA
280.	 For tea, Mauritius imported black tea 
principally from Kenya (a SADC member 
state). South Africa only represented 1% of 
imports of black tea. The import price (USD/
kg) in 2022 was lower than the global price 
(by 2.6%), suggesting that Mauritius obtained 
better prices for its imports from Kenya. 
3.7.	 	COFFEE
281.	 In 2022, Mauritius sourced 14% of coffee 
from South Africa, which forms part of SADC, 
while the rest of the coffee imports mostly 
came from Switzerland, France and Italy. In 
terms of exportation, Mauritius exported 
coffee in lower volumes to Madagascar 
(77%) and Seychelles (23%), which are both 
part of RECs, COMESA and SADC. 
3.8.	 	WHEAT
282.	 Mauritius relies on imports of wheat for 
production of flour. Over the period 2018 
to 2022, wheat was mostly imported from 
non-African countries, namely France and 
Australia.
3.9.	 	RICE
283.	 There are no regional imports for rice (long 
grain or luxurious). The major sources of 
imports are India (66%) and Pakistan (31%). 
However, in 2022, Mauritius exported rice 
to Madagascar (92%) and Seychelles (3%), 
both forming part of the COMESA and SADC 
regional trading blocs. 
3.10.	TOMATOES
284.	 With regard to tomatoes, there is a ban 
on importation of fresh tomatoes. Using 
an import substitution strategy, Mauritius 
has been able to achieve self-sufficiency in 
production of tomatoes over the past few 
years, rising from 9,190 tonnes in 2018 to 
14,269 tonnes in 2022. This can be explained 
by the increasing adoption of soilless 
agriculture by farmers in Mauritius, which 
basically refers to growing tomatoes under 
protected structures.     
3.11.	SEEDS FOR SOWING
285.	 For the period under review, the most 
imported type of seeds by Mauritius were 
for vegetable, which mainly came from 
India. It was also observed that, in 2022, 
seeds for plants, namely Lucerne, forage and 
herbaceous, were imported in low volumes 
from South Africa, which forms part of the 
SADC region. In terms of exportation of 
seeds, Mauritius exported only fruits and 
spores in negligible volumes. 
3.12.	ONIONS 
286.	 With regard to importation of onions, 
Mauritius sourced the majority from India 
and Netherlands and, to a lesser extent, 
from African countries from 2018 to 2022. 
In 2022, Mauritius imported 1,736 tonnes 
(out of 10,696 tonnes) from Egypt (COMESA 
member state) and 73 tonnes from South 
Africa (SADC member state).

287.	 The study aimed at understanding the 
regional trade dimension of highly traded 
food products and agricultural inputs, 
through an emphasis on cross-border trade 
flows of these products, as well as any 
potential competition concerns in relation to 
these identified products. 
288.	 An assessment of the trade flow data was 
carried out for the period 2018 to 2022, 
which covered important food categories 
including agricultural inputs, vegetable oils, 
grains, protein, fruits and vegetables, and 
other commodities like sugar, tea and coffee. 
289.	 Mauritius’ intra-trade with Africa has been 
progressively increasing; with rising imports 
from Africa from US$ 747m in 2018 to US$ 
890m in 2022, and exports to Africa growing 
from US$ 522m in 2018 to US$ 620m in 2022. 
119During this period, Mauritius has primarily 
sourced certain products, namely fertilisers, 
sugar, tea and citrus fruits amongst others, 
from Africa at various costs.   
290.	 As highlighted in the above chapters, 
Mauritius’ imports of fertilisers from Africa 
have increased from 1,142 tonnes (1%) in 
2018 to 6,849 tonnes (26%) in 2022: mostly 
from South Africa. The analysis tends to show 
that import prices for nitrogenous fertilisers 
are higher from African countries than from 
other sources. Simon Roberts (2017)120 
highlighted issues such as presence of cartels 
and high prices in Southern Africa’s fertiliser 
sector that may have parallels in Mauritius. 
Addressing these problems could enable 
Mauritius to access more competitively 
priced fertilisers. Enhancing regional trade 
within Africa, particularly with South Africa 
serving as a hub for fertilisers, could offer 
Mauritius cost benefits and reduce its 
reliance on imports from outside the African 
continent. This potentially depends on 
various factors, including freight costs.    
291.	 The study analysed sugar; the main cash 
crop of Mauritius. The sugarcane sector in 
Mauritius is an integrated system that not only 
produces sugar, but also generates electricity 
for local consumption, as well as by-products 
such as alcohol, molasses, and bagasse. This 
integration with electricity generation and 
renewable energy sources makes domestic 
policies particularly important. While the 
study focused on sugar, it is essential to 
broaden the scope to include the entire 
sugarcane sector. The price analysis for white 
sugar tends to show that domestic prices of 
white sugar are higher when benchmarked 
against world and export prices. Further 
analysis of the sugarcane supply chain is 
necessary to understand the underlying 
factors behind the high local sugar prices.
292.	 With 
regard 
to 
the 
tea 
sector, 
through 
the 
NAPRO 
Act, 
the 
government 
implemented 
a 
policy,121 
which allows for importation of black tea 
only for blending purposes. The study found 
that Mauritius primarily sourced black tea 
from Kenya, only for blending purposes. 
When benchmarked against prices of other 
4
CONCLUSION 

ACF African Competition Forum
172
countries, the domestic tea prices tend to be 
higher. Mauritius has implemented strong 
domestic policies to protect its domestic 
industries, particularly in the sugar and tea 
sectors. These policies have led to higher 
prices domestically for these products, 
which might have been more competitive 
otherwise.  
293.	 As far as poultry is concerned, Mauritius is 
self-sufficient in production of poultry meat, 
with local production revolving around 
50,000 tonnes per annum. The country has 
imported, to a lesser extent, other forms of 
poultry, particularly turkey meat. With regard 
to regional trade, importation of poultry from 
Africa accounted for less than 1% in 2022. 
Mauritius’ reliance on Africa for inputs in 
livestock feed is low.       
294.	 One such significant input for animal feed 
analysed in the study was oilcake: which 
is obtained through extraction of oil from 
soybeans. Trade data analysed for the period 
2018 to 2022 indicated that Mauritius relied 
traditionally on imports of oilcake from 
South American countries: Argentina and 
Paraguay. Interestingly, Mauritius imported 
oilcake from South Africa in 2022, albeit at a 
low volume (0.35%).  There is considerable 
scope for further strengthening regional 
trade with Africa.  
295.	 Mauritius’ reliance on Africa for agricultural 
inputs and food commodities is relatively 
low. Mauritius mostly depends on other 
countries for seeds, oilcake, and edible oils, 
sourcing these from major producers such 
as Indonesia, Malaysia, China, and Belgium. 
Given that Africa itself is a large importer of 
these commodities, the question arises as to 
how regional trade can be better exploited.

1.	
Agriculture plays a significant role in the South 
African economy, as it contributes to food 
security, job creation, raw material supply to 
agro-industrial and manufacturing sectors, 
as well as export-driven foreign exchange 
income.1 The Competition Commission of 
South Africa has prioritised the food and 
agro-processing sector in its Prioritisation 
Framework since 2008, due to the sector 
being a source of staple foods, as well as the 
potential of the sector to create significant 
employment opportunities – thereby serving 
as a driver of inclusive growth in the South 
African economy.2 
2.	
Similarly, the significance of agriculture holds 
true for the rest of the African continent as 
well. Africa’s agriculture sector accounts for 
about 35% of the continent’s gross domestic 
product, and provides the livelihoods of more 
than 50% of the continent’s population.3 
Agriculture is at the core of fostering economic 
growth, reducing poverty, and improving 
food security on the continent.4
3.	
Recent supply shocks, such as the Covid-19 
pandemic and the Russian Ukraine war, 
along with climate change, and the high 
food inflation environment, have shown the 
vulnerability of the African food system, and 
the need to increase resilience and food 
security. In South Africa, Agri-food markets 
are generally characterised by high import 
dependencies, low prices for Small and 
Medium Enterprise (SME) producers, and 
high prices for end consumers – which are 
often related to the concentration of market 
power of large integrated firms that operate 
along regional or international value chains.5 
4.	
In recent years, African agriculture and 
food markets have undergone notable 
transformations, including the expansion of 
global input suppliers, increased agricultural 
production by medium and large-scale 
farmers, as well as the expansion of 
commodity traders through key acquisitions.6  
Furthermore, while there has been relatively 
strong growth in agricultural output in many 
African countries since 2000,7 food processing 
has not seen the same growth as observed 
in agricultural output production. It is also 
observed that there are vast differences in the 
trade flows of agricultural markets between 
1
INTRODUCTION

ACF African Competition Forum
179
African 
countries, 
highlighting 
concerns 
about how well markets may be working for 
African consumers and producers. 
5.	
There has been increased attention on 
regional integration as a contributor to 
African economic development, and the 
African Continental Free Trade Area (AfCFTA) 
is an important step towards the long-term 
goal of regional integration, which can 
provide great impetus for regional trade and 
industrialisation.8 In a recent article, AfCFTA 
Secretary-general, Mr Wamkele Mene, has 
highlighted that conflicts in Ukraine and the 
Middle East have disrupted global supply 
chains, and are affecting food security in 
Africa. He noted that the inflationary pressure 
resulting from these conflicts means that 
the continent needs to focus on, “boosting 
food production and ramp up intra-African 
trade,”9 and so this context further emphasises 
regional integration and more opportunities 
for cross-border trade, especially for other 
countries to sell agricultural products to South 
Africa. 
6.	
As firms are integrated into value chains, 
be it in the form of ownership relations, 
strategic partnerships, as well as distribution 
agreements, a regional perspective to firm 
decision-making – such as that which relates 
to investment and location of production, is 
necessary. Ordinarily, given scale economies 
and relatively small national markets, firms 
make decisions across countries in a region. 
Studies indicate the huge potential for regional 
and continental cooperation, to bring win-win 
outcomes through increased value addition 
and upgrading at the firm, value chain, and 
industry levels across national boundaries.10 
8	
Black, A., Edwards, L., Gorven, R., Mapulanga, W. 2022. How agro-processing can boost regional integration and 
development. How agro-processing can boost regional integration – and development | SA-TIED (unu.edu)
9	
Magubane, K. 2024. Wamkele Mene, the African Continental Free Trade Area secretary-general, says contintent must act 
as global conflicts hit food security. Sunday Times (date: 10 March 2024). 
10	
Fessehaie, J., Roberts, S., & Takala-Greenish, L. 2015. Key findings from case studies of mining capital equipment, 
infrastructure inputs and soy agro-processing in Southern Africa and implications for regional industrial development 
agenda. Pretoria: TIPS. CCRED.
7.	
Furthermore, a value-chain approach to trade 
in Africa and across different regions can 
also assist in showing the ultimate product 
beneficiation where, for example, a product 
like fertiliser is a significant input into various 
other agricultural products, and as such 
there is a multiplier effect in increasing the 
productivity of other sectors or industries 
related to a product like fertiliser. As a key part 
of food value chains, it is well established that 
agro-processing has the potential to drive 
economic growth via the potential for higher 
productivity, scale economies and linkages. 
Agro-processing 
activities 
have 
strong 
linkages with other activities in an industry. 
These include backward linkages that extend 
to primary agriculture (such as manufacture 
of chemical inputs like fertiliser) as well as 
forward linkages that include packaging 
industries, logistics, storage, and retail and 
wholesale services.
8.	
As such, the purpose of this study is to 
understand 
what 
regional 
economic 
activity there is in the agriculture and agro-
processing sector. The study identifies trends 
in respect of trade flows over the period 
2017 to 2022 and, where possible, indicates 
the limiting factors to the potential for more 
economic activity between African countries, 
and across regional economic communities. 
Further, the study identifies, where possible, 
opportunities that exist to strengthen the 
regional economic activity in these sectors. 
Furthermore, the study highlights where there 
might be competition issues in the agriculture 
and agro-processing sector across regions, 
and explore what further research could be 
done.

ACF African Competition Forum
180
11	
The Harmonised System is an international nomenclature for the classification of products. It allows participating 
countries to classify traded goods on a common basis for customs purposes. At the international level, the Harmonised 
System (HS) for classifying goods is a six-digit code system. The first 4 digits are called the tariff heading and represent a 
broad category of products. 
2.1.	 STUDY OBJECTIVES
9.	
Considering 
the 
increased 
focus 
on 
regional integration and value addition as 
possible contributors to Africa’s economic 
development, this study seeks to use trade 
flow data and related research to better 
understand the current regional economic 
activity in agriculture and agro-processing. 
The study aims to provide a regional focus 
on important, highly-traded food products 
and agricultural inputs, via an emphasis on 
cross-border trade flows of these products, 
as well as any potential competition concerns 
in relation to these identified products. More 
specifically, the ACF study objectives include 
the following:
9.1.	 	To understand the dynamics of regional 
value chains in the agriculture and agro-
processing 
sectors 
in 
ACF 
member 
countries. The focus will be to explore the 
trade (export and import) flows of countries 
within the region, identify what food value 
chains are being traded currently, and 
consider average pricing trends in food 
value chains.
9.2.	 To understand the potential and current 
competition issues within certain value 
chains, and suggest what future research 
might investigate in relation to interventions 
that could be required at a regional level. 
9.3.	 To provide an overview of factors which 
facilitate 
regional 
trade, 
as 
well 
as 
those aspects which hinder increased 
agricultural trade in Africa – taking into 
 
account regulatory features and feasible 
opportunities for furthering regional trade. 
10.	 The location of agricultural trade patterns 
across the different regions, along with 
regional and product-related trade insights 
and opportunities, would then further pave 
the way for additional and future research 
into certain problematic food value chains, or 
simply those food value chains that warrant 
more intensive analysis going forward. 
2.2.	 SCOPE OF THE STUDY
11.	 The scope of the study relates to the cross-
border analysis of trade flow patterns (exports 
and imports) for products in the agriculture 
and agro-processing sector, at a global level, 
continent level (i.e. trade at the African level), 
and trade at a regional economic community 
level (such as trade at the Southern African 
Development 
Community 
‘SADC’ 
level), 
along with any further country-level trade 
insights and opportunities for increased trade 
more broadly. The relevant period for which 
trade data is analysed covers the 2017 to 
2022 period. 
12.	 The study relies on the Harmonised System 
codes, at a 4-digit level, for trade flow data,11 
and as a research guide, the selected food 
products arise from the following key food 
categories:
12.1.	Agricultural inputs, such as fertiliser, seeds 
for sowing, and oilseed cake;
12.2.	Vegetable oils, such as sunflower oil and 
palm oil;
2
OVERVIEW OF THE STUDY

ACF African Competition Forum
181
12.3.	Grains and legumes, such as maize, wheat, 
rice and soybeans;
12.4.	Protein foods, such as poultry;
12.5.	Vegetables, like onions;
12.6.	Fruits, like citrus fruits;
12.7.	Beverages and other commodities, 
including coffee, tea and sugar. 
13.	 The scope of the South African chapter 
includes the following key food products, 
namely: fertilisers, seeds for sowing, oilseed 
cake, sunflower oil (crude), maize, poultry, 
onions, citrus fruit, coffee, tea, and sugar. 
2.3.	 RESEARCH METHODOLOGY
14.	 The ACF study incorporates both quantitative 
and qualitative research methods, and utilises 
a combination of publicly available primary 
and secondary sources of information. Primary 
data in the study relates to the quantitative 
trade flow data, including annual export and 
import volume, and value data (and thus 
average prices per unit),12 sourced by each 
country’s national statistics base, or a trade 
online database in relation to their respective 
trade flows for important and traded food 
products at the different geographic trade 
levels (i.e. global, continental, or regional).
15.	 Secondary and qualitative data related to 
the study includes data sources from various 
economic reports, surveys, publications, and 
online reports, which were used to research 
and 
understand 
certain 
trade-related 
observations from the data.
16.	 The ACF study incorporates these kinds of 
data, using the primary, SARS-based ITC 
Trade Map data, on an annual basis, to track 
export and import values and volumes over 
the 2017 to 2022 period. This data covers the 
important products traded (as noted above) 
12	
The average export and import prices are calculated by dividing the annual trade value (in dollar terms) by the annual 
trade volume (in tons) for each product to get an average dollar per ton price. 
13	
 The annual data in Trade Map for products at 2, 4, and 6-digit level of the Harmonised System are mainly based on UN 
Comtrade, the world’s largest database of trade statistics, maintained by the United Nations Statistics Division (UNSD). 
This data is complemented by national sources, when the information is not available in UN Comtrade. The quarterly 
and monthly data come from national and regional sources.
for South Africa in relation to the different 
geographic trade levels, and the annual 
data accounts for seasonality effects which 
are commonly seen in agricultural markets. 
The time period analysed also accounts 
for, and would capture movements in trade 
flows related to, the pre-Covid, Covid, and 
post-Covid rebound period, as well as likely 
impacts from the Ukraine-Russian conflict in 
the 2021/2022 period. 
17.	 In terms of the ITC Trade Map data, the data 
follows international standards in that exports 
are reported in Free on Board (FOB) terms, 
while imports are reported in Cost, Insurance 
and Freight (CIF) terms. As noted above, ITC’s 
trade data is based on South African Revenue 
Services (SARS) data, and global data is based 
on the United Nations COMTRADE statistics.13 

ACF African Competition Forum
182
14	
This section includes World Bank Commodity price data where available and where most relevant/comparable, which 
applies to the following products: fertiliser; maize; chicken/poultry; coffee; tea; and sugar. The data has also been 
converted to a $/kg metric for ease of comparison (if the commodity data was captured in any other measurement).

The prices are volume-weighted average prices
18.	 This section covers the analysis of bilateral 
trade statistics for South Africa’s trade, in 
terms of import and export data (volume and 
value), with different geographic regions. The 
study first assesses the overall trade flows 
and pricing trends with South Africa, per 
product, on an African level (which is assessed 
alongside 
South 
Africa’s 
corresponding 
global trade), and at the relevant regional 
level – that being its trade with the SADC 
region as a whole. 
19.	 Where comparable data exists, international 
benchmarking is included by way of tracking 
the World Bank’s commodity price data, 
relative to average import and export prices, 
as calculated from ITC Trade Map data.14 
Furthermore, the trade of a narrower set of 
commonly traded, key food products (namely 
maize, sugar, coffee, tea) is also analysed on an 
individual country basis, to map out examples 
of trade on a more disaggregated basis, and 
to consider how this might add to insights 
from other geographic levels of trade. 
20.	 Furthermore, this section of the study closes 
with a brief examination of trade and regulatory 
elements, emphasising both facilitators and 
hindrances to increased agricultural trade 
in Africa. It explores feasible opportunities 
and insights aimed at advancing regional 
trade. Factors that ease regional trade are 
highlighted alongside those that impede it, 
providing an understanding of the challenges 
and potential avenues for improvement in 
fostering agricultural trade across Africa. 
21.	 The selection of agricultural food products 
for the study relates to the importance these 
products play in terms of their contribution 
to the average consumer’s grocery basket 
(and especially for poorer households), 
their nutritional and strategic importance 
for agriculture, and the products having a 
significant amount of trade (volume and value) 
data recorded relative to a similar product in 
each food category. The selection of food 
items, therefore, creates a comprehensive 
overview of key traded food products, which 
can assist in identifying current trade flows 
at different geographic levels of trade, and 
what this might reveal or point to in terms of 
competition issues, or areas of improvement 
for continental and regional trade going 
forward. 
3.1.	 SOUTH AFRICAN TRADE FLOWS AT THE 
AFRICAN AND GLOBAL LEVEL
22.	 This section provides an overview of South 
Africa’s trade flows and average prices15 with 
the rest of Africa and with the world – of which 
the trade data recorded for the world also 
contains the trades recorded for Africa, which 
means there is a degree of overlap in the 
trade flow data at the different regional levels. 
23.	 The eleven products considered in this study, 
namely fertiliser, seeds for sowing, oilseed 
cake, crude sunflower oil, maize, poultry, 
onions, citrus fruit, coffee, tea and sugar, are 
each analysed in the subsections below.

3.1.1.	 Fertilisers
24.	 At the global level, the fertiliser industry is 
characterised by high and increasing levels 
of concentration. There are a handful of 
countries which account for the bulk of the 
world’s production capacity for the major 
nitrogen, phosphate and potash fertilisers, 
namely, China, US, Russia, India, Canada 
and Indonesia.16 The leading five fertiliser 
companies worldwide, as of January 2024 
(based on market capitalisation) include 
Wesfarmers from Australia, Nutrien from 
Canada,Tthe 
Saudi 
Arabian 
Fertilizer 
company, CF Industries  and The Mosaic 
Company, both from the US.17  
25.	 Low-income regions like Sub-Saharan Africa 
are highly dependent on imported fertiliser, 
and the price of fertiliser imports is generally 
a large percentage of fertiliser supply costs in 
the region. In this section, the study focuses 
on fertiliser under the description, ‘mineral 
or chemical fertilisers, nitrogenous’ with the 
HS heading 3102, as this fertiliser product is 
generally significantly traded relative to other 
types of fertilisers,18 and is a key input (and 
high-cost item) at the origin or base of many 
other agricultural value chains. For example, 
in South Africa, maize accounts for around 
41% of total fertiliser application, while other

display a marked increase from 2020.

since 2020.

production, which is a key component of 
nitrogen fertiliser. The diminished global 
supply of fertiliser has knock-on effects 
beyond just those market players interacting

3.1.2.	 Seeds for sowing
38.	 A general seed value chain involves actors 
who are interlinked through both commercial 
and non-commercial relationships in the 
development, 
production, 
assessment, 
transport and use of seeds.33 This section 
considers 
another 
valuable 
input 
into 

ACF African Competition Forum
188
agricultural value chains, that being input 
seeds for sowing, named as ‘Seeds, fruits 
and spores, for sowing (excl. leguminous 
vegetables and sweetcorn, coffee, tea)’ with 
HS code ‘1209.34 
39.	 Seeds or planting materials of a crop or 
plant are an important component of crop 
production, as good seeds interact with the 
environment around them, and determine the 
health of the crop or plant. Even when there 
is a conducive farming environment, a poor-
quality seed can cause production to be less 
than if quality input seeds are used. At a basic 
level, selecting good quality seeds for sowing 
a crop is essential for achieving high crop 
yields and better-quality produce. Access to 
quality input seed is thus a crucial component 
of agricultural inputs into production and 
agro-processing. 
40.	 One of the high input costs farmers face 
(especially smaller farmers) is the price of 
seeds, as they become more commodified – 
with the major seed companies touting hybrid 
and genetically modified seeds as better 
options for African smallholder agriculture.24 
The Commission has previously done work 
in the seed industry, such as looking at 
concentration in grain seed, as part of its 
Concentration Tracker report, which measures 
levels and trends in concentration and 
participation in the South African economy.35 
41.	 The report noted that four major agricultural 
input companies in the world in 2017 were 
previously 25 smaller companies before 1996, 
which highlights the level of consolidation in 
the seed industry. Merger activity has been a 
key driver of concentration in the global seed

Three major international mergers occurred 
in 2017, namely the ChemChina/Syngenta 
merger, the Bayer/Monsanto merger, and 
the Dow/Du Pont merger.36 The report notes 
that concentration is of particular concern 
in South Africa, because it is usually not an 
efficient form of growing concentration, but 
rather inefficient because, “the economy 
inherited a concentrated market structure 
from the Apartheid era, with entrenched 
leaders that remain dominant today. Inefficient 
concentration is associated with higher margins 
and is seen to impose a structural constraint 
on growth.” A United Nations report on global 
agriculture also highlights the dangers of 
market concentration in seed markets, as a 
limited number of seed suppliers leads to the 
concentration of research and development 
of a limited number of seed varieties.37 The 
research points out that concentration can 
make it difficult for new companies to enter 
the market, as well as having anticompetitive 
effects by leading to massive price increases 
for seeds.

3.1.3.	 Oilseed cake or oilcake
48.	 Oilseed cake or oilcake describes the 
residues obtained after the extraction of 
oil from the plant source, such as oilseed, 
by expelling or by solvent extraction. The 
extraction of oil from seed or fruit is carried 
out by two methods – by pressing, or with 
chemical solvents.42 Oil-cake is one of the 
largest inputs for farmers in the livestock 
industry, as it serves as valuable feed for 
livestock due to it being rich in protein. These 
agricultural residues provide major valuable 
feedstock for cattle, and are further used for 
the production of value-added products like 
biogas, biofuel, biopolymer, antibiotics and 
enzymes.43 Furthermore, numerous products 
like edible films, protein concentrates, isolates 
and emulsifiers can be extracted by properly 
processing the waste and by-products from 
oilseeds.
49.	 Trade research as far back as 2010 shows 
that soya oil-cake at the time comprised 
around 25% of the total feed costs in South 
Africa, which translated to around 17,5% 
of the total costs associated with livestock 
rearing.44 Because of its economic relevance, 
and nutrients such as protein, fibre, carbs, 
and antioxidants, oilseed cakes have found 
a desirable place in livestock and poultry 
feed, and are thus an important part of 

ACF African Competition Forum
192
the agricultural industry as they affect the 
input costs for other food value chains. The 
assessment evaluated oil-cake under the 
code HS’2304, formally named ‘oil-cake and 
other solid residues, whether or not ground 
or in the form of pellets, resulting from the 
extraction of soya-bean oil.’ 
50.	 The figure below shows that in terms of 
intra-continental trade, South Africa is a net 
exporter of oil-cake, with export volumes 
multiples larger than import volumes since 
2019 (where prior, from 2017 to 2018, South 
Africa imported more oil-cake from Africa 
than it exported to Africa). This change seen 
in 2019 can potentially be attributed to 
establishing domestic crushing plants, and 
the increase in the production of soybeans, 
which resulted in increased oilcake and 
assisted in import replacements, as well as 
new soybean seed technology investments,

when considering South Africa’s global trade 
in oil-cake. South Africa has, for the bulk of 
the period (2017 – 2021), been a net importer 
of oil-cake volumes, after which imports 
decreased substantially to equalise with 
export volumes in 2022 (as also seen in the 
trade values). The main supplying markets for 
South Africa’s global oil-cake imports include 
Argentina (which accounts for 97% of South 
Africa’s total imports), and the rest, which 
account for much smaller values include 
Zambia, Zimbabwe, Eswatini and South Africa 
(where trade between South Africa and itself 
is explained by re-import activity).47

3.1.4.	 Crude sunflower oil
53.	 Crude sunflower oil is obtained from partially 
dehulled seeds, by mechanical pressing 
followed by extraction and water degumming. 
In basic terms, it is the oil pressed from the 
seeds of the sunflower. Crude sunflower oil 
is used in the production of refined edible 
sunflower oil, mixed edible oil, blended edible 
vegetable fat, fat spreads, mayonnaise and 
related products. Sunflower oil is generally 
used in food or technical applications such as 
cooking oil, for biofuels and cosmetics. 
54.	 In South Africa sunflower oil is mainly used 
for cooking purposes,49 and it is a basic 
food staple bought by many households. 
During the 2021 to 2022 period there were 
many reports surrounding the steep price 
hikes seen in cooking oil, mainly due to oil-
producing crop shortages internationally, 
and higher-than-usual demand from the main 
consumer countries like China and India, in 
addition to the supply shortage resulting from 
the Russian-Ukraine conflict.50 
55.	 The Commission also analysed cooking oil 
price trends in its September 2023 Essential 
Food Price Monitoring report.51 The report 
covered the importance of Eastern European 
countries to the global supply of sunflower 
seeds and edible oils, while also noting 
that high and growing concentration along 
the sunflower oil value chain (from sowing 
seeds to the refining level) creates the risk 
of opportunistic price increases above cost 
increases, during periods of inflation.

has been linked to the Covid-19 pandemic, 
and exacerbated by the Eastern European

and exporter of vegetable oils and seeds.53

3.1.5.	 Maize
61.	 Maize is an important staple food for millions 
of people, and is particularly key for food 
security in developing countries, where it 
represents a dietary staple and a critical 
source of calories, protein, and micronutrients 
for many people. Due to maize being a 
versatile crop that can grow in a variety of 
climates and soils, it is a suitable crop for 
small-scale farmers in developing countries, 
and therefore contributes to agricultural 
production and employment. Furthermore, 
maize is a low-cost food source that is easily 
accessible to low-income households.
62.	 Maize is a source of food for both humans and 
animals, and can also be used for industrial 
purposes such as the production of biofuels, 
starch and oil. In many countries, maize is 
the primary staple food, and it provides a 
significant portion of people’s daily calorie 
intake. In sub-Saharan Africa, for example, 
maize is reported to account for more than 
30% of the daily calorie intake, and in some 
56

calorie intake.56

3.1.6.	 Poultry
73.	 Within South Africa’s agriculture and agro-
processing sector, poultry as a protein food 
and as an industry is of special interest. The 
poultry industry accounts for around 65% of all 
animal protein consumed in South Africa, due 
to the production of both chicken meat and 
eggs. The poultry industry, including broiler 
chicken and egg production, is furthermore 
the biggest contributor to South Africa’s 
agricultural sector, with a total annual gross 
value of production of almost R72 billion in 
2022 (and R61 billion the previous year).65 
74.	 The poultry industry’s overall agro-processing 
component has also grown rapidly. With 
respect to the poultry industry, it supports 
many marginal businesses and industries 
(such as the feed industry) and others in the 
downstream levels of the value chain. The 
poultry industry also provides direct and 

ACF African Competition Forum
201
indirect employment, with over 118,000 
people estimated to be employed across the 
value chain.66 
75.	 The poultry value chain in South Africa 
includes poultry feed processing, to broiler 
and egg production and supply. There are 
various activities which form part of the 
poultry value chain, with another key part of 
industry being the poultry feed market (which 
accounts for around 70% of total poultry 
industry costs). Essentially, the poultry value 
chain is comprised of activities starting with 
feed production, day-old chick supply, broiler 
chicken production, egg production and 
then the distribution and cold storage. There 
is an increasing trend among big poultry 
producers to vertically integrate the whole 
poultry business, from the production of day-
old chicks, to the growing of chickens through 
contracts farming, slaughtering, processing, 
and supply of feed.67
76.	 Significant investments and coordination 
throughout the supply chain highlight the role 
of large corporations in the poultry industry. 
Moreover, due to the generally limited size 
of markets in many countries, the industry 
tends to be highly consolidated, and marked 
by 
comprehensive 
vertical 
integration 
arrangements. 
77.	 Major players in the poultry industry expanded 
both horizontally and vertically within the value 
chain. For instance, Astral acquired National 
Chicks (Natchix) and Earlybird Farms during 
that period.68 Rainbow similarly broadened its 
reach through strategic acquisitions, such as 
Vector Logistics, in 2004. From 2010 to 2013

78.	 As an earlier example of a specific case 
in the poultry industry, the Competition 
Commission reached a settlement agreement 
in 2012 with Astral Operations Limited, under 
which Astral had admitted to colluding with a 
competitor to fix the price of fresh poultry in 
the Western Cape. Astral had also admitted 
that the terms of its Elite joint venture was an 
abuse of dominance, and had an exclusionary 
effect undermining Country Bird’s ability 
to compete.70 Astral had agreed to pay an 
administrative penalty amounting to 3% of 
County Fair (a division of Astral) fresh poultry 
revenue in the Western Cape for the 2008 
financial year.71
79.	 The Commission has also recently initiated 
a scoping study in order to understand the 
levels of competitiveness and participation 
within the poultry industry, and whether 
further intervention by the Commission 
may be required.72 The study reported 
that the poultry industry in South Africa is 
concentrated, with a few vertically integrated 
companies accounting for the bulk of overall 
output, which creates entry and expansion 
barriers within the poultry value chain. 

ACF African Competition Forum
202
The report highlights that contract grower 
relationships could signal a lack of sufficient 
space for independent entry and expansion 
within the industry, outside of contracting with 
the major producers – who appear to have 
become gatekeepers to the industry via their 
control of genetic stock, production of parent 
stock, control of abattoirs and commercial 
relationships with retailers and restaurants.73  
80.	 In terms of poultry breeding, the global 
concentration in this industry, and the 
expansive 
influence 
of 
multinational 
corporations in many industries, implies 
that mergers and cartel agreements in one 
region, like Europe or north America, could 
affect countries all over the world, including 
in Southern Africa.74 The concentration and 
vertical integration in the industry resulting 
from mergers has shaped poultry value chains 
beyond the merging parties’ borders (as well 
influencing other industry value chains),75 and 
this points to the importance of enhanced 
cross-border competition enforcement, to 
try to minimise unwanted or unforeseen spill-
over anti-competitive effects.

context, where average prices are seen to be 
under pressure, as noted above.

have recorded an increase of around 18%

the previous quarter. Brazil, the USA and

poultry imports to South Africa. Australia is

since the fourth quarter of 2021.79 
88.	 Poultry retail prices have remained elevated 
in response to the High Pathological Avian 
Influenza and the Ukraine conflict-induced 
feed costs, which are both negatively affecting 
production expansion locally. A further 
strain on local poultry production and feed 
manufacturing is the continued electricity 
supply challenge in South Africa. In light of 
these developments, the increase in imports 
may have dampened recent local prices to a 
certain degree.80
3.1.7.	 Onions
89.	 Onions are a versatile and widely used 
vegetable in many cultures around the world, 
including Africa. They are a member of the 
allium family, which also includes garlic, leeks 
and shallots. Onions represent a staple food 
globally, and have economic importance 
as a vegetable crop for food, farming and 
household income generation, as well as 
medicinal uses. Onions are one of the most 
widely traded vegetables in Africa, and are 
particularly notable for their storage life and 
versatility, making them a central agricultural 
product in many African markets. In South 
Africa, the onion vegetable crop constitutes 
around 14% of fresh produce crops trade. For 
the country, onion is the third-largest crop, 
preceded by potatoes at 34% of traded fresh 
produce crop, and tomatoes at 16% of the 
total value of traded fresh produce.81 

ACF African Competition Forum
205
90.	 Considering the importance of a vegetable 
crop like onion, this study looks at South 
Africa’s trade dynamics with Africa and 
globally for onions over the 2017 to 2022 
period. The figure below shows that South 
Africa has recorded high export volumes and 
value for onions, while the country’s imports 
are much lower in terms of volume and value. 
Again, as with many other traded products, in 
terms of bilateral trade between South Africa 
and Africa, there is a bias towards South 
Africa’s export market for onions relative to 
Africa. This points to opportunities for the 
rest of Africa to sell their key vegetables, like 
onions, to South Africa. 
Figure 24: SA trade with rest of Africa, Onions (2017 -2022)
Source: ITC Trade Map (2023)
91.	 The study notes that, for the onion trade 
to the world, the picture does not change 
much – there is still a clear, large magnitude 
of exports to the world, and much lower 
volumes of onions imported into South Africa. 
This appears to be the case due to the overlap 
in the trade data for Africa, which is also being 
counted as part of the global trade data (as 
noted in sections before). When considering 
the actual annual export volumes to the 
world, around 93 to 99% of these volumes 
are accounted for by Africa (i.e, exported to 
Africa). 
92.	 What is also seen across these figures is 
that South Africa’s imports from Africa do 
not overlap with global imports to the same 
degree as its onion exports – there is a fair 
measure of overlap, but this is considerably 
less than the export overlap (with the measure 
of overlap in terms of African imports 
accounting for global imports being in the 
44 – 64% range). This could potentially be 
explained by a consideration of whether (and 
which) African countries are major exporters 
to the world, and when looking at the rankings 
of the top onion exporters globally, there are 
no African countries listed (aside from Egypt, 
with much less export value than compared to 
China, for example). In terms of South Africa’s 
top supplying markets for onions, there is a 
mix of African and non-African countries who 
export onions to the country – which could 
explain the higher magnitude of import 
volumes at the global level relative to the 
African trade level.

3.1.8.	 Citrus fruit
95.	 Citrus is a key fruit in South Africa’s fruit 
industry, and the citrus fruit industry is a 
mature and established industry. Citrus 
accounts for around 32% of total fruit 
production (in volume terms) in South Africa, 
and it also represents the main export fruit 
at around 45% of total fruit export earnings 
in the country. In global terms, South Africa 
is the second-largest exporter of citrus fruit, 
after Spain.84
96.	 South Africa has a citrus fruit industry which 
is export-oriented, with over 100 years of 
exporting to developed countries. This is 
observed in the figures below, which show 
the country’s export dominance in terms 
of its citrus fruit85 trade at both the African 
level and the global level. The top export 
markets for South Africa’s citrus fruit include 
the Netherlands, the UK, Russian Federation, 
United Arab Emirates, China and the US.86 The 
figures below show that South Africa imports 
only a negligible amount of citrus fruit, 
with these markets including countries like 
Morocco, Spain, Eswatini, Turkey and Israel.

Africa show generally lower average prices 
(on left side). The highest average price for 
citrus exports globally reached around $797/t 
in 2021, while the highest average price for 
citrus imports reached only $545/t in 2020.

98.	 Even though the citrus industry in South Africa 
is export-focused, there is still considerable 
citrus processing activity within the industry. 
A sizeable portion of citrus fruit production is 
used in the manufacturing of fruit juices, citrus 
fruit jams, jellies, marmalades, purees, pastes 
and preserved citrus fruit. The citrus fruit 
value chain provides opportunities for job 
creation and employment, as many small and 
medium-sized citrus growers are able to sell 
their produce to fruit processing companies 
– or themselves venture into fruit-processing 
operations. 
3.1.9.	 Coffee
99.	 In terms of value, coffee is one of the world’s 
most important commodities. It is a vital 
crop for developing countries, as it provides 
foreign exchange earnings and an income 
for farmers. Importantly, the coffee crop is an 
ideal crop for areas with poor infrastructure, 
as it is one of few tropical horticultural crop 
products that can be stored for relatively long 
periods without perishing.87 The three main 
growing regions for coffee across the world 
include Africa/Arabia, Asia, and Central/South 
America.
100.	 This section looks at bilateral trade statistics 
(exports and imports) between South Africa 
and the African continent, as well as the 
global level of trade for coffee, (HS’0901) 
formally named, ‘coffee, whether or not 
roasted or decaffeinated; coffee husks and 
skins; coffee substitutes containing coffee in 
any proportion’.
101.	 The figure below shows South Africa’s annual 
coffee trade with the rest of Africa from 2017 
to 2022. Many African countries actually 
import substantial amounts of coffee from 
Europe, including the coffee that was grown 
in Africa. The country’s import and export 
volumes appear fairly matched, until 2021 
when more coffee imports from Africa are 
seen than the volume of exports to Africa. The 
export value shows a decrease from 2018 
until 2020, after which an increase in value 
is observed, while import value remained 
fairly constant but climbed significantly from 
2021, despite import value only showing a 
moderate increase during this period.

3.1.10.	 Tea
107.	 This section of the study looks at the trade 
flows of tea (HS’0902 tea, whether or not 
flavoured) between South Africa and the 
continent, as well as at the global level. The 
tea industry is one of the oldest agricultural 
industries, with massive direct employment 
numbers, particularly in remote rural areas. 
Tea is a significant commodity for various 
developing countries, as it contributes to 
job creation and export earnings.92 
108.	 Production and exports of tea generates 
foreign 
exchange, 
employment 
opportunities, and provides a material 
base for national economic growth. The 
tea industry also contributes significantly 
to food security by helping to cover food 
import bills, which makes tea trade critical 
for policies around food security, trade 
 
and rural development in developing 
countries.93
109.	 The main export markets (supplying markets) 
to South Africa, as seen in the figure below, 
for tea include (based on exported value 
in 2022) Malawi, Zimbabwe, Sri Lanka, 
Tanzania, China and Kenya. As a supplying 
market, Malawi has consistently been the 
largest exporter of tea to South Africa over 
the 2017 - 2022 period. Globally, the top tea 
exporters include China, Kenya, Sri Lanka, 
India and the United Arab Emirates.94 
110.	 It is worth noting that Kenya is a large global 
producer and exporter of tea, but within 
the African continent it only ranks as the 
6th largest supplying market to African 
countries. This could imply that certain 
product grades or quality classifications

3.1.11.	 Sugar
114.	 The sugar industry has played an important 
role historically in developing economies 
around the world, encouraging job creation 
and investment in non-sugar sectors. 
The importance of sugar production to 
developing countries comes from the 
fact that sugar cane is a crop able to 
withstand weather extremes that exist in 
tropical regions. The industry creates large-
scale employment in rural areas, and the 
expansion of the sugar cane industry in 
developing countries goes hand in hand 
with establishing small grower schemes and 
projects. Furthermore, sugar production is an 
agro-industrial process, which supports the 
development of various engineering skills 
that transfer to other parts of the economy, 
 
and sugar industries provide much needed 
export earnings and tax revenues. 
115.	 The South African sugar industry supports 
around one million livelihoods through 
direct and indirect employment. This sector 
has, however, been experiencing challenges 
due to factors such as global prices below 
South Africa’s cost of production; increasing 
volumes of low-priced, tariff-free exports 
from Eswatini into the Southern African 
Customs Union; and the Health Promotion 
Levy (a tax on sugar-containing drinks). 
These factors have resulted in many South 
African sugarcane farmers and millers 
incurring 
unsustainable 
losses, 
with 
potentially devastating consequences for 
rural unemployment and poverty.

ACF African Competition Forum
216
116.	 To aid the challenging industry situation the 
government, after extensive consultation 
with 
industry 
stakeholders, 
published 
the South African Sugarcane Value Chain 
Master Plan to 2030 (Sugar Masterplan) 
in 
November 
2020. 
The 
government 
has developed the Sugar Masterplan to 
address challenges facing the local sugar 
industry, which has experienced declining 
production, job losses, and negative impacts 
on rural areas. The plan aims to decrease 
the 
industry’s 
decline, 
preserve 
jobs, 
restructure and transform the industry, and 
invest in sustainable value chains.99 The plan 
focuses on improving industrial capacities, 
increasing sugar exports and reclaiming 
the domestic market lost to imports. The 
Sugar Masterplan, with long-term goals for 
2030, includes seven key action plans which 
focus on restoring the local market – price 
restraint, trade protection, employment 
preservation, 
support 
for 
small-scale 
growers, 
transformation 
and 
industry 
restructuring.100  
117.	 In terms of more recent enforcement in 
the industry, the Competition Commission 
granted the South African Sugar Association 
(SASA) and its members a conditional 
exemption until 30 June 2023 (unless 
extended further). The exemption has been 
granted subject to conditions to ensure 
that the information SASA and its members 
share is limited to what is necessary to give 
effect to the purposes of the Sugar Master 
Plan, without compromising competition 
in the long run.101 The exemption covers

105	 ‘Dumping’ in trade occurs when a country or company exports a product at a price that is lower in the foreign importing 
market than the price in the exporter’s domestic market. Dumping leads to international price discrimination where the 
price of a product sold in the importing country is less than the price in the market of the exporting country.
120.	 When considering South Africa’s global 
sugar trade, South Africa is a net exporter of 
sugar from 2018, with large increases seen 
in its exported volumes in 2019 particularly, 
along with rising export values for sugar 
at the time.104  In addition to the sugar 
market being a ‘dumped’ market,105 it is also 
reported that Eswatini had enjoyed market 

ACF African Competition Forum
218
access in the EU for a while, but since the 
preferential EU quotas were abolished in 
September 2017, Eswatini has been pushing 
its sugar exports into South Africa.106 Trade 
data does indeed show that South Africa 
imports most of its sugar in Africa from 
Eswatini.107 
121.	 In terms of global sugar trade, the top 
exporting nations, by 2022 exported value, 
include Brazil, India, Thailand, France 
and Germany. The main sugar exporting 
countries in Africa are Morocco, Eswatini, 
South Africa, Mauritius and Egypt. 
122.	 The average prices of South Africa’s sugar 
trade globally are seen in the chart below, 
Figure 39, (as, due to the overlap, these 
prices show the same general trends as 
with the African level of trade), where it is 
clear that average prices for imports ($/t) 
are higher than average prices for exports, 
as well as higher than the World Bank’s 
commodity prices for sugar (the grey, 
dotted line),108  and there is an increase seen 
in all price series from 2020. Sugar prices 
have remained fairly low relative to other 
commodity prices – tea for example, as

noted in the section above, has an average

123.	 This is generally attributed to the sugar 
industry being heavily subsidised, as well 
as increased supply and dumping practices 
internationally (which reflects in the low 
World Bank commodity prices for sugar).109 
SA Cane Growers Association reported 
dumping as a key cause of the local sugar 
industry’s lower profitability in recent years, 
as the cheap, low quality imports caused 
losses for the industry, while the country 
has to export its surplus sugar into an over-
supplied world market at large losses.110 
Overall, the sugar industry in South Africa 
has faced significant challenges and decline 
due to a combination of factors, namely: 
distorted global prices for sugar (below 
South Africa’s cost of production), the 
increasing volumes of low-priced, tariff-
free exports from eSwatini, and the Health 
Promotion Levy (a tax on sugar-containing 
drinks implemented in April 2018) which has 
reduced sugar demand in South Africa.111

3.2.	 SOUTH AFRICAN TRADE FLOWS AT THE 
REGIONAL LEVEL 
124.	 Trade is fundamental to the economic 
development of the Southern African 
Development Community (SADC) Region, 
and has broader benefits that support the 
process of regional integration. Countries 
that increase and expand trade with others 
through 
trade 
liberalisation 
increase 
economic growth, and improve the quality 
of life of their people.112 
125.	 The main objectives of SADC are to, 
“achieve development, peace and security, 
and economic growth, to alleviate poverty, 
enhance the standard and quality of life 
of the peoples of Southern Africa, and 
support the socially disadvantaged through 
regional integration, built on democratic 
principles and equitable and sustainable 
development.”113 Furthermore, the mission 
of the SADC regional economic community 
is, “to promote sustainable and equitable 
economic 
growth 
and 
socio-economic 
development through efficient, productive 
systems, 
deeper 
co-operation 
and 
integration, good governance, and durable 
peace and security; so that the region 
emerges as a competitive and effective 
player in international relations and the 
world economy”.114
126.	 The trade flow data, as measured by ITC 
Trade Map, shows significant overlaps 
in traded export and import volumes, as 
one shifts from analysing global trade to 
continental trade, and now in this section 
analysing the trade data at a regional level 
– which is at the SADC level. There is a large 
degree of overlap between trade data from 
Africa and from the SADC regional level, 
which means that SADC trade accounts for a 
large portion of recorded volumes between 
South Africa and Africa.

ACF African Competition Forum
220
127.	 This section considers the same key 11 
agricultural products as analysed before 
(at the continent and global level), but here 
the focus is on the bilateral trade between 
South Africa and the SADC region, which 
captures the aggregate level of trade 
between South Africa and the SADC country 
group combined, to further provide insights 
into food trade flows in the region. There

3.2.1.	 Fertiliser
128.	 The figure below clearly illustrates the 
data overlap between South Africa’s trade 
volumes (imports and exports) with Africa 
and its trade volumes with the SADC region 
as a whole. The grey bars indicate South 
Africa’s export volumes to SADC and to 
Africa, and the figure shows that these 
amounts are extremely close, which is seen 
each year from 2017 to 2022. The blue bars 
also show that there is a large degree of 
overlap between what South Africa imports 
from Africa and from SADC – this is, however, 
to a lesser extent in 2019 and 2020, where 
only 61% of the country’s fertiliser imports 
were from SADC, and the rest from other 
countries in Africa during that period, 
including Nigeria and Egypt.116 
129.	 Overall, the graph clearly shows that South 
Africa is a net exporter of nitrogenous 
fertiliser within Africa and the SADC region 
(which is different to its global trade position 
as noted in the section above), and that a 
significant portion of its African exports are 
supplying SADC member countries. This 
corresponds to public reports on South 
Africa’s fertiliser trade, where it is noted 
that the SADC countries have been notable 
importers of nitrogenous fertilisers from 
South Africa.117

ACF African Competition Forum
222
131.	 Research related to fertiliser supply in 
Africa has also shown that markets in East 
and Southern Africa are oligopolistic in 
nature, with just a few large firms having the 
capability to source fertiliser internationally in 
bulk, and having the necessary bagging and 
warehousing facilities at the main ports.119 
In relation to competition enforcement 
in the fertiliser industry, the South African 
fertiliser cartel was characterised by a range 
of complex information sharing schemes, 
and fertiliser firms had been found to be 
involved in allocating markets and price 
fixing until around 2006.120 The cartel 
affected prices for fertiliser not only in 
South Africa, but also in several Southern 
African countries too, which highlights the 
potential overspill into other countries due 
to regional, cross-border and transnational 
dynamics in fertiliser trade.121 
132.	 Additionally, the Competition Commission 
addressed a case of price fixing against 
the 
Fertiliser Association 
of 
Southern 
Africa (FERTASA) and five agricultural lime 
manufacturers, which was referred to the 
Competition Tribunal in 2014, following 
an investigation that revealed that the

141.	 In terms of anti-competitive conduct in the 
broader grain industry, the Commission 
found that, from 1999 to 2007, a number 
of key firms had engaged in cooperative 
behaviour which resulted in the uncovering 
of three main cartels – the bread cartel, flour 
cartel and the maize meal cartel. The four 
firms involved in all three cartels (Premier 
Foods, Tiger Brands, Pioneer Foods and 
Foodcorp) represented major firms in the 
milling industry, and accounted for more 
than 90% of all milled wheat sales.129 As 
seen from the regional trade data above, 
most SADC countries import key grains, 
such as maize, from South Africa, and so 
anti-competitive conduct in one market can 
spill over into other country trade partners 
– which points to the need and potential 
benefit of more cross-border collaboration 
in terms of competition enforcement, 
especially in key markets such as maize.

3.2.8.	 Citrus fruit
148.	 As noted in the previous section on citrus 
fruit at the Africa continent and global 
level, the South African citrus industry is 
export-orientated, with the Citrus Growers 
Association of Southern Africa reporting 
that the country exported 77% of the citrus 
it produced, with around 18% allocated 
for processing into juice and other by-
products, and around 5% for local fresh 
consumption.133
149.	 With regard to South Africa’s trade in the 
SADC region for citrus fruits, the top SADC 
countries importing SA’s citrus fruit (based 
on 2022 value) include Mauritius, Zambia, 
Namibia, Botswana, Mozambique, and then 
Angola and Eswatini. The EU has been South 
Africa’s main trading partner in citrus since 
2000 under the Trade, Development and 
Cooperation Agreement. In October 2016, 
a new SADC-EU Economic Partnership 
Agreement (EPA) became effective, with the 
aim of providing for improved market access 
for oranges and lemons as a key feature of 
the agreement. The EPA strengthens the 
parties’ commitment towards sustainable 
development in agriculture, and includes 
improved tariff rate quotas for frozen orange 
juice, and no export subsidies under the 
EPA to level the playing field.134 
150.	 The figure below shows a similar trend 
to the trade flows seen at the continental

231

noted in the section 3.1.8 above. There is 
also less overlap between Africa and the

other food products analysed in the study. 
It still, however, remains that a large portion

3.3.	 SOUTH AFRICAN TRADE FLOWS AT THE 
INDIVIDUAL COUNTRY LEVEL 
163.	 In this section, South Africa’s agricultural 
trade flows are analysed at the individual 
country level, mainly considering individual 
SADC countries as well as Kenya, in respect 
of key products such as tea, coffee, maize 
and sugar. This section therefore provides 
a more in-depth look at some key traded 
products between South Africa and certain 
countries, as well as top trading partners 
per product, which could potentially explain 
some of the trade flow patterns seen at the 
regional, continental and even global levels, 
in addition to pointing to certain trade flow 
gaps that may exist.  
164.	 The traded products considered in this 
section cover a smaller set of four key 
agricultural products which are widely 
traded in the SADC region amongst its 
member states, and are important staple 
agricultural crops for most developing 
nations. These key products include maize, 
sugar, coffee and tea. This is considered on 
an individual country basis below, along 
with the relevant global and African trade 
context.

3.4.	 TRADE POLICY AND REGIONAL 
ECONOMIC ACTIVITY IN THE 
AGRICULTURE AND AGRO-PROCESSING 
SECTOR 
178.	 This section provides a brief overview of 
trade and regulatory features in South Africa, 
which includes a consideration of factors 
facilitating regional trade, as well as those 
aspects which hinder increased agricultural 
trade in Africa. The section closes with some 
feasible opportunities and insights that 
could benefit increased regional trade in 
Africa, considering the current challenges 
facing agricultural trade.
179.	 South Africa’s agricultural trade policy 
has been evolving, with a strong focus on 
increasing regional trade and boosting the 
agricultural and agro-processing sector. 
Several key policies and agreements have 
been designed to enhance the country’s 
agricultural trade capabilities and regional 
integration, which are covered in brief 
below. 
180.	 In terms of regional and international trade 
agreements, South Africa is a member of 
SADC and SACU, which have been pivotal in 
creating a free trade area, offering a common 
external tariff and facilitating smoother trade 
across member countries. Additionally, the 

ACF African Competition Forum
244
AfCFTA aims to create a single market for 
goods and services across Africa, enhancing 
trade and economic integration.142 
181.	 South Africa’s trade policy underlines an 
export-led growth strategy, aiming to 
extend its agricultural exports beyond 
traditional markets like the EU.143 This 
approach has led to the growth of exports 
driven by productivity gains, although 
challenges remain, such as high tariff and 
non-tariff barriers, as well as delays in export 
permits,144
182.	 Various 
support 
and 
development 
programmes 
have 
been 
implemented 
to 
assist 
previously 
disadvantaged 
and smallholder farmers, such as the 
Comprehensive 
Agricultural 
Support 
Programme (CASP) and the Agri-Industrial 
Fund.145 These initiatives focus on providing 
inputs, capacity building, and facilitating 
access to markets.146 Additionally, the 
government has been involved in land 
redistribution efforts, to enhance agricultural 
development 
and 
rectify 
historical 
inequalities.147
183.	 Trade policy in South Africa also focuses 
on infrastructure and service support. 
Investment in rural infrastructure and the 
development of food security strategies are 
key components of South Africa’s agricultural

policy.148 This includes upgrading irrigation 
and water systems, improving roads and 
railways, and providing technical and 
advisory services to enhance agricultural 
productivity and sustainability​.149 
184.	 Furthermore, South Africa’s trade policy 
includes considerations towards climate 
change mitigation and adaptation, as 
policies have been adapted to address 
climate change impacts. This includes a 
commitment to reduce greenhouse gas 
emissions, as well as the implementation of 
a carbon tax. These measures are designed 
to align with global climate goals, and to 
improve the sustainability of agricultural 
practices.150 
185.	 There are a number of factors which facilitate 
and develop South Africa’s agriculture 
and agro-processing trade in the SADC 
region and across Africa. These factors 
include: regional trade agreements; trade 
facilitation measures (such as simplified 
customs procedures); export promotion 
programmes to penetrate new markets in 
Africa; infrastructure investment; capacity 
building and technical assistance to enhance 
skills and knowledge; the promotion of 
value addition and agro-processing; as well 
as support to smallholder farmers.151 

ACF African Competition Forum
245
186.	 While the factors above currently do support 
and facilitate South Africa’s increased 
regional and African trade, there are various 
key challenges and limiting aspects which 
hinder trade development and growth in 
agriculture. These challenges are outlined 
briefly below. 
186.1.	  Trade barriers: Tariffs and non-tariff 
barriers imposed by importing countries 
can restrict South Africa’s agricultural 
exports. High tariffs on certain products 
and stringent import regulations, such 
as sanitary and phytosanitary standards, 
can increase the cost and complexity 
of exporting agricultural goods.152 For 
example, South African citrus fruits, 
especially oranges and lemons, face 
high tariffs when exported to the EU, 
which is further compounded by strict 
phytosanitary 
regulations.153 
Nigeria 
imposes high tariffs on several processed 
food products, including those from South 
Africa. Products such as processed meats 
and food preparations are particularly 
affected, making it challenging for South 
African exporters to compete in this 
market.154 Despite being a major market for 
agricultural products, China imposes high 
tariffs on certain fruits and nuts exported 
from South Africa, including macadamia 
nuts and avocados.155 Brazil’s high tariffs 
on sugar and dairy products affect South
