African exports of these goods. The tariffs, 
along with other trade barriers, reduce the 
competitiveness of South African sugar 
and dairy in the Brazilian market.156
186.2.	  Competition 
concerns: 
South 
Africa 
faces competition from other countries 
in the SADC region and beyond, which 
produce similar agricultural products. 
Lower production costs, subsidies, and 
preferential trade agreements enjoyed 
by competitor countries could challenge 
the competitiveness of South African 
agricultural 
exports.157 
More 
recent 
research highlights the absence of robust 
regional competition in East and Southern 
Africa, and emphasises the need to 
investigate transportation, storage and 
logistical challenges to food trade. The 
research points to price disparities along 
transport routes, which result in profits for 
transporters, and arbitrage opportunities 
for major traders. It also indicates that 
intermediaries purchase supplies at low 
prices during harvests, and withhold 
them to artificially inflate prices. Given the 
vulnerable nature of food systems and the 
growing consolidation within critical value 
chains, there is a clear call for a regional 
competition policy to foster resilient 
and sustainable regional value chains, 
while ensuring a level playing field for all 
stakeholders.158

ACF African Competition Forum
246
186.3.	  Infrastructure 
constraints: 
Inadequate 
transportation networks, storage facilities, 
and 
logistical 
infrastructure 
hinder 
the efficient movement of agricultural 
products within South Africa and across 
borders.159 Poor infrastructure increases 
transportation costs, delays shipments, 
and reduces the quality and shelf life of 
perishable goods.
186.4.	  Market access challenges: Limited market 
access and trade barriers within the 
SADC region and Africa constrain South 
Africa’s ability to expand its agricultural 
exports.160 Non-tariff barriers, such as 
restrictive import licensing regimes and 
cumbersome customs procedures, create 
barriers to entry into certain markets.
186.5.	  Climate 
change 
and 
environmental 
pressures: 
Climate 
change-related 
challenges, such as droughts, floods, and 
extreme weather events, pose significant 
risks to agricultural production in South 
Africa.161 Erratic weather patterns and 
environmental degradation can disrupt 
supply chains, reduce yields, and increase 
production costs for farmers and agro-
processors.
186.6.	  Land 
reform 
and 
property 
rights: 
Uncertainty surrounding land reform 
policies and property rights in South 
Africa can deter investment in agriculture 
and agro-processing. Concerns about 
expropriation without compensation and 
land tenure insecurity discourage long-
term investment in the sector.162

186.7.	  Skills 
shortages 
and 
Labour 
issues: 
Shortages of skilled labour, and a lack 
of technical expertise in agriculture 
and agro-processing, limit productivity 
and innovation in the sector. Difficulty 
in attracting and retaining qualified 
workers, particularly in rural areas, thus 
hampering the adoption of modern 
farming techniques and agro-processing 
technologies.163
186.8.	  Volatility in input costs and currency 
fluctuations: Fluctuations in input costs, 
such as fuel, fertiliser, and electricity, affect 
the profitability of agricultural production 
and 
agro-processing 
activities.164 
Moreover, 
currency 
fluctuations 
can 
impact the competitiveness of South 
African agricultural exports in international 
markets, making pricing unpredictable for 
both producers and buyers.
187.	 Addressing these challenges would require a 
coordinated approach involving government 
intervention, private sector investment, and 
stakeholder collaboration to enhance the 
resilience and competitiveness of South 
Africa’s agriculture and agro-processing 
trade. Following from (and linked to) the 
above-mentioned challenges, and trade 
facilitating factors, there are numerous 
opportunities for improving and developing 
South 
Africa’s 
agricultural 
and 
agro-
processing trade, discussed briefly below. 
187.1.	  Investment in infrastructure: Fluctuations 
in input costs, such as fuel, fertiliszer, 
and electricity, affect the profitability 

ACF African Competition Forum
247
of agricultural production and agro-
processing 
activities. 
Moreover, 
currency fluctuations can impact the 
competitiveness 
of 
South 
African 
agricultural 
exports 
in 
international 
markets, making pricing unpredictable for 
both producers and buyers.
187.2.	  Market 
diversification: 
South 
Africa 
should explore new markets within the 
SADC region and Africa. Strengthening 
trade 
agreements 
and 
partnerships 
with neighbouring countries can lead to 
increased export opportunities for South 
African agricultural products. Additionally, 
tapping into emerging markets with high 
demand for specific products, such as 
fruits, vegetables, and processed foods, 
can drive export growth.
187.3.	  Value-addition 
and 
agro-processing: 
Encouraging agro-processing industries 
can 
add 
value 
to 
raw 
agricultural 
products, creating more lucrative export 
opportunities. Government incentives, 
such as tax breaks or subsidies for agro-
processing companies, can stimulate 
investment in this sector. Furthermore, 
supporting research and development 
initiatives 
to 
improve 
processing 
technologies and techniques can enhance 
the competitiveness of South African 
agro-processing products in regional and 
international markets.
187.4.	  Technology adoption: Promoting the 
adoption of technology and innovation 
in agriculture can increase productivity 
and efficiency. This includes the use of 
precision farming techniques, irrigation 
systems, and mechanisation. Government 
support for research and development 
in agricultural technology, as well as 
incentives for farmers to adopt these 
technologies, can drive modernisation in 
the sector.
187.5.	  Capacity building and skills development: 
Investing in agricultural education and 
training programs can build the skills and 
capacity of farmers and agribusinesses. 
This can involve initiatives such as 
vocational training, extension services, 
and 
knowledge-sharing 
platforms. 
Building a skilled workforce will improve 
productivity and competitiveness in the 
agricultural sector.
187.6.	  Sustainable 
practices: 
Emphasising 
sustainable 
agricultural 
practices 
is 
crucial for long-term environmental and 
economic viability. Policies that promote 
sustainable land management, water 
conservation, and biodiversity protection 
can 
enhance 
resilience 
to 
climate 
change, and ensure the sustainability 
of agricultural production. Additionally, 
certification 
schemes 
for 
sustainable 
agricultural products can improve market 
access and consumer confidence.
187.7.	  Trade 
facilitation: 
Simplifying 
trade 
procedures and reducing barriers to 
cross-border trade can facilitate the flow of 
agricultural goods within the SADC region 
and 
Africa. 
Harmonising 
regulations, 
streamlining customs procedures, and 
implementing digital trade platforms 
can 
reduce 
transaction 
costs 
and 
improve market access for South African 
agricultural products. To bolster weak 
food security in Africa, exacerbated by 
climate change, it is key to ensure access 
to produce from various parts of the 
region. This approach not only presents 
the most economical means of meeting 
customer demands, but also serves as a 
way to incentivise producers to increase 
their supply.
187.8.	  Financial support and access to credit: 
Providing access to affordable financing 
and credit facilities is essential for 
smallholder farmers and agribusinesses 
to invest in their operations and expand 

ACF African Competition Forum
248
production. 
Government-backed 
loan 
programs, microfinance initiatives, and 
agricultural insurance schemes can help 
mitigate risks and support growth in the 
sector.
188.	 By implementing policy options and trade 
opportunities along these lines, South 
Africa can enhance its agriculture and agro-
processing trade within the SADC region 
and with the rest of Africa, contributing to 
economic growth, food security, and rural 
development.

on more general aspects affecting South 
Africa and regional agricultural trade, the 
next section focuses on the key insights 
and findings emanating from the trade 
statistics and trade flows of the selected 
significantly traded products across different 
geographical levels, namely agricultural 
trade globally and in Africa, regionally, as 
well as at the individual country level.

190.	 The study maps out the key trade flow 
patterns of eleven highly traded and 
significant agricultural products over the 
2017 to 2022 period, which cover important 
food 
categories 
including 
agricultural 
inputs, vegetable oils, grains, protein, fruits 
and vegetables, and other well-traded 
commodities like coffee, tea and sugar. 
191.	 South 
Africa’s 
agricultural 
and 
agro-
processing 
trade 
flows 
at 
different 
geographic trade levels are covered in 
the study. The recent trends of high food 
inflation, and global issues relating to 
food trade, underscore the importance of 
understanding global, regional and country-
level trade flows.
192.	 This section presents the main findings 
and observations from the trade flow data, 
detailed below according to the different 
geographic levels of trade per agricultural 
product, after which the section concludes 
with the overall findings from the study. 
4.1.	 FINDINGS AT THE AFRICAN AND GLOBAL 
LEVEL OF TRADE
193.	  Fertilisers. Agricultural inputs, like fertiliser, 
are of strategic importance to agricultural 
production and to many other agricultural 
value chains. The analysis of trade flows 
between South Africa and the rest of Africa 
as a whole, show that South Africa exports 
much more fertiliser (in volume and value 
terms) than it imports from the rest of the 
continent. In terms of South Africa’s global 
trade, however, the country imports more 
fertiliser (HS’3102)165 than it exports, and is a 
thus a net importer of fertiliser at the global 
level. This points to South Africa’s role as 
a gateway point for globally imported 
fertiliser to reach other African countries, 
in that South Africa could be fulfilling a re-
exporter role – where it imports a large 
volume of fertiliser globally, to then export 
to other African countries.
194.	 In terms of average prices for fertiliser, the 
trade data shows that around the time of 
Covid-19 in 2020, there was an increasing 
trend in average prices. The trade data 
shows a slightly widening gap or difference 
in absolute terms between African export 
prices and global import prices, with the 
average export prices to the rest of Africa 
increasing by slightly more than global 
import prices. This could potentially point 
to higher costs (particularly transport 
costs) of conducting trade between African 
countries, or signify that South Africa, as a 
large exporter of the relevant products to the 
continent, engages in further beneficiation 
of value-add activities before exporting 
such products to the continent. 
195.	 The prices of chemical fertilisers started 
to rise with surges in natural gas prices in 
2021, causing the reduction of ammonia 
production (a key component of nitrogen 
fertiliser). Rising fertiliser prices can impact 
agriculture and food production globally, 
as farmers struggle to afford important 
components necessary for production, 
which then adds to knock-on supply chain 
4
INSIGHTS AND OBSERVATIONS  
FROM TRADE FLOW DATA

ACF African Competition Forum
250
disruptions.166 Furthermore, there are large 
gaps between fertiliser prices in other 
African regions and international prices, 
which could signal excessive margins and 
other issues within the fertiliser supply chain. 
The World Bank commodity prices for Urea 
also showed increases from 2020, moving 
closer to South Africa’s export prices for 
fertiliser, which seems to follow trends in 
fertiliser prices showing more volatility and 
increases since 2020. 
196.	  Seeds for sowing. In terms of input seeds for 
sowing, South Africa’s trade with the rest of 
Africa shows that it is a net exporter of input 
seeds. Similarly, its trade globally shows 
a larger volume of exported seeds than 
imports. When inspecting the trade data 
further, at a more disaggregated 6-digit 
level, it appears that the majority of the input 
seeds exports relate to the alfalfa (lucerne) 
seed, which was mainly exported to China. 
Average price trends, on a global level, for 
input seeds show a general increasing trend 
from around the Covid-19 pandemic in 
2020, while average prices for trade with the 
rest of the continent show more volatility, 
particularly in average import prices. 
197.	  Oilseed cake. In terms of its intra-continental 
trade, South Africa is a net exporter of 
oilseed cake, as its export volumes are 
multiples larger than its import volumes 
since 2019. For its global trade in oilseed 
cake, South Africa has generally been a net 
importer. This also points to South Africa’s 
role as a gateway to African trade on oilseed 
cake, in similar fashion as fertiliser. The 
average prices for South Africa’s oilseed 
cake trade in Africa, and its trade globally, 
show that prices trend upwards from 2020 
to 2021, and again more volatility is seen at 
African level. 
198.	  Crude Sunflower oil. The trade data shows 
how, at the African level of trade, South 
166	 Larsen, C. 2022. The Fertiliser Supply Chain Situation (MAERSK). https://www.maersk.com/insights/
resilience/2022/07/06/the-fertiliser-supply-chain-situation 
Africa plays the net exporter role of crude 
sunflower seed oil, while at the global level, 
South Africa is a net importer. As observed 
with other products, this could point to a 
gateway or re-export role for South Africa, 
as it imports globally and then exports crude 
sunflower oil to other African countries. At 
the global level of crude sunflower oil trade, 
increased price inflation and volatility is 
seen, which is likely linked to the Covid-19 
pandemic, and further intensified by the 
Russian Ukraine conflict, as the region is a 
major producer and exporter of vegetable 
oils and seeds. 
199.	  Maize. Over the period analysed, from 2017 
to 2022, it is clear that South Africa is a strong 
net exporter of maize to the rest of Africa and 
globally. When considering the potential 
data overlap between South Africa’s exports 
globally, and what is exported to the rest of 
Africa, it is evident that Africa as a region 
accounts for the majority of all South Africa’s 
exported maize. The data also points to the 
trend of non-African countries from the rest 
of the world accounting for a larger share 
of South Africa’s maize exports. The upward 
trend observed in average maize prices, 
at the African and global level, as well as 
from the World Bank’s commodity prices, 
arose from high maize demand along with 
increasing energy costs, fertiliser and labour 
costs, in combination with low global maize 
inventories, which intensified the price rise 
from 2021 to 2022. 
200.	  Poultry. 
South 
Africa’s 
intra-continental 
poultry trade shows that South Africa is a 
key exporter of poultry to the continent, 
while importing very little to negligible 
amounts of poultry from Africa. This changes 
considerably 
when 
considering 
South 
Africa’s global trade of poultry, with South 
Africa being a net importer of poultry. South 
Africa’s poultry trade (in relation to average 
import and export prices) has lagged below 

ACF African Competition Forum
251
international price benchmarks, such as 
the World Bank’s commodity price series 
for chicken – likely due to challenges such 
as dumping practices. In terms of South 
Africa’s retail prices for poultry, this has 
remained elevated in response to the High 
Pathological Avian Influenza and the Ukraine 
conflict-induced feed costs, which are both 
negatively affecting production expansion 
locally. 
201.	  Onions. In terms of South Africa’s onion 
trade with the rest of Africa, it has recorded 
high export volumes and value for onions, 
while the country’s imports are much lower 
in terms of volume and value.  In relation 
to its global onion trade – there is still a 
clear, large magnitude of exports to the 
world, and much lower volumes of onions 
imported into South Africa. Both South 
Africa’s average price in trading with the 
rest of Africa, and with the world show 
that its average import prices are higher 
than average export prices for onions, with 
global average prices showing less volatility, 
and at a higher price range than for trade at 
the Africa level. 
202.	  Citrus fruit. South Africa’s citrus fruit 
industry is export-oriented, as observed 
in the trade flow data, which illustrates the 
country’s export dominance at both the 
African and global level. In terms of average 
trade prices, the global export prices are 
relatively high, while exports to the rest of 
Africa show generally lower average prices. 
The citrus value chain offers opportunities 
for employment, as there are also citrus 
processing activities within the industry.
203.	  Coffee. In terms of its global coffee trade, 
South Africa imports significantly more 
coffee volumes than it exports globally. 
The country’s import and export volumes 
appear fairly matched in terms of its Africa 
trade, until 2021 where more coffee imports 
from the rest of Africa are observed than the 
volume of exports to other African countries. 
With regard to average coffee prices, we 
see that average export prices tend to be a 
distance above the average import prices, 
and a drop in export prices is recorded 
in 2020, with a rebound in price for the 
following year. Arabica coffee prices, from 
the World Bank’s commodity price data, 
tracked below South Africa’s average import 
and export prices until 2020, after which 
they rose moderately (trending higher than 
import prices), reflecting shifting market 
dynamics since 2020.
204.	  Tea. The trade data on tea shows that South 
Africa does not export much tea, and is a 
net tea importer within Africa mainly, as we 
see a large degree of overlap between its 
African and global import volumes. Similarly, 
there is also overlap between South Africa’s 
exports to the rest of Africa and globally, 
and so most of the country tea exports are 
for African markets.
205.	 The average import prices for tea (along 
with the World Bank’s commodity price for 
tea) are observed at a much lower price 
level than average export prices for tea, 
with export prices showing a dip lower in 
2020, likely due to trade and supply effects 
from the Covid-19 pandemic, as export and 
import restrictions during this time led to 
uncertainty in tea markets.
206.	  Sugar. In terms of its intra-continental trade, 
South Africa is generally an importer of sugar, 
which is seen in the trade data from 2020 
to 2022. Interestingly, South Africa’s global 
trade in sugar shows it is a net exporter from 
2018, with significant increases observed 
in its export volumes (particularly in 2019), 
along with increasing export values for 
sugar at the time. Underlying this trend 
could be that there exists demand for sugar 
from Africa, which is then exported globally. 
The sugar industry faces challenges from 
higher import prices, driven by subsidies 
and 
dumping 
practices 
internationally, 
which is also reflected in the World Bank’s 
low commodity prices for sugar over the 
2017 to 2022 period. 

ACF African Competition Forum
252
207.	 Broadly, the main observations emanating 
from the trade data include the following:
207.1.	 South Africa is likely fulfilling a gateway 
and ‘re-export’ role for certain key 
products to reach other African countries, 
such as fertiliser and crude sunflower oil. 
There is a trade imbalance towards South 
Africa, which points to opportunities for 
increased trade and more balance in trade 
in Africa. This highlights the prospect for 
the rest of Africa to work towards selling 
product to the South African market. By 
way of context, the Secretary General 
of the AfCFTA noted that the observed 
trade imbalance across the continent 
is largely due to the differences in 
industrial development capacity on the 
continent. He noted that, “[One] challenge 
is the differentials in African industrial 
development capacity. Some [countries] 
can take immediate advantage of the 
industrial capacity. But the focus must be 
on those that don’t have the industrial 
development to ensure that they export 
and import goods.”167
207.2.	 For strategic agricultural inputs, like 
fertiliser, seeds for sowing and oilseed 
cake, SA is a net exporter at the African 
level of trade. Inputs, such as fertiliser, 
input seed and oilseed cake have seen 
increases in average prices over the 2017 
to 2022 period –  mainly caused by global 
factors such as the Covid 19 pandemic 
and its effect on global supply chains, 
as well as the Russian-Ukraine conflict 
which affects key production regions for 
these inputs. Similarly, for other important 
products, like poultry, there have also 
been disease-related aspects impacting 
the sector.  
207.3.	 In some instances, such as with seeds 
for sowing, the trade data shows that 
167	 Magubane, K. 2024. Wamkele Mene, the African Continental Free Trade Area secretary-general, says contintent must act 
as global conflicts hit food security. Sunday Times (date: 10 March 2024). 
demand shifts in large economies like 
China can materially influence a country’s 
trade position, which points to the 
interconnectedness of trade at the global 
level.
207.4.	 Trade at the global and continent level 
points to the numerous value chain linkages 
across various agricultural industries. Key 
inputs like seeds and fertiliser influence 
the costs and production in other food 
value chains. Another example is where 
cooking oil finds its way into other 
products like animal feed, which means 
even marginal increases can imply higher 
prices for meat at the consumer level. 
207.5.	 Key producer regions, such as the Black 
Sea region for wheat, sunflower oil, crude 
oil and fertiliser, influence global supply 
and can subsequently affect any linked 
food value chains. For example, the 
continuous rise in cooking oil prices was 
reported to dampen demand for poultry. 
207.6.	 For certain agricultural products, like citrus 
fruit for South Africa, or tea for Kenya, when 
a country is dominant in its exports for 
that product – there are opportunities for 
selling at higher prices when the produce 
is sold in the overseas market, particularly 
if that market has a shortage. Where 
countries have a competitive advantage 
could point to where there may be value 
chain opportunities in that sector, to create 
more employment opportunities, like with 
citrus processing activities for example.
207.7.	 Across the African and global trade data, 
the trade data generally shows a change 
in trend, or the pre-existing trend gets 
steeper around the Covid-19 pandemic 
in 2020. This speaks to the connection in 
trade across continents, and the profound 
and extensive impact that the Covid-19 

ACF African Competition Forum
253
pandemic has had on both global and 
regional trade. 
4.2.	 FINDINGS AT THE REGIONAL LEVEL OF 
TRADE
208.	 This section looks at the same products 
above, but considers the trade flow data 
and observations at the SADC regional level 
of trade. 
209.	  Fertiliser. When considering fertiliser trade 
at the regional level, the trade data clearly 
shows that South Africa is a net exporter 
of nitrogenous fertiliser within Africa and 
the SADC region (which is different to its 
global trade position), and that a significant 
portion of its African exports are supplying 
SADC member countries. This corresponds 
to public reports on South Africa’s fertiliser 
trade, where it is noted that the SADC 
countries have been notable importers of 
nitrogenous fertilisers from South Africa.  
210.	  Seeds for sowing. In terms of seeds for 
sowing, South Africa is a net exporter of 
seeds for sowing to the SADC region. The 
trade data shows that import volumes 
grew by 18,5% on a compound annual 
growth rate (CAGR) basis for the period 
2018 to 2022. Meanwhile, export volumes 
also grew, but at a slower CAGR of 10,3% 
for the same period. This highlights South 
Africa’s growing role as a key supplier of 
seeds for sowing within the SADC region, 
reflecting the country’s strategic position in 
agricultural inputs within the regional trade 
ecosystem. 
211.	  Oilseed cake. When looking at the trade 
flow data for African trade compared to 
the SADC level data, it is clear that most of 
South Africa’s oilseed cake trade occurs at 
the SADC regional level. The average price 
($/t) trends over the 2017 to 2022 period, 
at the SADC level, are consequently the 
same as the pricing movements seen at 
the African level. The average prices show 
increases from 2020 to 2021, after which 
import prices decrease, while export prices 
still show some increase from 2021 to 2022.
212.	  Crude Sunflower oil. South Africa has 
become a major player in supplying SADC 
countries with sunflower oil – which is 
evident from the trade flow data, as South 
Africa is seen to be a net exporter of 
sunflower oil volumes in the region. Even at 
the SADC level of trade, the global rise in 
edible oil prices and its effect are noticeable 
from the increases seen at the regional 
level from 2020 onwards. Reports cite poor 
climate conditions globally, resulting in 
poor harvests of sunflower, canola, palm 
and other edible oils, in their respective 
production regions, which has created 
global shortages – with the consequence 
of higher shelf prices for popular oils like 
sunflower oil.
213.	  Maize. There is a large degree of overlap 
between South Africa’s maize trade with the 
rest of the continent and its trade with the 
SADC region, as the region represents the 
bulk of its maize exporting markets. What 
stands out from the main SADC importers 
is Zimbabwe’s imported value of maize in 
2020, which was 168% larger than the next 
biggest importer that year (Botswana) and 
likely due to Zimbabwe’s poor domestic 
maize harvest from the 2018/19 production 
season, and the resultant lack of domestic 
maize supply , as noted in reports at the 
time.
214.	  Poultry. At the SADC level, South Africa is 
also seen as a key exporter of poultry to the 
region, while importing very little poultry 
from the SADC region. When mapping out 
regional trade for poultry, it is clear that 
South Africa’s poultry trade is concentrated 
within the SADC region, especially in 
relation to countries bordering or close to 
South Africa.
215.	  Onions. As observed at the continental 
level, it is also clear that at the SADC level 
South Africa is a net exporter of onions. 

ACF African Competition Forum
254
Even though the country is a net exporter 
at the regional SADC level, South Africa’s 
local consumption of onion is also quite 
high, which leaves around 17% of onion 
production going to export markets.
216.	  Citrus fruit. A large portion of South Africa’s 
citrus fruit exports to the rest of Africa are in 
fact exported to SADC countries. Over the 
six-year period, it is observed that export 
volumes have shown a slight increasing 
trend, while South Africa’s imports displayed 
a general sideways trend for the period 
analysed. Average pricing data shows that 
South Africa receives substantially higher 
prices for its citrus fruit exports to SADC, 
than the average prices of its citrus fruit 
imports from the SADC region.
217.	  Coffee. The trade data indicates that South 
Africa’s coffee trade with the SADC region 
is mainly accounted for by its coffee export 
volumes to the region, as import volumes 
of coffee at this trade level are fairly low 
(aside from a sizeable increase seen from 
2021 to 2022). South Africa’s coffee exports 
saw a large, temporary increase in 2020, 
which seems to have been driven by a large 
increase in coffee volume (in tons) imported 
by Namibia, which saw an increase of 130% 
in its import volumes from 2019 to 2020.
218.	  Tea. For the SADC level trade in tea, South 
Africa is a net importer of tea, as its import 
volumes are much higher than its export 
volumes of tea. In terms of the average 
prices for South Africa’s tea imports and 
exports, the trade data shows much higher 
average prices for tea exports, on a dollar 
per ton basis, which was seen to decrease 
temporarily in 2020 – likely due to the Covid 
pandemic and related supply issues.
219.	  Sugar. South Africa is generally a net 
importer of sugar, and its import volumes 
have shown a slightly increasing trend, while 
its exports have shown a decreasing trend 
over the 2017 to 2022 period. The trade 
data indicates that the bulk of South Africa’s 
bilateral trade in sugar occurs at the SADC 
regional level (which is also confirmed in 
the large degree of trade data overlap 
between Africa and SADC). The same 
observations from the Africa level of trade 
apply to South Africa’s trade with the SADC 
region as a whole – in that, average import 
prices ($/t) are higher than average export 
prices, and the sugar trade in the SADC 
region also sees fairly low prices relative to 
other commodities, due to subsidised and 
dumped imports in the sugar market.
220.	 Broadly, the main observations emanating 
from trade at the regional level include the 
following:
220.1.	 Movements in supply and prices at the 
global level influence and affect prices 
seen at the regional level. For instance, 
the global rise in edible oil prices due 
to climate-related supply shortages had 
noticeable impacts at the regional level of 
trade, from 2020 onwards;
220.2.	 Crop production and harvest at a country 
level influence or drive trade flows (and 
trade volumes) at the regional level. For 
example, Zimbabwe’s poor maize season 
was seen to cause a rise in recorded 
exports from South Africa to the region; 
220.3.	 There are some trade gaps at the regional 
level, evidenced for example in terms of 
coffee and tea trade. The trade data shows 
South Africa does not import meaningful 
amounts of coffee from the SADC region, 
which shows there may be opportunities 
for cross-border, regional collaboration 
in the labour-intensive coffee value 
chain. The coffee value chain therefore 
also offers employment opportunities 
along the production process. There 
exist opportunities for SADC markets 
supplying tea to South Africa to expand 
into producing premium teas (where 
currently this demand is met mainly by 
countries like Kenya and Sri Lanka). 

ACF African Competition Forum
255
4.3.	 FINDINGS AT THE COUNTRY LEVEL OF 
TRADE
221.	 In terms of South Africa’s agricultural trade 
at the individual country level, the section 
below 
summarises 
the 
main 
findings 
for each of the key analysed agricultural 
products. 
222.	  Maize. South Africa is one of the largest 
African exporters of maize globally, and also 
within the SADC region, where it trades as 
a net exporter of maize. The top importers 
of South Africa’s maize in the SADC 
region, by order of value in 2022, include 
Botswana, Eswatini, Namibia, Mozambique 
and Lesotho. Egypt is one of the few larger 
maize importers globally, and interestingly it 
sources the bulk of its maize from countries 
outside of Africa, like Brazil and Argentina, 
despite Africa being a major production 
region for maize. 
223.	  Sugar. As noted above, South Africa is a 
net importer of sugar in the SADC region, 
with most of its traded volumes taking place 
at the SADC level. South Africa exports 
most of its sugar in the SADC region to 
Namibia, Mozambique, Lesotho, Botswana 
and Madagascar, while Eswatini is a major 
exporter of its sugar to South Africa, which 
as noted above, has been intensified further 
due to its EU preferential quotas coming 
to an end in 2017. Individual country level 
trade data shows that there are countries 
in Africa which are large importers of sugar 
(by global standards) and these countries 
source their sugar predominantly from non-
African countries like Brazil, Switzerland, 
Poland, Spain, France and Belgium. 
224.	  Coffee. In terms of South Africa’s SADC 
trade in coffee, its largest importers are 
Namibia and Botswana, while South Africa 
generally sources its coffee from Tanzania, 
and in terms of general African countries 
(outside the SADC region), it mainly imports 
coffee from  Ethiopia, Uganda, Rwanda and 
Kenya. Kenya is also a major coffee exporter 
globally, and ranks as the 5th largest 
exporter of coffee from Africa by 2022 trade 
value (in dollar thousand). 
225.	  Tea. South Africa is a net importer of tea 
at the SADC level, with most of its imports 
coming 
from 
Malawi, 
Zimbabwe 
and 
Tanzania. Outside of the SADC region, other 
African countries exporting tea to South 
Africa include Kenya, Morocco, Nigeria, 
Cameroon and Rwanda. Kenya is a major tea 
producer, and its main global markets for its 
tea exports include Pakistan, Egypt, the UK 
and UAE. In Africa, its main export markets 
are Egypt, Sudan, Nigeria, Somalia and 
Cote d’Ivoire, while within the SADC region 
these markets are South Africa, Tanzania, 
Mauritius, Zambia and the DRC. 
226.	 Broadly, the main observations emanating 
from the trade data at the country level 
include the following:
226.1.	 Maize trade data shows that there could be 
some gaps in trade within Africa. The case 
of Egypt being one of the largest maize 
importers globally, but sourcing most of 
its maize from countries outside of Africa, 
might point to gaps in current regional 
trade, and thus offer opportunities to 
understand the current structure of the 
maize trade, and potentially encourage 
maize trade on an intra-continental level. 
226.2.	 Similarly to the maize trade, there appears 
to be a gap in African sugar trade, as large 
sugar importers source their sugar mainly 
from non-African countries, where this 
could point to missed opportunities for 
more African and regional trade. 
226.3.	 Further to maize and sugar, the coffee 
trade market and tea trade also point 
to potential trade gaps, where it could 
be that African-based producers can 
share more in the benefits of trade in 
the key crops. For instance, South Africa 
imports most of its coffee from non-SADC 
countries, while tea presents another case 

ACF African Competition Forum
256
of African producers selling most of their 
tea globally, which could imply these 
crops can offer value chains with more 
trade potentially, within SADC and Africa. 
4.4.	 SUMMARY OF OVERALL FINDINGS 
227.	 South Africa’s agricultural trade flows present 
a multifaceted picture, reflecting its strategic 
position in both regional and global markets. 
At the global and African levels, South Africa 
emerges as a crucial player, serving as a 
net importer of key agricultural inputs like 
fertilisers, while also acting as a significant 
exporter of agricultural products to other 
African countries. This dual role points to 
South Africa’s importance in connecting 
African markets with global supply chains, 
particularly evident in its export of maize, 
citrus fruits, and poultry, among others. 
228.	 The country’s agricultural trade dynamics 
are influenced by global events, such as 
the Covid-19 pandemic, climate change, 
disease 
outbreaks 
and 
geopolitical 
tensions, which have led to increased 
prices and market volatility – highlighting 
the interconnectedness of global supply 
chains, and the importance of South Africa 
in maintaining regional food security.
229.	 Within the Southern African Development 
Community (SADC) region, South Africa’s 
trade flows highlight its role as a central 
agricultural hub. It is a net exporter of 
essential crops like maize and a key supplier 
of agricultural inputs, including seeds and 
fertilisers, to neighbouring countries. This 
regional trade is crucial for food security and 
economic stability within the SADC region, 
with South Africa’s exports supporting both 
direct consumption needs and agricultural 
production across the region. The country’s 
strategic export of sugar, despite being a 
net importer at the SADC level, along with 
its significant trade in coffee and tea, further 
illustrate the depth of its agricultural trade 
relationships within the region.
230.	 At the individual country level, South Africa’s 
agricultural trade flows reveal a landscape 
of bilateral trade relationships. The country’s 
role as a major exporter of maize to nations 
like Zimbabwe and Botswana highlights 
its importance in addressing food security 
challenges within the continent. Conversely, 
its import of sugar from countries like 
Eswatini, alongside its export of sugar to 
other SADC countries, reflects nuanced 
trade 
dynamics 
driven 
by 
regional 
agreements and preferential quotas, and 
market demand. Furthermore, South Africa’s 
importation of coffee and tea from specific 
African countries, along with its global export 
markets for these commodities, showcases 
its strategic approach to diversifying trade 
partners, and optimising its agricultural 
export portfolio.
231.	 Despite being a net exporter for many 
products, 
South 
Africa’s 
dependency 
on imports for key inputs like fertilisers 
and seeds for sowing underscores the 
interdependence within the SADC region, 
the persistence of concentration in key 
input industries, and the importance of 
maintaining robust trade relations with both 
regional and global partners.
232.	 Membership in regional and international 
trade agreements such as SADC, SACU, and 
the AfCFTA plays a pivotal role in creating 
a free trade area and facilitating smoother 
trade across member countries. Export-
led growth strategies and various support 
programs target smallholder farmers and 
promote value addition, while infrastructure 
investment and capacity building initiatives 
aim to improve market players’ skills, and 
enhance productivity. However, challenges 
such as trade barriers, competition concerns, 
infrastructure 
constraints, 
and 
climate 
change impacts hinder trade development 
and growth in agriculture.
233.	 Addressing 
these 
challenges 
requires 
a coordinated approach and improved 
stakeholder 
collaboration. 
Despite 
the 

ACF African Competition Forum
257
trade challenges, there are numerous 
opportunities for improving South Africa’s 
agriculture and agro-processing trade, 
including 
investment 
in 
infrastructure, 
market 
diversification, 
value 
addition, 
technology adoption, capacity building, 
sustainable practices, trade facilitation, 
as well as financial support and access 
to credit. By implementing these policy 
options and trade opportunities, South 
Africa can strengthen its agriculture and 
agro-processing trade within the SADC 
region and across Africa, contributing to 
economic development and food security.
234.	 The study illustrates that trade flows (and 
pricing) 
for 
agricultural 
products 
are 
significantly influenced by global market 
dynamics, due to market connectivity, 
including supply chain disruptions, global 
demand shifts, disease outbreaks and 
international pricing trends. This indicates 
the necessity for SADC countries to enhance 
their adaptive capacities and resilience to 
external shocks, which further suggests that 
missing market opportunities or gaps in 
trade should be explored.
235.	 Finally, the study shows that there exist 
opportunities 
for 
value 
addition, 
and 
potential trade gaps. Higher prices placed 
on certain exports from South Africa to 
other SADC countries, such as citrus fruits 
and sunflower oil, point to opportunities 
for value addition within the region, and 
within other areas of the value chain 
like processing. Developing processing 
capacities in other SADC countries could 
lead to increased intra-regional trade and 
economic diversification. Certain trade 
gaps, such as the dynamic whereby an 
African country is sourcing most of its maize 
requirements from outside of Africa, could 
also point to the potential of fostering new 
trade flow opportunities in new markets. 
Although South Africa accounts for a larger 
portion of intra-African and regional trade, as 
it is well-known that South Africa is primarily 
an exporter to the rest of the continent, this 
does again reflect the potential for the rest 
of Africa to sell more of their agricultural 
products to South Africa.
236.	 South Africa’s trade at different geographic 
levels thus plays a key role in shaping 
regional trade development and integration. 
The insights from the study suggest the 
potential for a more integrated, resilient, 
and prosperous SADC region, which then 
also enhances trade in other regional 
economic 
communities. 
More 
cross-
border collaboration and cooperation for 
agricultural trade, as well as for competition 
enforcement, 
can 
result 
in 
increased 
awareness of food trade and linked value 
chains at African and regional levels, more 
trade beneficiation and therefore more food 
security, and agricultural growth for African 
countries. 
237.	 The analysis of agricultural trade patterns 
across the different regions, along with 
regional and product-related trade insights, 
also offers the opportunity for additional, 
future research into certain problematic 
food value chains, and food value chains 
where there are currently trade gaps, or 
simply those food value chains that warrant 
additional analysis going forward.

ACF African Competition Forum
261
Zambia’s agricultural market and its export 
potential remain high, given the country's resource 
endowment and affiliation to preferential regional 
blocks. However, these factors are yet to be fully 
utilised to the full benefit of the country. This paper 
analyses Zambia’s trade flows of essential food 
products and agricultural inputs within Africa, 
regional economic communities (RECs), and key 
counterparts, and seeks to highlight competition 
issues or constraints related to traded food 
products and agricultural inputs. The study further 
considers pricing trends for the traded goods. The 
main findings of the study are that Zambia’s main 
imports for agriculture, and import segments, are 
crude edible oils, fertilisers and tilapia fish, while 
the main exports include raw cane sugar, maize 
seed, oilcake, and industrial products such as 
sweet biscuits and sugar confectionaries. There 
is room to reduce imports, as seen with efforts 
in investments in the fertiliser and fish sectors, 
while the right policies and support can increase 
emerging industrial exports of agriculture food 
products. While markets, particularly fertiliser and 
sugar, remain highly susceptible to competition 
issues, 
changing 
policies 
and 
increasing 
competition promotion will be key to changing 
the markets' structures and firm behavior. Export 
market pricing appears to extract margins in 
near, less competitive markets, than far, more 
competitive markets. Given the limited reach of 
country specific competition laws, this requires 
regional competition promotion, in order to 
protect less competitive markets from being 
subjected to unfairly high prices

Phiri, J.; Malec, K.; Majune, S.K.; Appiah-Kubi, S.N.K.; Gebeltová, Z.; Kotásková, S.K.; Maitah, M.; Maitah, K.; Naluwooza, P.

1.	
Zambia covers 75 million hectares (752,000 
km2), out of which 58 percent (42 million 
hectares) is classified as medium-to-high-
potential for agriculture production. Despite 
the 42 million hectares of arable land, an 
abundance 
of 
groundwater 
resources, 
nutrient-rich soil, and low population density, 
Zambia’s agriculture sector has not yet 
been exploited to reach its full potential. 
The government recognises that increasing 
agriculture and agribusiness is critical for 
improving 
incomes 
and 
food 
security, 
reducing poverty, and creating a more 
diversified and resilient economy.1 In addition, 
Zambia stands to benefit from global and 
regional markets as food demand continues 
to grow for crops such as soybeans and 
maize, which are inputs to animal feed and 
vegetable oil.2 Eastern and Southern Africa 
have persistent agricultural trade deficits, and 
are subject to extreme weather shocks, such 
as the 2021/2022 droughts in Kenya, and in 
the Horn of Africa. It is projected that over 
time, the East and Southern Africa regions 
will have more frequent and deeper shocks, 
and it will therefore be even more important 
that Zambia sustainably expands agricultural 
production and exports for the region’s 
adaptation to climate change.3
2.	
However, 
trade 
restrictions 
and 
anti-
competitive conduct are major roadblocks 
to realising Zambia’s full potential. Zambia’s 
farmers in crops such as soybeans and maize 
have consistently been subjected to returns 
as low as  40-50%, which is  lower  than what 
they could have obtained in more attractive 
export markets. Prices for key imported inputs, 
such as fertiliser, have been inflated by up to 
45%, exacerbating the drain on subsidies for 
the Farmer Input Support Program (FISP), 
and eroding the competitiveness of non-
subsidised agriculture.4 
3.	
Notably, despite the slow diversification 
process, agriculture remains a prioritised 
sector due to its high poverty reduction, 
job creation, foreign exchange earnings, 
and industrialisation potential.5  The low 
contribution to GDP in Zambia is due to high 
rainfall dependence, limited investments 
and 
value 
addition, 
poor 
productivity, 
and 
immature 
markets.6 
Furthermore, 
contemporary challenges such as climatic 
change have awakened Zambia and the entire 
globe to the need for food security. This is 
essential as 840 million people are expected 
to go hungry by the year 2030.7 
4.	
Thus, it is paramount for Zambia to not only 
expand its agriculture food and input product 
scope by partaking in international trade, but 
2
INTRODUCTION

ACF African Competition Forum
263
to also enhance the resilience of its agricultural 
exports.8 To accelerate the diversification 
process, 
the 
Zambian 
Government 
is 
promoting agriculture as a way to diversify 
the economy away from the overreliance 
on copper, through the development of 
farm blocks in all 10 provinces for large and 
medium commercial farming, fish farming, 
and livestock production.9
2.1.	 BACKGROUND 
5.	
The African Competition Forum (ACF), a 
network of African Competition Authorities 
(ACF) embarked on a regional research 
study focusing on regional and continental 
trade flows of agriculture food products and 
agro-input products. The study of trade flows 
and regional value chains in key agricultural 
sectors 
involved 
six 
African 
countries; 
Kenya, Mauritius, South Africa, Zambia, 
Zimbabwe, and Angola, with the aim of 
providing critical insights into the economic 
interdependencies, competitive dynamics, 
and structural challenges within the continent. 
The regional research aims to elucidate trade 
flows and competition issues that shape the 
agricultural and agro-processing sectors. 
6.	
The 
examination 
of 
the 
intricate 
trade 
relationships 
seeks 
to 
foster 
a 
deeper 
understanding of how regional integration and 
market structures influence economic outcomes 
and agricultural frameworks. By integrating the 
insights from six diverse but interconnected 
markets, this study aims to contribute to the 
discourse on regional economic integration and 
agricultural development in Africa. Through this 
study, the African Competition Forum seeks to 
inform industry stakeholders, and researchers, 
about the intricate dynamics of agricultural 
trade at different geographic levels, and the 
critical measures needed to optimise the 
benefits of regional integration and enhanced 
regional trade in agriculture.
8	
Phiri, J.; Malec, K.; Majune, S.K.; Appiah-Kubi, S.N.K.; Gebeltová, Z.; Kotásková, S.K.; Maitah, M.; Maitah, K.; Naluwooza, P. 
Durability of Zambia’s Agricultural Exports. Agriculture 2021, 11, 73. https://doi.org/10.3390/agriculture11010073
9	
https://www.export.gov/apex/article2?id=Zambia-Agricultural-Sector
2.2.	 STUDY OBJECTIVE
7.	
The overall study objectives are to:
i.	
Analyse trade flows of essential food products 
and agricultural inputs within Africa, regional 
economic communities (RECs), and key 
counterparts.
ii.	
Understand competition issues or constraints 
related 
to 
traded 
food 
products 
and 
agricultural inputs.
iii.	 Conduct market structure and pricing trends 
analysis for the traded goods.
iv.	 Provide recommendations to the study 
outcomes. 
2.3.	 SCOPE
8.	
The Zambian Chapter (Chapter) is aimed 
at examining trade dynamics, in terms of 
both Zambia’s exports and imports within 
the region. Further, the Chapter sought to 
identify the current food value chains being 
traded, and to analyse the associated prices 
at different stages of the value chain. 
2.4.	 METHODOLOGY
9.	
The study relied on secondary data and 
information gathered from previous research, 
reports 
by 
the 
Commission, 
concluded 
investigation cases, and studies conducted 
by other institutions. It focused on analysing 
the top food products and agricultural inputs 
traded by Zambia. The chosen products for 
analysis encompass a range of categories that 
are significant in the agricultural trade dynamics 
of Zambia, and its neighbouring countries 
within the Southern Africa Development 
Community (SADC) and the Common markets 
for Eastern and Southern Africa (COMESA) 
regions. These products were selected based 
on their prevalence and importance in trade 
interactions within the region.

10.	 According to the Diagnostic Trade Integration 
Study Update (DTISU) for Zambia published 
in 2023, Zambia is open to domestic and 
foreign trade; with foreign trade accounting 
for 86 percent of the country’s GDP. Zambia 
is the 83rd largest export economy in the 
world, and the 78th most complex economy, 
according to the Economic Complexity Index 
(ECI).10 Zambia’s merchandise exports are 
broadly concentrated in primary commodities 
with agriculture exports, particularly oilcakes 
and cane sugar, together with other Non-
Traditional Exports (NTE) accounting for 19% 
of exports, while unrefined copper ($6.2billion) 
and refined copper ($2.2billion), constituted 
56 percent as of 2021. Zambia’s top imports 
include fertilisers ($279.56) as an agro input, 
while other imports such as petroleum oils, 
medicaments, and motor vehicles accounted 
for ($610. 35 million), ($229.88), ($212.67) 
constituting about 9 percent, 4 percent, and 3 
percent respectively.11 
11.	 Zambia’s exports encounter several non-
tariff measures (NTMs) in its various markets. 
The NTMs include government involvement 
in 
trade, 
restrictive 
practices, 
customs 
and 
administrative 
entry 
procedures, 
technical barriers, SPS measures, labeling 
requirements, 
and 
licensing 
constraints. 
The lack of coordination among border 
agencies, checkpoints, stringent rules of 
origin, burdensome technical regulatory 
frameworks, and insufficient knowledge of 
procedures and requirements for importing 
and exporting are also hindering progress. A 
recent study by the World Bank highlighted 
that NTBs reduce the competitiveness of 
Zambian exports in the region, including for 
small-scale trade (World Bank, 2018). Here 
are some examples of non-tariff measures 
that have been identified as limiting Zambia’s 
exports to market destinations.12  
3.1.	 AFRICA’S REGIONAL TRADE
12.	 Africa’s recorded cross-border trade has 
grown modestly in recent decades, with 
limited growth in merchandise trade, and 
an unchanged share of services trade in 
GDP. These trade patterns are consistent 
with the continent’s limited integration, 
reflecting 
its 
fragmented 
trade 
policy 
landscape, and marked by multiple regional 
economic communities, a challenging trade 
environment with gaps in structural factors 
such as transport networks, customs and 
border processes, and a lack of access to 
finance.13 Beyond the structural constraints, 
intra-regional trade in Africa faces numerous 
challenges, such as lack of resource and 
production 
complementarities 
between 
many African countries, and low levels of 
incomes and investment. There are various 
infrastructure-related 
and 
policy-induced 
impediments to intra-African trade.14
3
ZAMBIA'S TRADE

ACF African Competition Forum
265
13.	 It should be noted that intraregional trade 
importance remains high for the continent. 
Although intraregional trade still accounts 
for only a small share of total trade in Africa, 
it has increasingly grown in importance. Still, 
Africa suffers from challenges that most other 
regions do not face; hence, the region requires 
unprecedented levels of commitment for 
interregional 
trade 
to 
succeed. 
These 
challenges include geographic and political 
fragmentation and thick borders (a complex 
mix of tariff and nontariff restrictions that slow 
trade) – all of which increase the per unit cost 
of moving goods across borders.15
14.	 There is potential for Zambia to take 
advantage of its “geographic bias” – which 
favours intraregional trade. Despite the 
broad trend toward globalisation and the 
reduction of trade barriers, the relative share 
of intraregional trade should be a priority, 
due in part to trade-related advantages such 
as proximity, lower transport costs, increased 
knowledge from repeated interaction, cultural 
and historical affinity, as well as preferential 
trade agreements that Zambia has made with 
other economies.

facilitate 
trade, 
Zambia 
has

improvements at key border crossings,

at Nakonde, the Zambia-Malawi border at

at Chanida. The cross-border movement 
of goods remains low, and combined with 
an insufficiently developed and maintained 
network of primary, secondary, and tertiary 
 
roads, this creates steep transportation costs 
for traded agricultural goods.16

17.	 Agricultural 
products 
account 
for 
approximately 23.9 - 29% percent of Zambia’s 
Non-Traditional Exports (NTEs).18 Zambia’s 
key agricultural exports include maize, sugar, 
and soybean products. The export volume for

million. Key trading partners in this sector 
include Zimbabwe, the Democratic Republic 
of Congo, and South Africa.19 Zambia is a net 
importer of fish, baby foods, dairy, edible oils, 
horticulture, and processed poultry products. 
Some imported foods need to be substituted 
with locally processed foods. The local fish 
supply gap of up to 127,000 tons per annum 
presents great aquafeeds and fish processing 
opportunities. Zambia is also faced with a 
104,436-ton per annum edible oils deficit, 
 
which needs reducing with locally produced 
products.20

over the 2015 to 2022 period, as shown in 
fig 3 below, the magnitude of the surplus 
indicates only a slight difference between its 
agriculture exports and imports.

3.3.	 TRADE FACILITATION 
19.	 Transport 
and 
trade 
facilitation 
costs 
significantly 
impact 
Zambia’s 
export 
competitiveness. High logistics and trade-
related costs have been a persistent barrier to 
enhancing the country’s export performance, 
reducing its ability to compete in international 
markets. The Zambia Statistical Bulletin for 
January 2024 indicates that road transport has 
continued to account for the highest value of 
exports, representing a 43.6% share (imports 
50.6 %). Rail transport is second with 3.9% 
(imports 0.4%) and air transport was third 
with 2.3 percent (Imports 6.5%).21 The Zambia 
Development Agency compilation of the cost 
of doing business for 2014 showed that road 
transport costs on average between USD120 
to USD250 per ton, while railway costs 
between USD30 to USD130 per ton.22 Further, 
a 2015 study by the Centre for Competition, 
Regulation 
and 
Economic 
Development 
(CCRED) showed that the range of $100-
$110/ton serves as a good benchmark for 
the break-even point of trucking companies 
operating in the Zambian market, with return 
load, while without return load it is about 
$185/ton.23 The high cost was attributed to 
the high cost environment compared to their 
counterparts, for example in Tanzania,  driven 
by high fuel costs, road tolls, and taxes and 
levies which operators in other countries did 
not face, as well as fuel levy, inspection of 
trucks at $6/truck for every four months, and 
road tax of approximately $200/truck per year 
among others.24 
20.	 Zambia, being a landlocked country, faces 
substantial challenges related to transport. 
The cost of moving goods to and from 
seaports through neighbouring countries 
adds significantly to overall export costs. 

ACF African Competition Forum
268
For example, inefficiencies in road and 
rail networks, along with high fuel costs 
and border delays, increase the expenses 
associated with exporting Zambian goods. 
These transport costs can be as high as 
40% of the product value, making Zambian 
exports less competitive compared to those 
from coastal countries with more efficient and 
cheaper logistics networks.25​
21.	 Beyond transport, various trade facilitation 
issues further impact export competitiveness. 
Complex customs procedures, inadequate 
infrastructure at border posts, and lack of 
automation contribute to longer clearance 
times and higher costs. UNCTAD highlights 
that inefficiencies in trade facilitation can 
increase trade costs by 8-15%, affecting 
the speed and reliability of supply chains, 
which are critical for maintaining export 
competitiveness.26 Efforts are being made 
to address these challenges. For instance, 
Zambia has been working on implementing 
the WTO Trade Facilitation Agreement, 
which aims to simplify and harmonise 
international trade procedures. Investments 
in infrastructure, such as improving road 
networks and border facilities, are also being 
25	
Economic and Social Commission for Asia and the Pacific (December 2009); Impact of trade facilitation on export 
competitiveness : a regional perspective; accessed on https://www.unescap.org/publications/impact-trade-facilitation-
export-competitiveness-regional-perspective-studies-trade-and#
26	
https://unctad.org/topic/transport-and-trade-logistics
27	
Economic and Social Commission for Asia and the Pacific (December 2009); Impact of trade facilitation on export 
competitiveness : a regional perspective; accessed on https://www.unescap.org/publications/impact-trade-facilitation-
export-competitiveness-regional-perspective-studies-trade-and#
undertaken. The introduction of automated 
systems like ASYCUDA (Automated System 
for Customs Data) is expected to streamline 
customs procedures and reduce delays​.27​

3.6.	 REGIONAL TRADE
30.	 Zambia belongs to the 21-member Common 
Market for Eastern and Southern Africa 
(COMESA), which allows for preferential 
tariff duties among member states.  Zambia 
also belongs to the 16-member Southern 
African Development Community (SADC), 
which established a free trade area in 2008.  
The establishment of a single market through 
the merged Tripartite Free Trade Area 
(COMESA, the East African Community [EAC], 
and SADC) was formally launched in June 
2015.36  Zambia deposited the instruments of 
ratification for the African Tripartite Free Trade 
Area Agreement with COMESA on January 
15, 2021. 
31.	 Zambia is a signatory to the African 
Continental Free Trade Agreement (AfCFTA). 
The country signed to the Agreement on 10th 
February 2019, and deposited instruments 
of ratification on 5th February 2021. Though 
a signatory to the AfCFTA, Zambia had not 
finalised its participation as of February 2022, 
as it has sought more time to consult with the 
private sector before committing.37
32.	 Since the operationalisation of the Southern 
Africa Development Community (SADC) and 
the Common Market for Eastern and Southern 
Africa (COMESA), Zambia’s most stellar 
exports to SADC and COMESA happened 
in 2013, when the country recorded exports 
worth US$ 3.1 billion and US$ 1.8 billion, 
respectively, 
in 
Non-Traditional 
Exports 
(NTEs). Post the 2013 export boom, Zambia’s 
utilisation of its regional export markets has 
been in decline, only accounting for 4.7% 
and 17.2% of aggregated intra SADC and 
COMESA exports respectively, in 2020.38
3.6.1.	 COMESA Trade
33.	 COMESA 
trade 
statistics 
indicates 
that 
Zambia’s total exports to the COMESA region 
increased by 3 percent between 2019 and 
2020. The increase in nominal value terms 
was from US$1.23bn in 2019 to US$1.27bn 
in 2020. Zambia’s imports from the COMESA 
region declined by 40 percent from 2019 
to 2020 – from US$700 million in 2019 to 
US$422 million in 2020. Major destinations 
for Zambia’s exports into COMESA region in 
2020 were the Democratic Republic of Congo, 
Malawi, Zimbabwe, Kenya and Burundi.

3.7.	 EXPORT DURABILITY
37.	 In order to consider specific pricing trends, 
it can be useful to first establish the export 
durability – as this indicates the long-term 
influence of the product in the pricing 
structure. For example, one paper looking 
at Zambia’s export durability of agriculture 

ACF African Competition Forum
275
products in Zambia, with specific attention to 
maize, sugar, cotton, and tobacco between 
1996 and 2019, found that approximately 
39% of Zambia’s agricultural products were 
exported beyond the first year of trading, and 
less than 10% lasted up to 6 years of trading.43

3.8.	 SPECIFIC PRODUCT CATEGORY PRICING 
TRENDS 
3.8.1.	 Maize seed
40.	 Zambia has a mature seed industry, which 
exports over 60,000 metric tons of seed, mainly 
maize, each year.44 Zambia’s seed certification 
standards are considered equivalent to all 
other OECD member countries within the 
same seed schemes, giving the Zambian 
seed companies the capability to trade using 
the globally recognized OECD seed label for 
export, and to have access to markets with 
stringent seed regulations. This, coupled with 
investments into the seed industry, has made 
Zambia the largest exporter of maize seed 
within the SADC region, and across Africa.45 
Figure 8 below shows the existence of trade 
for Zambian maize seed in Kenya, Malawi and 
Zimbabwe.

3.8.3.	 Soya beans 
44.	 In 
2022, 
Zambia 
exported 
$13.6M 
in 
Soybeans. The main destinations of Zambia 
exports of soybeans were South Africa ($3M), 
Pitcairn Islands ($2.96M), Zimbabwe ($2.26M), 
Botswana ($2.1M), and Equatorial Guinea 
($1.15M)49 as shown in fig 10 below. The 
Pitcairn Islands, officially Pitcairn, Henderson, 
Ducie and Oeno Islands, are a group of four 
volcanic islands in the southern Pacific Ocean 
that form the sole British Overseas Territory in 
the Pacific Ocean. The inhabitants of this tiny 
isolated economy exist on fishing, subsistence 
farming, handicrafts, and postage stamps. 
The fertile soil of the valleys produces a wide 
variety of fruits and vegetables, including 
citrus, sugarcane, watermelons, bananas, 
yams, and beans. Bartering is an important 
part of the economy. The major sources of 
revenue are the sale of postage stamps to 
collectors, and the sale of handicrafts to 
passing ships.50 It therefore would appear that 
the soybeans exports of close to USD 3 million 
dollars were for tax avoidance purposes.

46.	 As of March 2023, soybean was trading at

result of a well-supplied market, with limited 
large parcel trades. Zambia’s soybeans have 
since found export markets in India and China, 
which will continue to drive up the demand 
and prices. The elevated market prices will 
incentivise growers, particularly small-holder 
farmers, to include soybeans in their growing 
programs.51

Most Favoured Nation, which means…
56	
WT/TPR/S/219 Zambia Trade Policy Review
57	
https://www.tralac.org/documents/publications/trade-data-analysis/2159-zambia-intra-africa-trade-and-tariff-profile-
september-2018/file.html
49.	 Zambia’s tariff structure for agricultural 
products 
varies 
significantly 
between 
COMESA (Common Market for Eastern and 
Southern Africa) and SADC (Southern African 
Development Community) member countries, 
reflecting different levels of trade integration 
and preferential treatment within these blocs. 
Within COMESA, Zambia benefits from a 
Free Trade Area (FTA), which significantly 
reduces or eliminates tariffs on goods traded 
between member states. While there has 
been progress with tariff reductions, both 
COMESA and SADC regions face challenges 
with Non-Trade Barriers (NTBs), which can 
impede the smooth flow of trade despite 
reduced tariffs. These NTBs include complex 
customs procedures, stringent standards, 
and inefficient infrastructure, which can delay 
shipments and increase costs​.
50.	 Zambia’s 
trade 
policy 
has 
remained 
substantially unchanged since the country 
undertook 
a 
comprehensive 
reform 
programme in the 1990s, removing exchange 
controls, reducing import duties, eliminating 
import and export license requirements, 
abolishing export bans, introducing a number 
of export incentives, removing subsidies, and 
decontrolling prices. According to the World 
Bank’s Trade Tariff Restrictiveness Index, 
Zambia’s trade regime is more open than that 
of an average sub-Saharan African or low-
income country.54 The simple average applied 
MFN55 tariff of 13.4% has dropped only slightly 
since the late 1990s, but Zambia continues to 
work toward lower trade barriers, especially 
through the COMESA and SADC free-trade 
areas. In terms of customs operations, Zambia 
remains one of the world’s least efficient 
countries, according to the World Bank 
classification scheme. It ranks 153rd out of 
181countries, due to the higher number of 
required documents, considerably longer 
processing times for exports, and relatively 
high per container export and import costs, 
compared with those of an average sub-
Saharan African or low-income country.56
51.	 Zamba is part of the SADC group, meaning 
most of the goods imported from SADC 
countries are duty free. However, some 
products continue to face duties of up 
to 25% – for example various knitted and 
woven fabrics, some petroleum and fresh 
persimmons. There are also separate duties 
for South Africa and other SADC countries. 
For example, wheat flour from South Africa is 
duty free, while wheat flour from other SADC 
countries faces a 25% tariff.57
52.	 Zambia’s customs valuation is tallied on a Cost 
Insurance and Freight (CIF) basis.  Most goods 
fall into one of three tariff bands: 0-5 percent 
(Capital Equipment and Raw Materials); 15 
percent (Intermediate Goods); or 25 or 40 
per percent (Finished Goods).  The duty 
on productive machinery for agriculture, 
aquaculture is zero percent.  Other duty-free 
items include veterinary supplies among 
others, fertilisers, and seeds.  Import Value 
4
TARIFF STRUCTURES   
AND DUTIES

ACF African Competition Forum
281
Added Tax (VAT) is collected on behalf of the 
VAT Division and is charged on the Taxable 
Value, that is, Customs Value + Customs Duty 
(+ Excise Duty, where applicable) at the rate 
of 16 percent.58
53.	 COMESA member countries often benefit 
from tariffs as low as 0-5%, significantly lower 
than the general MFN rates that can go up to 
40%. For example, the tariff rate for Uganda 
(5%) compared to the general MFN rate (up 
to 25%) represents a significant reduction, 
reflecting 
a 
20% 
difference. 
Ethiopia’s 
rate of 22.5% versus the MFN rate of 25% 
shows a smaller but still notable preferential 
margin. This preferential treatment is aimed 
at enhancing trade flows and economic 
integration within the COMESA region.59 
54.	 Similarly, SADC has established an FTA, which 
reduces tariffs on intra-regional trade. However, 
the tariff rates and non-tariff barriers (NTBs) 
within SADC are sometimes different compared 
to COMESA. For non-FTA COMESA countries 
or certain SADC countries, the General ad 
valorem MFN (Most Favoured Nation) tariffs 
apply ranging from 5% to 40%, depending on 
the specific agricultural product. The average 
tariff for many agricultural products is 25%, but 
it can be higher for certain goods. This is part of 
Zambia’s broader tariff bands, which are 0-5% 
for capital equipment and raw materials, 15% 
for intermediate goods, and 25% for finished 
goods.60

4.2.	 CRUDE OILS
56.	 Products under HS1563 which comprise 
of animal, vegetable or microbial fats and 
oils and their cleavage products; prepared 
edible fats; and animal or vegetable waxes 
are generally subject to a 5% customs duty. 
There are certain sub-headings that attract 
either a free customs duty rate, or a 15% 
customs duty rate, while others are free. For 
example, imports under code1507.10.00 – 
Crude oil, whether or not degummed, attract 
a free customs duty and a standard VAT rate 
which currently is at 16% on Value for Duty 
Purposes. Products under code 1508.10.00 
or 1511.90.10 – Palm Stearin, when imported 
in bulk for further processing, attract a 15% 
customs duty rate and a standard VAT rate.64  
4.3.	 FISH 
57.	 Chapter 365 products, that is, fish and 
crustaceans, mollusks and other aquatic 
invertebrates, are subject to a 40% customs 
duty, with the standard unit of quality being 
kgs. The heading is not subject to Excise Duty 
Rate and is VAT exempt as of 2023/24.
