5.1.	 FERTILIZERS 
59.	 Since the inception of the Farmer Input 
Support Program (FISP), Government has 
increased budgetary allocations for fertiliser 
procurement. In 2024, Government provided 
K8.56 billion (approximately USD 360 million) 
with only a 6.5% reduction from the 2023 
Budgetary allocation to the program. In 
2011, US$ 184 million was spent on fertiliser 
procurement and distribution, representing 
approximately 29% of the agriculture budget 
in 2010,66 and this figure was only increased 
to 56%67 in 2016. 
60.	 Over 65% of the total fertiliser consumed in 
Zambia is under the FISP program, and this 
is mainly focused on small scale farmers. 
Commercial fertiliser, or private market 
importers, have their own licenced agents in 
major towns and well-established farm areas.68 
Agents supply large commercial farmers 
as well as very large agricultural producers, 
mostly by placing advance orders directly 
with the importer, or with an agent. Despite 
commercial prices of fertiliser in Zambia being 
quite competitive in comparison with prices in 
other countries in the region, such as Tanzania 
 
 
and Mozambique,69 prices have remained 
higher than benchmark international prices. 
61.	 Zambia imports most of its required fertiliser, 
where it is received through Beira, Dar-es-
Salaam and South African ports. The national 
fertiliser consumption is estimated at 250,000-
300,000 MT, 200,000 of which is under the FISP 
program.70 Currently, total national fertiliser 
consumption is approximately 400,000 MT. 
However, estimates by fertiliser companies 
and other actors in the chain indicate that the 
potential fertiliser consumption in Zambia 
stands higher – between 500,000 - 600,000 MT 
if the right policy environment were to prevail, 
and farmers were to receive appropriate and 
adequate extension services.71
62.	 Zambia applies a zero rate to fertiliser 
imports,72 that is, there is 0% duty and 0% tax 
on major fertiliser ingredients like Nitrogen, 
Phosphorous and Potassium – to promote 
the importation of these raw materials for the 
production of fertiliser locally.73 In general, 
the tax regime is designed to promote local 
manufacturing of fertiliser, and discourage 
importation of finished fertiliser. For instance, 
income from chemical manufacturing of 
fertiliser is taxed at a reduced rate of 15%74 
 
5
SPECIFIC TRADE COMMODITIES    
AND COMPETITION DYNAMICS 

ACF African Competition Forum
284
compared to the standard rate of 35% (2018 
and 2019) for importers.75 Fertiliser produced 
locally is thus expected to be cheaper, 
compared to imported finished fertilisers.
63.	 Incumbent 
companies, 
especially 
multinational companies, have an established 
supply network which makes it easier for 
them to access fertiliser and/or fertiliser 
raw materials than small companies, as well 
as local and new companies. Moreover, 
companies like Yara and Omnia have parent or 
sister companies operating in other countries, 
where they source fertiliser or fertiliser raw 
materials. These difficulties in accessing 
the supply of either fertiliser or fertiliser raw 
materials may be a hindrance, not only to 
downstream players seeking to enter upper 
stream, but also to new entrants.
64.	 Logistics and management constraints pose 
challenges that potentially hinder entrants 
in the market. Due to the fact that Zambia 
is landlocked, it receives most of its imports 
through ports in neighboring countries, 
such as Beira (Mozambique), Dar-es-salaam 
(Tanzania) and Durban (South Africa). This 
implies long haulage distances along mostly 
poor roads that are prevalent in Zambia, 
which ultimately translates into increased 
transportation costs for the fertiliser, or the 
materials used in the blending thereof.
65.	 Threats of cartel conduct have continued in 
the fertiliser sector due to the market structure 
and the existence of the FISP program. In 
2013, CCPC found the FISP fertiliser market 
to have been plagued with allegations of 
bid-rigging and market allocation between 
two input suppliers, Omnia Zambia Limited 
and Nyiombo Investments Limited, who were 
the winners of the government’s FISP tenders 
between 2007 and 2011.76 Investigations 
led to the fining of Omnia Fertilizer Zambia 
Limited and Nyiombo Investments Limited, 
75	
PWC Zambia-budget-2019.pdf and 2019 Budget Speech 
76	
https://one.oecd.org/document/DAF/COMP/GF(2014)1/en/pdf
77	
https://allafrica.com/stories/201306070434.html
with a fine of five per cent each of their 
respective annual turnovers for engaging in 
cartel conduct.  The cartel was estimated to 
have cost the Zambian people more than $20 
million over the years during which the two 
companies had been supplying fertiliser to 
the Government.77
5.2.	 SUGAR SECTOR
66.	 Domestic sugar retail prices in Zambia have 
remained high, and are in fact higher than 
retail prices in Kenya, a high-cost producer 
subject to tariff and non-tariff barriers. 
Furthermore, prices in Zambia do not seem 
to follow the trend of international prices. The 
high price persists despite the fact that Zambia 
is a low-cost producer, able to compete in the 
depressed international export market. The 
highly concentrated structure of production, 
paired with barriers to import competition, 
are likely to have an influence on prices. 
67.	 In 2017, CCPC fined Zambia Sugar K76,728,650 
(USD 3,069,146) for price discrimination and 
unfair pricing. This was after investigations 
were carried out, covering the period 2008 
to 2012. Investigations revealed that both 
industrial sugar consumers and household 
sugar consumers were being exploited. 
Further findings were that industrial sugar 
users on contract agreements with Zambia 
Sugar were categorised as A and B, allegedly 
based on volumes purchased. Category A was 
contracted for at least 1000 tons and Category 
B for at most 1000 tons of sugar per year. 
However, CCPC found that some consumers 
within category A were still charged lower 
prices for the same industrial sugar compared 
to others. Further, the investigation revealed 
that customers that purchased the highest 
quantities within Category A, such as Trade 
Kings Zambia, paid much more than other 
customers that purchased lower volumes, 
namely Zambia Breweries PLC over the 2008-

ACF African Competition Forum
285
2012 investigation period. The investigations 
thus revealed that industrial customers were 
not accorded similar discounts even based 
on volumes. Investigations also revealed that 
domestic household sugar was priced higher 
than export prices for the same commodity, 
mostly destined for the Great Lake’s region.78 
68.	 In addition to the investigations, CCPC has 
made several representations to have the 
technical barriers to sugar imports revised. 
Currently, household domestic sugar sold 
on the Zambian market is required by law to 
have micro nutrient fortifications. While this 
has been touted as a health measure meant 
to increase access to micronutrients (Vitamin 
A) by vulnerable groups, it has indirectly 
served as a technical barrier to trade, and has 
strengthened the dominant positions of the 
Zambian sugar players. 
5.3.	 ANIMAL FEED 
69.	 The animal feed sector is part of Zambia’s 
growing agro-processing industry. The sector 
falls under the manufacturing sector. Though 
specific contributory statistics for the animal 
feed sector are scant, the manufacturing 
sector is reported to contribute around 7.5% 
to Zambia’s Gross Domestic Product (GDP). 
In terms of value, the value of animal feed 
produced in Zambia is about K1.5 billion per 
annum (put the USD dollar amount here), 
which is mainly biased towards poultry. 
Poultry constitutes about 50% of all meat 
consumed in Zambia, resulting in about 80% 
of the feeds produced being destined for the 
poultry market.79
70.	 The Zambian animal feed industry is a K1.5 
billion (USD 60 million) per annum industry, 
which is shared across all the investors 
involved in animal feed industry. Most of the 
value within the value chain is localised in 
Zambia. Data from the Food and Agriculture 
Organisation (FAO) shows that between 1975 
78	
https://cuts-lusaka.org/pdf/Consumer_Watch-Aug-Oct2017.pdf
79	
https://tradingeconomics.com/zambia/exports/malawi/oilcake-vegetable-fats-oils
and 2016, imports of soybeans into Zambia 
constituted on average only about 7% of 
total soybeans available in the economy, 
demonstrating that Zambia largely grows 
its own soya beans. The FAO statistics also 
reveal that Zambia imports only about 19% 
of its soybean cake, which also shows that 
cake demand is largely sourced from the 
local market. This implies that the K1.5 billion 
(also put USD value) animal feed industry 
largely rewards the local value chain, which 
demonstrates that the industry is very critical 
for sustaining livelihoods in Zambia.
5.4.	 EDIBLE OILS 
71.	 Despite a fairly large number of producers, 
the edible oil industry is highly concentrated. 
Estimates from one edible oil producer, a 
recent entrant that is only about two years 
into production, are that there is a dominance 
of only one producer, Global Oil Industries, 
ahead of all the other players. It is estimated 
that Global Industries has a market share 
of about 60%, following their aggressive 
entry into the industry, with Mt Meru being 
a distant second player, with a much smaller 
market share of about 25%. This would 
therefore imply that two players in the edible 
oil industry have a market share of over 80% 
combined, thus pointing to a market that is 
highly concentrated.
5.5.	 MAIZE
72.	 From time immemorial, the Government of 
the Republic of Zambia has always played 
a role in the maize subsector. It is as a result 
of various regulations that have been put in 
place, that enable and allow the Government 
to intervene. Despite its efforts to completely 
liberalise the maize market, the government 
has failed to meet this intended objective, 
as it has always intervened in this market. 
These interventions in the maize subsector 
have been through the Farm Input Support 

ACF African Competition Forum
286
Programme (FISP) and the Food Reserve 
Agency (FRA), as a way of encouraging small 
scale farmers to increase productivity of maize 
– which is the staple food of Zambia.80 
73.	 Like any other commodity capable of being 
sold on a commercial basis, maize is subject to 
the Control of Good Act Cap 412 of the Laws 
of Zambia. Cap 412 provides for sweeping 
presidential powers with regards to trading in 
goods. It is an Act that enables the President 
to provide by regulation for the control of 
the distribution, disposal, purchase and sale, 
and the wholesale and retail prices of any 
manufactured or unmanufactured commodity 
or of any animal or poultry, or of any class of 
any such commodity, animal or poultry, for 
the control of imports into, and exports from 
Zambia, and for other purposes incidental 
and supplementary.
74.	 Besides the FISP program, Government 
activities in the sector are largely through 
the Food Reserve Agency (FRA). The Food 
Reserve Agency (FRA) was established by the 
Food Reserve Act Chapter 225 of the Laws 
of Zambia81 to ensure national food security, 
engage in market facilitation, and in the 
development and management of national 
storage facilities. This was done to ensure 
that designated agricultural commodities 
for the country are in good supply, thereby 
maintaining 
food 
security 
in 
case 
of 
emergencies.82 Through the Act, FRA is able 
to carry out a number of functions, such as 
the setting and buying of grain (mainly maize, 
which is Zambia’s main staple crop) from 
farmers. 
80	
 The National Agriculture Policy 2012-2030, (2011). 
81	
 http://www.parliament.gov.zm/sites/default/files/documents/acts/Food%20Reserve%20Act.pdf
82	
 http://fra.org.zm/about-us/who-we-are/   accessed on 8/11/2018

ACF African Competition Forum
287
75.	 Zambia has positioned itself in a fairly 
favourable position with respect to its 
regional trade policy. As a member of 
both the Southern African Development 
Community (SADC) and Common Market 
for Eastern and Southern Africa (COMESA) 
Free Trade Areas (FTAs), Zambia is one of 
the few countries whose exports enjoy duty-
free access to SADC and COMESA. At the 
same time, since SADC and COMESA are still 
Free Trade Agreements, and Zambia’s World 
Trade Organization (WTO) commitments are 
not particularly constraining, Zambia enjoys 
considerable freedom to adjust its extra-
regional tariffs and conduct its multilateral 
trade relationships as it sees fit. 
76.	 Fertiliser, crude edible oils, and frozen fish 
have remained Zambia’s largest imports from 
Africa and other regions. While strides have 
been made to reduce the deficits and make 
Zambia self-sufficient, the gap still remains. 
In addition to traditional exports such as raw 
cane sugar, oilcake, and maize seed, Zambia 
has seen industrial value-added products such 
as sweet biscuits and sugar confectioneries 
gaining prominence in Zambia’s export 
profile. This shows Zambia’s potential to value 
add and take advantage of its proximity to 
markets such as DR Congo and Zimbabwe. 
77.	 The fertiliser market has remained susceptible 
to cartel conduct at a time when Government 
has been increasing budgetary allocations 
to fertiliser procurement. This is largely due 
to the structure of the fertiliser market, which 
involves a huge percentage being procured 
through the Farmer Input Support Program, 
as well as the concentration and vertical 
integration of players in the sector – which 
make market allocation and cross sharing 
of information highly likely.  Competition 
concerns are also present in the sugar sector, 
which has seen high domestic sugar prices 
compared to regional peers, with a bearing 
on the sugar dependent industries such 
as confectionaries and others. There are 
also concerns of high animal feed prices, 
despite Zambia being largely self-sufficient 
in soybeans, and having had moderate 
investments in oil seed crushing and animal 
feed production. High animal feed prices 
entail high animal products, with the potential 
to render Zambian products uncompetitive 
on the export market. 
78.	 Regional markets equally appear to be 
affected by specific country markets, as seen 
with Zambia’s sugar exports. While Kenya 
and South Africa are relatively far markets 
compared to DR Congo, they enjoyed relatively 
lower prices than DR Congo, partly explained 
by the fact that Kenya and South Africa are 
competitive and mature markets while DR 
Congo is not, and exhibits a high demand for 
Zambian sugar. Therefore, margins may be 
extracted in near markets, to compensate for 
low margins in far and competitive markets. 
This also holds true with soya cake exports, 
with Zimbabwe a near market compared 
with exports to higher prices than Kenya and 
South Africa for Zambia-originating exports. 
While these are important competition issues 
and require markets to function properly, they 
require regional intervention. 
6
CONCLUSION

ACF African Competition Forum
288
Issue
Recommendations 
Trade Flow Analysis 
Zambia prominent imports include 
fertiliser, crude edible oils and fish, 
while exports include raw cane sugar, 
oilcake, maize seed, and other emerg-
ing industrial products such as sweet 
biscuits and sugar confectionaries. 
For Imports 
1.	
For crude edible oils, in addition to the promotion of 
oil seed growing, particularly soybeans, palm oils and 
other oil seed, crops such as sunflower and cotton must 
be supported, including through research, investment, 
and promotion. 
2.	
While fertiliser processing has seen growth of local 
capacity, reduction in the cost of imported fertiliser 
will require investments in transportation, particularly 
railway.    
For Exports 
1.	
Zambia puts in place an export-focused industrial 
policy, and invests in securing and protecting its 
proximity markets 
2.	
Support value addition through the creation of an 
enabling environment (energy, support infrastructure 
and labor policies) in addition to ensuring that anti-
competitive conduct does not unduly restrict and/or 
restrain the growth of the sector. 
Competition Analysis 
Sugar, fertiliser and animal feed sec-
tors, while having huge export poten-
tial, may be affected by anti-competi-
tive conduct including cartel conduct 
and unfair pricing. 
1.	
Empower institutions responsible for competition 
regulations, 
and 
promote 
effective 
competition 
regulation. In addition, and through policy measures, 
restructure some Government programs, such as FISP, 
in fertiliser and import restrictions on sugar, in order to 
induce competition disciplines in the markets. 
Pricing trends analysis
The general picture appears to be that 
of extracting higher margins in close 
markets with less competition, com-
pared to relatively far markets with 
competition. 
1.	
Regional competition promotion must be enhanced, 
to protect sub-regional markets with low internal 
competition in food markets, so as to lower their costs 
from imports.

ACF African Competition Forum
294
1.	
Zimbabwe’s 
National 
Development 
Strategy 
(NDS 
1, 
2021-2025) 
identifies 
agriculture as a key economic sector. The 
macroeconomic 
objective 
of 
the 
plan 
emphasises 
the 
acceleration 
of 
value 
addition and beneficiation in agriculture 
production, highlighting the importance of 
agro-processing in the food market. Boosting 
agriculture production and agro-processing 
has far-reaching benefits, including food and 
nutrition security, and increased income for 
various stakeholders in the agribusiness value 
chains.
2.	
Situated in Southern Africa, Zimbabwe boasts 
a thriving agricultural sector that plays a pivotal 
role in the nation’s economy. Agriculture has 
historically served as Zimbabwe’s economic 
backbone, providing substantial employment 
and contributing significantly to the GDP. 
The 
country’s 
agricultural 
landscape 
is 
characterised by the cultivation of crops such 
as tobacco, maize, cotton, and horticultural 
produce, 
alongside 
livestock 
farming. 
These products not only cater to domestic 
consumption but are also traded globally, 
underscoring the critical role of trade flows in 
sustaining Zimbabwe’s economic prosperity.
3.	
Conducting 
research 
on 
regional 
and 
continental 
value 
chains 
can 
prove 
instrumental in helping African economies 
seize opportunities for investment, growth, 
and price competitiveness in food markets 
and the agricultural inputs sector. This 
research aligns with broader efforts to 
support continental and regional integration 
initiatives. Many African countries, Zimbabwe 
included, have pursued structural reforms. 
They have opened up their economies to 
export-oriented policies driven by market 
dynamics and exemplified by agreements 
like the African Continental Free Trade Area 
(AfCFTA).
1
INTRODUCTION

ACF African Competition Forum
295
4.	
The focus of this study section on Zimbabwe 
is fourfold: 
i) 	 analysing 
trade 
flows 
of 
essential 
agricultural food products and agricultural 
inputs 
concerning 
Africa, 
regional 
economic communities (RECs), and key 
counterparts, 
ii)	 understanding competition issues or 
constraints related to traded agricultural 
food products and agricultural inputs,  
iii) conducting pricing trends analysis for the 
traded goods and 
iv) assessing the potential trade distortions 
in terms of tariff and non-tariff barriers at 
regional level.
5.	
For the methodology employed in this 
study, secondary data, and information 
gathered from Commission reports, including 
investigated cases and research studies, was 
utilised. The study concentrates on analysing 
the top and critical agricultural food products 
and agricultural inputs traded by Zimbabwe. 
The selected products for analysis include 
agricultural inputs (fertiliser, oilseed cake), 
vegetable oils (crude soybean oil), grains 
and legumes (maize, wheat, soybeans), 
protein foods (poultry), vegetables and fruits 
(citrus fruits), and other commodities (tea & 
sugar), as these represent commonly traded 
products among the countries participating in 
the study.

AND METHODOLOGY

6.	
This section delves into the examination of 
Zimbabwe’s trade dynamics in terms of both 
exports and imports of agricultural food 
products and agricultural inputs, considering 
interactions with Africa, regional economic 
communities (SADC & COMESA), and other 
significant partner countries. The analysis 
spans the periods before and after the onset 
of the COVID-19 pandemic, presenting the 
exchange of these goods in value terms, and 
providing insights into the balance of trade.
3.1.	 ZIMBABWE TRADE FLOWS AT THE 
AFRICAN LEVEL
7.	
In the last decade, Africa has consistently 
faced an average food trade deficit of 
approximately US$30 billion annually, despite 
the continent’s ample land availability and 
fertile soils.1 The significance of agricultural 
food product value chains and inputs cannot 
be overstated for African economies, serving 
as crucial sources of employment and poverty 
alleviation.2 The recent surge in food inflation3 
has heightened the importance of these 
sectors, prompting governments to explore 
strategies for achieving food self-sufficiency.
8.	
Between 2017 and 2022, Zimbabwe’s imports 
of agricultural food products from the rest 
of Africa exhibited fluctuations, with an 
overall upward trajectory. The figure 1 below 
illustrates this trend.
Figure 1: Zimbabwe imports of agricultural food products from Africa
Source: ITC Trade Map (2024)

9.	
Upon closer inspection of figure 1 above, a 
notable decline in agricultural food product 
imports occurred from 2018 to 2019, followed 
by a substantial surge leading into 2020. 
The steady rise from 2020 to 2022 can be 
attributed to the continued trading of essential 
goods, including food products, even during 
the peak of the COVID-19 pandemic.4 Figure 
2 below zooms in on critical agricultural food 
products such as wheat, maize, soybean 
oil, and agricultural inputs like fertiliser and 
oilcake, offering a more disaggregated view 
of Zimbabwe’s trade flows.

10.	 Of interest is the trend in maize imports, 
which is seen to show a sharp increase in 
the agricultural products imports from the 
period 2019 to 2020. Maize is a staple food 
in Zimbabwe, and its importation is prioritised 
during periods of shortages. Zimbabwe 
experienced adverse weather conditions 
during 
the 
2018/19 
cropping 
seasons, 
including rainfall deficits which resulted in a 
lower maize output of about 780 000 tonnes.5 
This prompted increased imports during the 
subsequent period.
Figure 2: Zimbabwe imports of specific food products and agricultural inputs from Africa
Source: ITC Trade Map (2024)
11.	 Among agricultural food products and 
agricultural inputs, soybean oil and fertiliser 
(ammonium nitrate & urea) emerged as crucial 
imports for Zimbabwe by 2022 respectively. 
The Zimbabwean government continues to 
invest heavily in agriculture, and this is leading 
to increased imports of agricultural inputs 
such as fertiliser. Furthermore, ammonium

298
nitrate fertiliser is commonly used in maize 
production in Zimbabwe. Zimbabwe’s largest 
import component in food products was 
maize in 2020, as seen above in figure 2.

assessment of the 5-year period indicates 
a general trend of slight increases in food

from 2020 to 2021, and a subsequent increase 
in exports in 2022.

indicates that Zimbabwe’s largest exports 
were sugar, tea, citrus fruits, and maize. 
Zimbabwe exported mainly tea and citrus 
fruits to South Africa, and maize to Zambia 
and Mozambique. The decline in exports in 
2020 to 2021, seen above in figure 3, can also 
be attributed to the huge decline in exports of 
sugar during this period. Zimbabwe exports 
 
of sugar have traditionally been directed to 
Botswana, Mozambique, and Kenya.

trade balance of agricultural food products 
with Africa from 2017 to 2022. The analysis 
shows a negative trade balance, indicating 
that Zimbabwe is importing more food 
products than it is exporting to the African 
region. This trade deficit has implications for 
Zimbabwe’s current account, and may lead to 
reduced foreign exchange income which can 
be used to finance the deficit.

9	
Zimbabwe has huge potential to replace imports, as it has phosphate rock deposits mined and extracted by Dorowa 
Minerals (Private) Limited - a 100% subsidiary of Zimbabwe Phosphates Industries Limited (Zimphos). The phosphate 
is then supplied to Zimphos to produce Single Super Phosphates (SSP) and Triple Super Phosphates (TSP). Ammonium 
nitrate (AN) has been manufactured by Sable Chemicals though the company has been struggling to meet local demand.
17.	 A more in-depth examination of the trade 
deficit in food products indicates a decline 
in the trade deficit from 2017 to 2019, and 
a subsequent increase in the trade deficit 
from 2020 to 2022. This suggests that 
Zimbabwe’s agriculture sector may not be 
meeting domestic demand and population 
growth adequately. This could be attributed 
to various factors, including inefficiencies and 
unfavorable weather conditions.7 Regarding 
competitiveness in the aggregate African 
market, the trade deficit may indicate that 
other countries might be able to produce and 
supply food products more efficiently and at a 
lower cost than Zimbabwe. 
18.	 This external dependence has been a concern 
for 
Zimbabwe’s 
policymakers, 
especially 
during times of global economic uncertainty. 
However, as nations in the AfCFTA aim 
at enhancing intra-Africa trade, there is 
an opportunity for countries to address 
shortfalls and trade more efficiently within the 
continent, particularly when African nations 
are not meeting the national requirements in 
terms of domestic production.8 
19.	 The ten products considered in this study, 
namely fertiliser, maize, wheat, soybean, 
oilcake, soybean oil, poultry, citrus fruit, sugar 
and tea, are each analysed in the subsections 
below. This section first considers the trade 
of these products at the continental (African 
level), cascading down to regional level, and 
then finally at the country level.
3.1.1.	 Fertiliser 
20.	 Zimbabwe, like other Sub-Saharan African 
countries, is largely dependent on fertiliser 
imports, although it has a fertiliser industry that 
consists of players at various stages of the value 
chain, namely mining, primary production9 

ACF African Competition Forum
301
and secondary production. In Zimbabwe, 
the three main fertiliser types used are the 
NPK granular compounds, the NPK blends, 
and top dressing (AN). Improving fertiliser 
production and application has not only been 
an agenda for Zimbabwe but for Africa as a 
region, which has been highlighted in the 
2003 Maputo Declaration initiative to raise 
agricultural investment, as well as the 2006 
Abuja Declaration on Fertiliser for an African 
Green Revolution, and the Comprehensive 
Africa Agriculture Development Programme.
21.	 Government 
input 
schemes 
such 
as 
Command Agriculture and Presidential input 
schemes are the major fertiliser consumers 
10	
Commission. 2018. A Study of the Zimbabwe Fertiliser Industry. Harare: Competition and Tariff Commission. 
in Zimbabwe. In 2018, the total estimated 
demand for fertiliser in Zimbabwe was about 
649  000 tonnes,10 while actual production 
was around 400  000 tonnes per year. The 
difference was thus supplemented by imports. 
The figure 6 below shows Zimbabwe’s import 
and export volumes in tons (primary axis) and 
the corresponding trade values (in US Dollar 
thousands on the secondary axis), in relation 
to its African trade. The figure 6 below shows 
that Zimbabwe imports more fertiliser from 
Africa than it exports, in volume and value 
terms, and that this trend for imports has 
shown an overall increase over the 2017 to 
2022 period.
Figure 6: Zimbabwe fertiliser trade with rest of Africa 2017 - 2022
Source: ITC Trade Map 2024
22.	 Zimbabwe imported most of its fertiliser from 
South Africa. South Africa supplied about 
56% of the fertiliser imports to Zimbabwe 
to meet its demand, especially from the 
government, to cater for its agricultural input 
scheme.  In 2021 the demand for imported 
fertiliser increased steeply, as the government 
intervened through the agriculture input 
scheme to enhance agricultural production 
and food security. Zimbabwe’s fertiliser cost 
of production is hindering its capacity to meet 
the local demand, due to the cost of ammonia, 
which doubled on the international market. 
This is due to the high natural gas prices 
after supplying countries imposed some 
restrictions on exports, driving local prices to 
high levels. Natural gas is a critical raw material 
for the production of fertiliser, and an increase 
in ammonia costs leads to pass-through price 
on the subsequent products that use this raw 

ACF African Competition Forum
302
material as an input, and thus a spike in prices. 
Zimbabwe has great potential in the fertiliser 
sector due to availability of some of the raw 
materials such as phosphates locally, and the 
potential gas available at Muzarabani.11
11	
World Oil. (2023). Invictus energy makes significant natural gas discovery onshore Zimbabwe. https://worldoil.com/
news/2023/12/7/invictus-energy-makes-significant-natural-gas-discovery-onshore-zimbabwe/

per tonne ($ per tonne) for imported and

during 2017 to 2022. 
Figure 7: Pricing trend for imported and exported fertilizer ($/t)

24.	 The price trend for imported fertiliser prices 
from Africa shows a general average rise in 
the price of fertiliser from $497 in 2017 to 
$593.52 in 2021. The average export prices 
are generally fluctuating, though showing 
an upward trend. Zimbabwe is mainly a net 
importer of fertiliser, and has insignificant 
exports, which explains the trend. 
25.	 In relation to market structure, the fertiliser 
production and distribution market structure 
analysis for 2016 indicated high concentration, 
with the top four players controlling 90% of 
the market, as highlighted in table 1 below. 
Concentration ratios (HHI and CR4) suggested 
a market which was highly concentrated, as of 
2016.

26.	 The market was oligopolistic in nature, as 
90% of the market was controlled by four 
large players. Notably, the international and 
Zambian fertiliser markets12 demonstrate 
considerable 
concentration. 
Furthermore, 
there are close links between ETG and FSG, 
due to ETG’s joint venture with SABIC from 
2022, and Meridian’s acquisition by Ma’aden 
in 2019. SABIC and Ma’aden, both owned 
by the Saudi Arabian state, have common 
shareholdings in fertiliser operations in Saudi 
Arabia 
(ibid). 
Government 
interventions 
via input schemes have an influence on a 
sizeable portion of the supply chain, and 
has an effect on the fertiliser market as a 
whole. Governmental influence on markets is 
manifested through direct involvement as a 
large purchaser of goods and services. Zambia, 
akin to Zimbabwe, features major suppliers 
playing pivotal roles in the fertiliser sector. 
These include: i) ETG/Zambian Fertilisers, 
holding approximately a 40% market share; 
ii) FSG/Meridian, owned by the Saudi Arabian 
company Ma’aden, also commanding roughly 
40% of the market; iii) Omnia, a South African 
producer specialising in ammonium nitrate 
and ammonium phosphate fertilisers; and 
iv) Yara, one of the largest global fertiliser 
producers with extensive operations across 
the African continent.13 
27.	 In 
relation 
to 
competition 
issues, 
the 
homogeneity of the product with inelastic 
demand made the market vulnerable to anti-
competitive practices, such as collusion and 
abuse of collective dominance.14 Barriers 
to entry and expansion were identified as 
capital, infrastructure, and regulation. Cross-
ownership among primary and secondary 
producers hindered innovation and efficiency 
in production systems. 
3.1.2.	 Maize
28.	 Maize is a staple food in Zimbabwe, and 
is prominent in Zimbabwe’s agricultural 
landscape. It is grown mainly for human and 
animal consumption, and is adaptable to 
various geographic locations. Zimbabwe has 
seen low levels of maize exports (see figure 
8 below) as it has been prioritising local 
self-sufficiency before it focuses on meeting 
international 
demand. 
The 
government 
announced the removal of import duties on 
maize in September 2023, with the aim to 

ACF African Competition Forum
304
help shore up domestic supplies, in view of 
the likely reduced harvest in 2024 (caused 
by the El-nino effect15 during the 2023/24 
agricultural season).16 The figure 8 below

30.	 The supply market for maize to Zimbabwe is 
dominated by South Africa, contributing about 
71% of the maize imported by Zimbabwe 
from Africa over the period. The Zimbabwean 
government values the importation of maize to 
supplement the domestic shortage, as maize 
is a staple food. In 2020, Zimbabwe imported 
more maize from Africa to supplement 
the national demand, as the country had 
experienced adverse weather conditions 
during the previous season, which limited 
domestic production.17 Zambia, on average, 
offered a competitive price per tonne, while 
South Africa (the major partner) offered less 
competitive prices than offered in Africa in 
general.  Mozambique has the highest price 
per tonne over the period.

31.	 The figure 9 below shows average price 
trends (dollar per tonne) for maize imports 
and exports in relation to Zimbabwe’s trade 
with the rest of the continent during 2017 
to 2022. Over the period, it can be seen that 
maize import prices increased sharply from 
2017 to 2018, and they started declining after 
2018. Zimbabwe has experienced adverse 
weather conditions such as El Nino over the 
years, including the 2015-2016 El Nino events 
which resulted in severe droughts significantly 
affecting agriculture, water resources and 
food security in Zimbabwe as crop yields 
were reduced. The price trend indicates 
convergence over the period with world 
commodity prices. The average export price 
has been mainly zero, given that Zimbabwe 
was mainly a net importer of maize during the 
period assessed.
Figure 9: Pricing trend for maize imports and exports ($/t)
Source: ITC Trade Map (2024); World Bank Commodities Prices18Data 2022
32.	 In terms of maize competition issues, in 2019 
the Grain Millers Association of Zimbabwe 
(GMAZ) sought the Commission’s approval for 
its Cost Review Technical Committee (CRTC), 
acknowledging 
its 
potentially 
collusive 
nature, but asserting its necessity for public 
and national interest. The Commission found 
that CRTC’s operations indeed restricted 

ACF African Competition Forum
306
competition by fostering information sharing 
among millers, resulting in uniform prices for 
staple foods like maize. The Commission’s 
investigations uncovered CRTC’s role as 
a hardcore cartel, negatively impacting 
consumers and hindering the competition 
process. The near-universal membership of 
millers in GMAZ intensified its monopolistic 
tendencies. 
33.	 Given these findings, the Commission deemed 
CRTC’s operations a restrictive practice 
against the public interest. It referred the case 
to the Commercial Crimes Division (CCD) for 
CCD to initiate prosecution processes. The 
immediate disbandment of CRTC and its 
operations was also advised.19 In 2019, due to 
SI 145 of 2019,20 the Grain Marketing Board 
(GMB) was the sole trader of maize, and this 
was amended through SI 56 of 2023,21 which 
has now added other authorised agencies to 
participate in the trade of maize. This move 
has also allowed farmers to benefit from 
receiving fair prices for their maize.22
3.1.3.	 Wheat
34.	 Wheat is the second most important cereal 
after maize in the food security basket in 
Zimbabwe, mainly because of the end 
product - bread. Bread has become a key 
staple food in the country and its demand 
has been rising, with the general populace 
switching consumption from traditional maize 
meal (sadza) to pasta and bread. Zimbabwean 
wheat is grown in winter under irrigation using 
electricity, making the crop costly to grow 
compared with other regions, where the crop 
is rainfed. Zimbabwe produced around 375 
000 tonnes of wheat in 2022, and the average 
annual wheat requirement for the country 
is estimated to be about 400 000 tonnes, 
19	
Commission. 2019. Report on Investigation into application for authorisation and condonation of the operations of 
GMAZ’s Cost Review Technical Committee.
20	
Grain Marketing (Control of Sale of Maize) Regulations, 2019 (S.I. 145 of 2019) provided for the control on the marketing 
of maize and declared maize to be a controlled product in terms of the Agricultural Marketing Authority Act.
21	
Grain Marketing (Control of Sale of Maize) (Amendment) Regulations, 2023 Statutory Instrument 56 of 2023 transitioned 
the sale of maize from a controlled market to market liberalisation.
22	
Herald. (2023, May 13). SI 56 will allow us to get maximum value of produce. The Herald. https://www.herald.co.zw/si-
56-will-allow-us-to-get-maximum-value-of-produce/ 
implying that Zimbabwe in recent times has 
been mostly self-sufficient.
35.	 In a bid to move towards self-sufficiency and 
reduce wheat imports, the Government made 
strides in 2016 to increase private sector 
participation in the production of wheat, 
through the command agriculture scheme. 
Private contractors have access to land 
through joint ventures with land beneficiaries 
from the land reform program, and rentals 
payable for the lease are a major cost element 
that makes the crop uncompetitive. Further to 
producing inadequate quantities of wheat, 
Zimbabwe produces mainly soft wheat, which 
is low in protein content. According to the 
bakers, the soft wheat is not suitable for quality 
bread production. The soft wheat is blended 
with hard wheat, which is usually imported, to 
meet the gristing qualities of flour demanded 
by bread bakeries. Imports of wheat are also 
there to cover periods of wheat shortages. 
The figure 10 below shows Zimbabwe’s wheat 
trade with the rest of Africa. It can be observed 
that Zimbabwe has been a net importer of 
wheat over the period reviewed.

generally lower compared to the average 
prices of wheat in Africa, pointing to potential 
competitiveness issues. The global supply 
market is competitive, with countries such 
as Poland, United Kingdom and Canada 
contributing 30% of the imported wheat to

wheat to Zimbabwe over the period. South

average, which is below the average prices for 
the Africa region. This is, however, still above 
the average for global prices. Mozambique 
has the highest price per tonne over the

39.	 The 
Russain-Ukraine 
war 
restricted 
importation from both countries, and caused a 
rise in wheat prices globally. The Zimbabwean 
government used to rely on wheat imports to 
balance low local production, but has found 
other means and implemented contract 
wheat farming to increase production, aimed 
at meeting the local demand and building 
strategic reserves.  
40.	 In relation to the market structure, wheat 
is mainly grown by contract growers, with 
some players vertically integrated. The main 
private players contracting farmers under 
the Command Agriculture Scheme include 
National Foods through affiliated company 
Paper Hole Investments (PHI), Northern 
Farming and Staywell. Wheat produced under 
private contract schemes accounts for at least 
70% of the national wheat production, though 
 
in terms of land usage, private contracted 
farmers occupy half of the land under wheat 
production. Private contractors’ yield per 
hectare is higher (at around 6 tonnes per 
hectare) than that of small-scale farmers (at 
around 4.5 tonnes per hectare).
41.	 Regarding competition issues, marketing of 
wheat was liberalised, allowing it to be traded 
on the open market. Despite deregulation, 
GMB remained the buyer of choice for 
many farmers, due to the Government’s 
announced prices. Higher producer prices 
set by Government are meant to incentivise 
farmers to venture into wheat farming. The 
producer price of wheat was increased from 
$500/tonne in 2017 to $630/tonne in 2018. 
Both contract and free farmers have therefore 
resorted to delivering all their wheat produce 
to the GMB. 

ACF African Competition Forum
309
42.	 GMB currently does not have a grading 
system for wheat, buying all the wheat at a flat 
fee despite differences in grain quality. The 
absence of a grading system disincentivises 
farmers to produce, maintain and deliver 
graded wheat. The GMB pricing system also 
does not recognise the differences in wheat 
protein content because of the uniform price. 
Due to the yield differences between hard 
and soft wheat, farmers tend to produce soft 
wheat, which yields 9 tonnes per hectare, 
relative to 6 tonnes per hectare for hard wheat.

3.1.5.	 Oilcake
49.	 Oilcake is primarily a by-product of crushing 
oilseeds such as soybean. Oilcake is crucial 
for the poultry production in Zimbabwe, as 
it is the main source of feed.30 Zimbabwe has 
witnessed a general decrease in production 
of oilseeds (i.e soybeans) over the years 
between 2000 to 2021, from about 140 793 
Mt in 2001 to 54 404 Mt in 2021,31 thus most 
of the oilcake shortages are covered through 
imported oilcake. The figure 14 below 
illustrates Zimbabwe’s reliance on imports for 
soybean, as its import volumes and values are 
significantly higher than its exports (which are 
relatively negligible), and this trend shows a 
general increase over the period.

the feed industry in Africa is producing more 
soybeans and could be failing to absorb the 
increased oilcake produced when crushing to 
get soybean oil, thereby causing the price to 
drop.

3.1.7.	 Poultry

shifted away from being a mainly beef-eating 
country to a poultry consuming country.

71.	 The HHI of 2068 reflects that the market is 
moderately concentrated, and the four top

72.	 In relation to competition concerns, two 
dominant breeds of chickens are used 
worldwide in the broiler production industry, 
namely the Ross breed supplied by Aviagen, 
and the Cobb breed supplied by Cobb-
Vantress of the United States of America.42 
Typically, the holder of the breeder license 
(intellectual property), in this case Aviagen 
or Cobb Vantress, sells grandparents to 
a distributor (usually through a franchise 
arrangement) who then breeds grandparents 
to supply day-old parent stocks to the market. 
73.	 Zimbabwe used to have two primary breeder 
licence holders for the production of the 
Cobb 500 and Hubbard breeds. Following 
the exit of Hubbard and Lunar Chickens, 
Zimbabwe currently has one primary breeder, 
Irvine’s Zimbabwe, with an exclusive breeding 
license for the Cobb 500 in Zimbabwe and for 
the rest of the African continent, except South 
Africa. The existence of exclusive breeding 
rights for the different breeds, which is an 
international practice, means the only way to 
enhance competition at this level is through 
attracting a second primary breeder in the 
country. Thus, at the primary breeding level, 
Zimbabwe currently has a monopoly. 
74.	 In 2018, the Commission initiated yet another 
investigation into the poultry value chain, which 
involved allegations of tying and bundling in 
42	
Ncube, Phumzile, Simon Roberts, and Tatenda Zengeni. “Development of the animal feed to poultry value chain across 
Botswana, South Africa, and Zimbabwe.” (2016).
43	
Commission. 2018. Report on Restrictive Business Practices in the distribution of Day-Old Chicks.

Citrus fruits are sub-tropical in origin, require wet warm summer favouring temperatures above 2 degrees Celsius, and can 
withstand temperatures above 30 degrees Celsius.

and Tariff Commission.
the sale of day-old chicks and poultry feed.43 
The allegation was that the buying of day-old 
chicks was conditional upon customers also 
buying stock feed. This practice is against 
the provisions in the Competition Act, and 
the Commission registered cease and desist 
orders which deterred firms from engaging in 
the practice in future.
3.1.8.	 Citrus Fruit
75.	 Zimbabwe has a well-established citrus fruit 
value chain due to its climatic44 conditions, 
which make it possible for the country to 
produce at a time when most countries are 
off-season for fruit production. Large volumes 
of fruits are produced for sale to the export 
market, as well as for the formal and informal 
local markets. The export market is served by 
the large-scale commercial farmers. 
76.	 According to the 2020 Zimbabwean Fruit and 
Vegetables sector standards for export and 
agro-processing, oranges make up about 
90% of Zimbabwe’s fruit exports. There are 
three major commercial citrus fruit producers 
in the local market, which include Nottingham 
and Bishopstone, both based in Beitbridge, 
and Interfresh, which is based in Mazowe. 
The two Beitbridge-based firms supply 
both Beitbridge Juicing Company and the 
South African market, as well as other export 
destinations.45 The ITC Trade Map data below, 
in figure 22 below, depicts Zimbabwe citrus 
fruit trade with the rest of Africa. As illustrated, 
Zimbabwe is a net exporter of citrus fruits.

start a commercial citrus growing business, 
making the minimum capital requirement 
close to US$5.5 million.46

3.1.9.	 Sugar

the sugar cane growers, whilst the secondary 
sugar industry are the millers. In relation to 
the primary sugar industry (the sugar cane 
growers), in Zimbabwe, sugar is produced 
almost exclusively from sugarcane. Sugarcane 
is grown under canal irrigation in the lowveld 
area of Triangle and Hippo Valley, in the 
Chiredzi District, Masvingo Province.  The

89.	 The HHI of 4,967.5, as shown in table 16 
above, indicates a highly concentrated market. 
Starafrica is the main competitor to THL in the 
white sugar category. Starafrica’s raw sugar 
supplier is THL, which affects the competition 
dynamics in the market. Starafrica’s prices 
are, to a larger extent, determined by the 
prices THL charges for raw sugar. THL can 
be hostile to SACL by increasing raw sugar 
prices exorbitantly, and could potentially take 
Starafrica out of the sugar market.
90.	 The Zimbabwe sugar processing value chain is 
largely monopolised by Tongaat Hulett. Inputs 
supplies, including fertilisers and chemicals, 

ACF African Competition Forum
327
are mainly imported. Water54 for irrigation is 
managed by Tongaat Hulett through its well-
established irrigation equipment. The sugar 
production mode significantly changed after 
the land reform programme, as land holding 
transformed from predominantly large-scale 
outgrowers and miller-cum-planter estates 
controlling about 75% of land, to around 56%, 
and small-scale private owners increasing 
from around 25% to around 44%. The 
transformation did not change much in terms 
of productivity, as the estates still contribute 
around 80% of sugar cane production, 
while the small-scale private owners only 
contribute around 20%. In other words, small-
scale growers’ productivity is too low as 
they lack the experience, technical expertise 
and funding to increase productivity. The 
estates’ continued dominance in sugar cane 
production may provide millers with more 
bargaining power to suppress and abuse 
growers.

sector as far back as December 2002, and 
54	
 Tongaat Hulett currently has water rights for Lake Mutirikwi.
55	
 Commission. 2019. State and Structure of Zimbabwean Economy: Harare: Competition and Tariff Commission.
submitted a report to the then Ministry of 
Industry and International Trade in 2004. 
Findings were that the sugar industry was 
highly concentrated and coordinated through 
Zimbabwe Sugar Sales (ZSS), to the detriment 
of the competition process. In its report, the 
Commission named the Zimbabwe Sugar 
Refineries Corporation Ltd (ZSR), Hippo Valley 
Estates Ltd (Hippo), ZSS, and Triangle Ltd 
(Triangle) as sugar industry kingpins, “involved 
directly or indirectly at all levels of production, 
from cane growing sugar refinement and 
distribution.” It highlighted how the lack of 
effective competition was cause for concern, 
since it stifled innovation and hindered 
efficient allocation of resources in the sugar 
industry. The Commission was concerned 
that the close linkages between production, 
processing and distribution companies made 
the market susceptible to abuse.55

96.	 South Africa has, over the period, been

to the variety of new and innovative ways of

of attempts to capture the attention and

locally.56

3.2.	 ZIMBABWE TRADE FLOWS AT THE 
REGIONAL LEVEL
100.	 Trade is pivotal to the economic development 
of regional economic communities such as 
COMESA and SADC, to which Zimbabwe 
belongs, and can foster regional integration. 
Regional integration is fundamental to 
developing countries such as Zimbabwe, 
to achieve economic development and 
growth through facilitating trade between 
developing countries.
101.	 Recognising the importance of regional 
economic communities, there is need 
to 
understand 
trade 
performance 
for 
Zimbabwe in the SADC and COMESA 
regions. Zimbabwe imports of agricultural 
food products59 from SADC indicate a similar 
trend to Zimbabwe imports from Africa, 
indicating that, even considering the whole 
of Africa, a larger percentage of Zimbabwe’s 
trade has been within the SADC region.
102.	 Zimbabwe’s largest imports of agricultural 
food products from SADC were soybean 
oil, wheat and maize, while in terms of 
agricultural inputs, fertiliser was the main 
agricultural input from SADC, as highlighted 
in the figure 28 below. The figure 28 below 
shows imports from SADC on the left, and 
imports from COMESA on the right. Maize 
is the staple food in Zimbabwe, thus, it can 
be seen that a similar trend can also be 
observed, as explained above in Zimbabwe’s 
trade in Africa. This trend being that the 
59	
ITC Trade Map. 2024.

FAO. 2019. GIEWS Special Alert: Available at: https://www.fao.org/3/ca3387EN/ca3387en.pdf.
value imported from the SADC region sees

103.	 Zimbabwe’s imports of agricultural food 
products from COMESA (as shown on the

worth 
of

104.	 A number of factors may have contributed 
to the increase in reliance of food imports 
from COMESA over the period, which 
include unfavorable weather conditions 
as well as increasing demand for food due 
to a rising population.60 Zimbabwe’s top 3 
imports from COMESA, seen in the figure 28 
below, among the selected products include 
fertiliser, oilcake and maize. Zimbabwe is 
an agriculture-based economy, and the 
availability of fertiliser is critical for the 
development of this strategic sector, which 
underpins the country’s food security. 
Fertiliser, which shows a marked increase in 
COMESA imports from 2020 onwards, was 
imported from Mauritius and Mozambique, 
while maize and oilcake was imported from 
South Africa and Zambia.

Safeguards is a temporary import restriction (for example a tariff increase or a quota) that the country can impose 
on a product if increased imports, of that product, are causing, or threatening to cause serious injury to a country’s 
(Zimbabwean) industry.
62	
Annandale, D. L. 2022. The African Continental Free Trade Area (AfCFTA): Opportunity or pipedream for South Africa’s

105.	 Zimbabwe’s exports to SADC were mainly 
citrus fruits (largely oranges), sugar and tea. 
Zimbabwe exported the oranges and tea 
mainly to South Africa. Zimbabwe has been 
exporting less food products because there 
is still a need to meet the national demand of 
a growing population, which means there are 
then less products available for export. Sugar 
has been Zimbabwe’s main export product 
into the COMESA region. The decline in 
exports of food products into COMESA can 
also be largely explained by a significant 
decline in sugar exports from Zimbabwe 
into the region, due to safeguards.61 The 
other main exports from Zimbabwe into the 
COMESA region were tea and maize.
106.	 An analysis of trade in regional economic 
communities indicates that Zimbabwe trades 
more with the SADC region as compared to 
the COMESA region. Zimbabwe’s imports 
from SADC in 2022 were about US$793m, 
while its imports from the COMESA region 
were much lower at US$173m, which is 
around 4 times less than the country’s trade 
with SADC. A study showed that South 
Africa’s 
regional 
orientation 
regarding 
exports of selected agricultural products 
has 
generally 
been 
strongly 
oriented 
towards the SADC region.62 South Africa is 
a neighboring country to Zimbabwe, and 
is Zimbabwe’s largest trading partner. The 
fact that Zimbabwe’s trade is also strongly 
oriented to SADC means that Zimbabwe can 
possibly experience difficulties in exploiting 
market opportunities in other RECs in Africa, 
despite tariff reductions, and this points to 
another opportunity for both Zimbabwe and 
South Africa to look for more trade markets 
beyond the SADC region.

Zimbabwe, and this has been part of a raft 
of measures aimed at mitigating the effects 
of the El-Nino-induced drought, which 
guaranteed national food security.

3.3.	 ZIMBABWE TRADE FLOWS AT THE 
COUNTRY LEVEL
146.	 This section considers Zimbabwe’s trade 
flows at individual country level, focusing 
on SADC countries and mainly South Africa, 
in respect to being Zimbabwe’s top trading 
partner for most products being analysed 
in this study. This section therefore provides 
more detail on products under study in 
relation to Zimbabwe’s value chain, its source 
of imports and average prices, market 
structure, and competition concerns that are 
arising from internal and external influences 
at the country level of trade.
147.	 Zimbabwe’s imports from South Africa 
were mainly related to maize, fertiliser and 
soybean oil. Zimbabwe has been importing 
more agricultural input products, because 
there is still a need to meet national demand 
of the agricultural input scheme and secure 
food security for Zimbabwe. Figure 59 below 
indicates the decline in agriculture inputs 
such as fertiliser in 2020, as result of high 
prices being driven up by natural gas price 
increases, which subsequently raised the 
cost of nitrogen production. This was then 
followed by the impact of Russia’s invasion 
of Ukraine in 2020 (with Russia being a major 
producer of Nitrogen), which then further 
increased fertiliser prices. In addressing the 
issues and reducing the adverse impact of 
weather conditions like El-Nino, Zimbabwe 
waived the duty on 250 000 tonnes of 
fertiliser in 2022, and further suspended 
import tax on fertiliser in 2023. 
Figure 59: Zimbabwe’s imports of specific agriculture food products and agricultural inputs from South 
Africa
Source: ITC Trade Map (2024)
148.	 Zimbabwe’s exports to South Africa were 
mainly tea, citrus and sugar. Zimbabwe has 
been exporting less food products, because 
there is still a need to meet the national 
demands of a growing population, so there 
have consequently been less products 
available for export. 

ACF African Competition Forum
356
Figure 60: Zimbabwe’s exports of specific agriculture food products and agricultural inputs to South 
Africa
Source: ITC Trade Map (2024)
76	
https://allafrica.com/stories/202008270830.html#:~:text=Last%20year%20international%20tea%20prices,matched%20
by%20corresponding%20world%20demand. 
149.	 In 2020, Zimbabwe exported less tea to South 
Africa as pandemic-related disruptions to 
logistics affected tea trade, and international 
tea prices weakened due to the increased 
supply of tea by Kenya, which was not 
matched by corresponding world demand.76 
For citrus, in 2021 Zimbabwe exported 
more citrus to South Africa because of the 
Covid-19 pandemic, which resulted in a 
surge in demand for citrus fruits due to their 
perceived immune-boosting benefits.
3.4.	 REGULATORY ENVIRONMENT, 
AUTHORITIES AND MAJOR AGRICULTURAL 
FOOD LAWS
150.	 	In 
relation 
to 
import 
and 
export 
requirements, given that the focus of this 
study is on agricultural food products, 
Zimbabwe has food and agricultural import 
and export regulations and standards 
that are developed, administered, and 
implemented by the MLAFWRD, Ministry of 
Health and Child Care, Ministry of Industry 
and Commerce, Ministry of Environment, 
Climate, Tourism and Hospitality, and the 
Ministry of Higher & Tertiary Education, 
Science 
and 
Technology 
Development 
(MHTESTD). These ministries also appoint 
independent agencies to undertake specific 
regulatory, certification and implementation 
functions. Due to the various ministries and 
agencies involved in regulating agricultural 
and food products, imported and exported 
products may be subject to multiple 
regulations under the various ministries.

ACF African Competition Forum
357
151.	 The ACF research into Zimbabwe’s regional 
agricultural trade has analysed: i) trade flows 
of essential food products and agricultural 
inputs in Africa and regional economic 
communities ii) competition issues and 
constraints related to traded food products 
and agricultural inputs, and iii) market 
structure and pricing analyses for traded 
goods and pricing trends over time.
4.1.	 TRADE FLOWS AND ANALYSIS
152.	 Zimbabwe faces a negative trade balance, 
importing 
more 
food 
products 
and 
agricultural inputs than it exports to Africa, 
potentially impacting the current account 
and foreign exchange reserves. Zimbabwe’s 
imports from Africa show fluctuations in 
imports between 2017 and 2022, with an 
overall upward trajectory over the period. 
There was a notable decline of imports in 
2018-2019, followed by a surge in 2020, 
attributed to continued trading during the 
COVID-19 pandemic. Zimbabwe’s exports 
to Africa indicated a slight decline in food 
exports over the 5-year period, with the main 
exported products being sugar, tea and 
citrus fruits.
4.2.	 REGIONAL TRADE ANALYSIS
153.	 In 
relation 
to 
Zimbabwe’s 
regional 
orientation, the trade data shows that its 
trade of selected agricultural products is 
strongly oriented towards the SADC region. 
Key imports include soybean oil, wheat, 
maize, and fertiliser. In Zimbabwe’s trade with 
COMESA, the decline in exports to COMESA 
was mainly due to reduced sugar exports. 
154.	 Zimbabwe’s key imports from COMESA 
include 
fertiliser, 
oilcake, 
and 
maize 
amongst the products analysed in this study. 
South Africa is a key trading partner for 
Zimbabwe, indicating potential challenges 
in exploring market opportunities in other 
African regions despite tariff reductions. The 
enactment of the AfCFTA will not provide 
a silver bullet in regard to expanding trade 
of food products and agricultural products 
into and from Africa, at least not in the short 
to medium term. Sectors which are export 
oriented, such as the citrus and tea sectors, 
have competition concerns, largely related 
to their international trade constraints.
4.3.	 GOVERNMENT INTERVENTION
155.	 Trade 
restrictions, 
through 
instituting 
safeguard measures or bans, has an 
implication on Africa realising improved intra-
Africa trade. Government participation as 
buyers of agricultural, inputs as an example, 
has implications on market concentration 
of agricultural sectors, as well as efficient 
functioning of markets.
4.4.	 MULTINATIONAL FIRM LINKAGES
156.	 Regional 
commodity 
traders 
and 
multinational corporations have effectively 
harnessed the benefits of economies of 
scale and bulk procurement of raw materials, 
leveraging their significant buying power to 
source products. This strategic approach not 
only enables them to dominate the regional 
market, but also enhances their efficiency 
in procurement, distribution, and overall 
supply chain operations. By capitalising 
on economies of scale, these entities can 
achieve cost efficiencies, streamline their 
4
INSIGHTS AND OBSERVATIONS     
FROM TRADE FLOW DATA

ACF African Competition Forum
358
operations, and exert substantial influence 
over the sourcing and distribution of 
commodities. 
Additionally, 
their 
adept 
utilisation of bulk procurement provides 
them with a competitive edge, allowing for 
strategic negotiations with suppliers, and 
facilitating seamless regional dominance in 
the trade of various products.
4.5.	 REMARKS ON FINDINGS: 
157.	 The study highlights and recommends the 
following:
•	
Competition Authorities should continue 
advocacy initiatives on the impact of 
government intervention in the development 
of agricultural markets.
•	
Promoting 
increased 
intra-Africa 
trade 
requires encouraging trade in key value 
chains where Africa has supportive climatic 
conditions, such as the soybean value chain, 
as it has multiple linkages in other sectors.
•	
Promoting regional integration efforts in 
the AfCFTA may also require significant 
investment in transport systems such as rail, 
to ensure that logistical costs are reduced 
significantly.
•	
In 
terms 
of 
enhancing 
agricultural 
productivity to achieve food self-sufficiency, 
Zimbabwe needs to heavily invest in high-
precision irrigation systems, to diminish 
effects of climate change.
