360
PART G
OVERVIEW OF 
MAIN FINDINGS

ACF African Competition Forum
361
1.	
	Agriculture remains the backbone of most 
African economies, playing a pivotal role 
in food security, employment, and gross 
domestic 
product 
(GDP) 
contribution. 
The focus on agricultural value chains, 
particularly for key, highly traded products, 
is driven by their economic significance, and 
their potential to enhance regional trade and 
economic resilience. Further, agricultural 
and 
agro-processing 
value 
chains 
are 
important, given the current high food 
inflation environment that continues to place 
pressure on consumers. 
2.	
	The agriculture sector faces numerous 
challenges, 
including 
climate 
change, 
market access barriers, and anti-competitive 
practices. These issues not only impede the 
optimal functioning of markets, but also 
affect the livelihoods of millions who depend 
on agriculture. In addition, supply shocks like 
the Covid-19 pandemic and the Russian-
Ukraine war, combined with climate change 
and high food inflation, have highlighted the 
African food systems’ vulnerability, and the 
need for greater resilience and food security. 
Emphasising regional and continental value 
chains allows African competition authorities 
to collectively conduct research and enforce 
policies 
that 
can 
enhance 
investment 
opportunities, economic growth, and price 
competitiveness within the framework of 
continental and regional integration efforts.
3.	
	The study of trade flows and regional value 
chains in key agricultural sectors across 
six African countries – Kenya, Mauritius, 
South Africa, 
Zambia, 
Zimbabwe, 
and 
Angola – provides critical insights into the 
economic interdependencies, competitive 
dynamics, and structural challenges within 
the continent. The research aims to elucidate 
trade flows and competition issues that 
shape the agricultural and agro-processing 
sectors. By examining the trade relationships 
(including 
large 
and 
small 
intra-Africa 
trade volumes) and regulatory landscapes 
governing these interactions, the study 
seeks to foster a deeper understanding 
of how regional integration and market 
structures influence economic outcomes 
and agricultural frameworks.
4.	
	By 
integrating 
the 
insights 
from 
six 
diverse but interconnected markets, this 
study aims to contribute to the discourse 
on regional economic integration and 
agricultural development in Africa. The 
findings underscore the importance of 
fostering competitive markets, reducing 
trade barriers, and enhancing regional 
collaboration to achieve sustainable growth 
and food security. Through this study, the 
African Competition Forum seeks to inform 
industry stakeholders, and researchers about 
the intricate dynamics of agricultural trade at 
different geographic levels, and the critical 
measures needed to optimise the benefits of 
regional integration and enhanced regional 
trade in agriculture. Further, the study shows 
the potential for developing and expanding 
smaller intra-African trade volumes further 
within the continent. 
5.	
	The 
objective 
of 
this 
chapter 
is 
to 
identify trends and trade linkages across 
the 
countries, 
challenges 
or 
barriers 
to improving regional trade, the main 
competition issues in agriculture and agro-
processing markets, and to offer key insights 
1
OVERVIEW    
OF MAIN FINDINGS

ACF African Competition Forum
362
towards opportunities for improving regional 
agricultural trade in Africa, as well as future 
research avenues that could be considered, 
going forward. 
1.1.	 ANALYSIS OF AGRICULTURAL TRADE 
ACROSS THE AFRICAN COUNTRIES
6.	
In terms of trade patterns across the 
countries, the discussion below outlines the 
key aspects for each country’s agricultural 
trade, after which a summary of trends and 
challenges is provided. 
7.	
In respect of Zimbabwe, the main agricultural 
exports include sugar, tea, citrus fruits and 
tobacco. Key import commodities include 
fertiliser, maize, wheat, and soybean oil. 
Zimbabwe’s main trade partners include 
neighbouring countries like South Africa, 
Mozambique 
and 
Zambia. 
Zimbabwe 
faces a number of trade challenges, 
such as inadequate infrastructure, high 
transportation costs, and complex regulatory 
frameworks which impede trade efficiency. 
The country’s trade dynamics reflect strong 
linkages to regional partners, for both 
agricultural exports and imports. The trade 
relationships within SADC and COMESA are 
crucial for meeting domestic food security 
needs and supporting the agricultural 
sector's growth.
8.	
In terms of Kenya’s trade, the country plays 
a pivotal role in East African trade, with a 
diversified agricultural export portfolio. The 
country’s major exports include tea, coffee, 
horticultural products, as well as floriculture. 
Kenya’s agricultural exports have grown 
significantly, driven mainly by demand in 
European and Asian markets. The country 
imports substantial quantities of maize, rice 
and wheat, primarily from regional partners, 
and some international markets. While 
Kenya benefits from relatively advanced 
infrastructure 
and 
logistics 
networks, 
challenges remain in reducing non-tariff 
barriers and improving regional trade 
integration. 
9.	
With 
regards 
to 
agricultural 
trade 
in 
Mauritius, despite its small size, the country 
is a key player in regional trade, particularly 
in the agro-processing sector. Mauritius 
exports a variety of processed food products, 
as well as sugar and seafood products. Its 
export destinations are diversified, with 
significant shipments to Europe, Africa and 
Asia. Mauritius imports food products, raw 
materials and agricultural inputs to support 
its processing industries. The country’s key 
import partners include India, South Africa, 
and China. Mauritius focuses on enhancing 
value addition, and leveraging its strategic 
location to boost re-exports and facilitate 
trade. It does, however, face significant 
trade challenges in achieving self-sufficiency 
on basic food commodities, due to its 
dependence on imports for most agricultural 
inputs and food products. 
10.	 Zambia’s trade profile reveals a strong 
reliance on agricultural exports within 
the Southern African region. Its major 
agricultural exports include raw cane sugar, 
oilcake, maize and soybeans. The key export 
markets for Zambia include the Democratic 
Republic of Congo, Zimbabwe, and South 
Africa. Zambia imports fertilisers, crude oil 
and processed foods. There is a notable 
deficit in edible oils, with imports meeting 
a significant portion of domestic demand. 
Zambia, despite being self-sufficient in many 
crops like maize and sugar, faces high trade 
barriers and transport costs that limit its trade 
efficiency at the African level. While Zambia 
generally maintains a positive trade balance 
in 
agricultural 
products, 
the 
country’s 
infrastructure and logistical challenges pose 
barriers to further growth in agricultural 
trade.
11.	 In terms of agricultural trade in South 
Africa, the country serves as a major hub 
for trade within the African continent, with 
well-developed 
agricultural 
and 
agro-
processing sectors. The country exports 
a wide range of agricultural products, 
including citrus fruits, oilseed cake, crude 

ACF African Competition Forum
363
sunflower oil, and maize. The export 
markets are diverse, encompassing Africa, 
Europe and Asia. South Africa’s key imports 
include fertilisers, crude sunflower oil and 
seeds, which are crucial for supporting its 
large-scale 
agricultural 
production 
and 
processing industries. South Africa's key 
export partners in the rest of Africa include 
Namibia, Mozambique, Botswana, Eswatini, 
and Lesotho, particularly for products like 
maize, sugar, coffee, and tea, while it imports 
significant 
agricultural 
products 
from 
Eswatini, Zambia, Mozambique, Malawi, 
and Mauritius. The identified challenges 
to South Africa’s agricultural trade include 
infrastructure constraints, market access 
challenges, climate-related risks, as well as 
factors like high tariff and non-tariff barriers, 
inadequate transportation networks and 
extreme weather events, which increase 
costs and reduce competitiveness, thus 
hindering trade development and growth.
12.	 For Angola, the country represents vast 
agricultural potential with significant arable 
land, and an agricultural sector that is 
predominantly driven by family farming, 
with significant underutilisation in business 
farming. The country’s trade in agriculture 
relies on imports of essential agricultural 
inputs 
and 
food 
products, 
including 
fertilisers, seeds, and sugar. Angola’s primary 
exports include coffee, with other exports 
like tea and corn (maize) being exported 
at lower volumes. Angola's key trading 
partners for food exports like sugar and 
potatoes in Africa include the Democratic 
Republic of Congo, Guinea, and Namibia, 
among others. For food imports such as 
corn, Angola’s dominant partner is South 
Africa, with additional contributions for food 
imports from countries like Zambia, Namibia 
and Kenya.
13.	 The country faces several challenges in 
agricultural trade, including inadequate 
infrastructure, 
a 
high 
dependence 
on 
rain, and regulatory barriers such as 
stringent sanitary and phytosanitary (SPS) 
requirements, as well as a dependency 
on imports for critical agricultural inputs, 
which can constrain agricultural productivity. 
Angola does, however, have vast agricultural 
potential, having around 35 million hectares 
of arable land, with only around 17% 
currently cultivated. 
14.	 When analysing agriculture trade flows across 
the participating member countries, several 
commonalities are revealed in the traded 
products. These commonalities highlight 
overarching product-specific insights and 
regional economic patterns, as well as a 
shared reliance on specific commodities. 
These common, product-specific aspects 
are outlined briefly below, with reference to 
key, highly traded agricultural commodities, 
namely maize, sugar, coffee, tea, processed 
foods and agro-processed products, as well 
as fertilisers and agricultural inputs.
14.1.	The trade of maize (a key staple food) 
is prevalent within the Southern African 
region, and across all the participating 
members, 
with 
countries 
like 
Zambia 
and South Africa exporting maize to their 
neighbours, while Kenya, Angola, Mauritius 
and Zimbabwe generally import maize 
to meet domestic demand. Kenya has 
increasingly imported maize from South 
Africa and Uganda, to address its domestic 
production deficits, with annual import 
volumes rising significantly over recent 
years. Average prices and volumes for maize 
trade have fluctuated over time, influenced 
by regional production levels and global 
market conditions, where both maize export 
and import volumes have shown an overall 
increasing trend across the participating 
countries. 
14.2.	The 
sugar 
sector 
is 
important 
for 
employment and economic stability for 
many African countries, notably Zimbabwe, 
Zambia, Kenya and South Africa, who have 
substantial sugar production industries. 
Kenya, despite being a producer, is also 

ACF African Competition Forum
364
a significant importer of sugar, sourcing 
primarily from COMESA countries, including 
Uganda, Mauritius and Zimbabwe. In 2022, 
Zambia exported $89.9 million in raw cane 
sugar, making it a larger exporter of sugar 
in Africa. The Zambian sugar industry is 
dominated by a few major players, such as 
Zambia Sugar, which cater to both domestic 
and regional markets. In South Africa, sugar 
production is significant, but the market is 
also characterised by high concentration 
and limited competition, impacting pricing 
and market accessibility.
14.3.	Coffee is a vital crop for developing 
countries, 
providing 
foreign 
exchange 
earnings and an income for farmers, as it 
is one of the few crop products that can be 
stored for relatively long periods without 
perishing.1 Coffee trade has generally 
shown an increasing trend in both export 
and import volumes over the available 
years. Kenya is a notable exporter of coffee, 
while other African countries such as South 
Africa and Mauritius, although not major 
producers, play important roles in the coffee 
trade in Africa, focusing more on coffee 
imports. Coffee production in Kenya holds a 
significant position, with a well-established 
market structure (from smallholder farmers 
to exporters) and competitive pricing. 
14.4.	Tea, similar to coffee, generates substantial 
foreign exchange revenue for developing 
countries, and provides employment for 
millions of people in these countries – 
including farmers, plantation workers, and 
those involved in processing and distribution. 
The tea industry in Kenya is well-structured, 
with 
major 
companies 
dominating 
production and export processes. However, 
both industries face challenges related to 
global market fluctuations, and competition 
from other major producers. In comparison, 
the other countries in the study have less 
1	
While coffee is not exclusively a developing country crop, its economic and social significance is more pronounced in 
these regions. Developed countries, such as the EU and the US, are significant consumers and importers of coffee, but 
they do not rely on coffee production as a primary source of income and employment.
significant coffee and tea sectors, focusing 
more on other agricultural commodities.
14.5.	In terms of processed foods and agro-
processed products, countries like Mauritius, 
South Africa and Kenya have significant 
agro-processing industries. These countries 
export processed food products while also 
importing raw materials for their agro-
processing sectors. The focus on adding 
value through processing is a common 
strategy to increase export revenues, and 
reduce reliance on raw commodity exports.  
14.6.	All the countries in the study import 
fertilisers and other agricultural inputs, to 
support their agricultural sectors. Access 
to these inputs is critical for improving 
agricultural productivity and ensuring food 
security. Fertilisers and other inputs are 
often traded within the region, reflecting 
the interconnectedness of the agricultural 
supply chains. Agricultural inputs are further 
discussed below. 
15.	 While the points above cover the product-
specific insights and commonalities, the 
traded 
products 
across 
Kenya, 
South 
Africa, Zimbabwe, Zambia, Mauritius, and 
Angola also exhibit notable divergences 
and different trade dynamics, reflecting 
each country's unique economic strategies, 
resource 
endowments, 
and 
market 
demands. 
Agricultural 
contributions 
to 
exports are based on a limited number of 
products, while other countries like South 
Africa, are reliant on several products for 
export.  South Africa, for example, has several 
products in its agricultural export portfolio, 
which include maize, sugar, citrus fruits, 
and sunflower oil, while other countries, like 
Kenya and Zimbabwe, primarily focus on tea 
and coffee exports. Kenya's tea and coffee 
sectors are critical to its economy, driving 
significant foreign exchange earnings and 

ACF African Competition Forum
365
employment. Zimbabwe, while also a key 
exporter of tea and coffee, faces structural 
challenges such as non-tariff barriers and 
inefficient infrastructure, that impede its 
trade efficiency.
16.	 Zambia's trade profile is heavily influenced 
by its maize production, positioning it as a 
significant supplier within the region. The 
country's ability to export maize is crucial for 
addressing food security in neighbouring 
countries. 
However, 
Zambia's 
reliance 
on imports for key agricultural inputs like 
fertiliser points to its vulnerability to external 
supply chain disruptions. Mauritius, despite 
its limited agricultural base, plays a strategic 
role in the regional sugar trade, both 
importing and exporting sugar to balance its 
domestic needs and economic goals.
17.	 Angola presents a trade dynamic of a 
combination 
of 
imports 
and 
exports 
influenced 
by 
infrastructure, 
economic 
strategies and regional trade relations. 
The country's trade is characterised by 
substantial imports of agricultural products 
to meet domestic demand, highlighting the 
ongoing efforts to develop its agricultural 
capacity. An interesting aspect of Angola's 
trade is its potential for growth in agro-
processing, which remains underexploited 
compared to other countries in the region. 
Despite its vast arable land and favourable 
natural conditions, the country imports 
a large amount of its agricultural inputs, 
spending around $26.10 million annually 
on fertilisers, seeds, and agrochemicals. 
This reliance on imports presents both a 
challenge and an opportunity for Angola to 
develop its domestic production capabilities, 
and reduce dependency on foreign inputs.
18.	 Noteworthy factors affecting these countries' 
trade include the impact of unique global 
events such as the COVID-19 pandemic 
and the Russia-Ukraine conflict. These 
events have caused significant disruptions 
in supply chains, affecting trade volumes 
and commodity prices. For instance, the 
pandemic led to fluctuating trade volumes 
and prices in South Africa's sunflower oil 
market, while the rising cost of fertiliser, 
exacerbated by the Ukraine conflict, has 
impacted 
agricultural 
production 
costs 
across the region. Furthermore, non-tariff 
barriers, such as sanitary and phytosanitary 
measures, 
pose 
significant 
challenges, 
particularly for countries like Zimbabwe and 
Zambia, which struggle with inadequate 
infrastructure and regulatory inefficiencies.
19.	 The diverse trade profiles of Kenya, South 
Africa, Zimbabwe, Zambia, Mauritius, and 
Angola reflect their distinct economic 
contexts and challenges. While shared 
regional trade in agricultural products is 
evident, and there are common features 
across key traded products, there is also 
the reality that each country’s unique factors 
(from infrastructure and regulatory issues to 
the impacts of global events) play a critical 
role in shaping their trade dynamics and 
economic resilience.
20.	 Further to the above, the ACF study has 
also pointed to key findings in relation to 
agricultural trade at different geographic 
levels, including aspects of global trade, 
intra-African trade, regional trade, and trade 
at the individual country level.
21.	 At the global level, South Africa emerges 
as a significant player due to its role as a 
net importer of essential agricultural inputs 
like fertilisers, while also serving as an 
exporter of key agricultural products such 
as maize, fertiliser, citrus fruits, and poultry. 
This dual role highlights South Africa’s 
strategic importance in connecting other 
African markets with global supply chains. 
The global market dynamics, influenced by 
events such as the COVID-19 pandemic and 
the Russia-Ukraine conflict, have highlighted 
the interconnectedness of global supply 
chains, and the role South Africa plays in 
maintaining regional food security.

ACF African Competition Forum
366
22.	 The study shows that there are positive 
developments related to intra-trade within 
the various trade blocs on the continent. 
This underscores the likely benefits that 
may arise from the implementation of the 
African Continental Free Trade Agreement, 
particularly if focus is given to the removal 
of restrictions to trade, and an enabling 
infrastructural and regulatory environment 
is put in place to facilitate increased trade. 
Regarding 
intra-African 
or 
continental 
trade, the trade patterns reveal significant 
interdependencies. South Africa is a central 
hub in the Southern African Development 
Community (SADC), exporting essential 
crops like maize, and supplying agricultural 
inputs including seeds and fertilisers to 
neighbouring 
countries. 
Zimbabwe's 
agricultural trade is oriented towards the 
SADC region, with key imports like maize 
and fertiliser primarily sourced from South 
Africa. 
23.	 At the regional level of trade, agricultural 
trade within regional economic communities 
such as SADC and COMESA show varied 
dynamics. For example, Mauritius imports 
significant quantities of sugar from South 
Africa and Zambia, which it processes and 
re-exports, leveraging its strategic position 
within the regional sugar market. Similarly, 
Kenya's participation in the East African 
Community (EAC) facilitates the import of 
essential commodities like maize and sugar, 
addressing domestic supply deficits and 
highlighting the critical role of regional trade 
agreements.
24.	 The trade dynamics within the East African 
Community (EAC), the Southern African 
Development Community (SADC) and the 
Common Market for Eastern and Southern 
Africa (COMESA), highlight the critical role 
of regional trade agreements in facilitating 
agricultural trade. Kenya, for instance, has 
leveraged its membership in these regional 
blocs to import essential commodities 
like maize and sugar, thereby addressing 
domestic supply deficits. Similarly, Mauritius 
and Zambia have positioned themselves 
as key exporters of sugar, benefiting from 
preferential market access under COMESA. 
South Africa's diversified agricultural trade 
portfolio underscores its role as a major 
trading partner within the Southern African 
Development Community (SADC), while 
Zimbabwe and Angola continue to navigate 
the complexities of regional trade to bolster 
their agricultural sectors.
25.	 In terms of trade at the individual country 
level, unique trade dynamics are observed. 
South Africa’s diversified agricultural export 
portfolio includes maize, citrus fruits, and 
sugar, which it exports to various African 
countries to address food security needs 
and stabilise markets. Zimbabwe exports 
products such as tea, citrus, and sugar 
primarily within Africa, though it faces 
challenges due to structural inefficiencies 
and adverse weather conditions that impact 
production. Angola, with a recovering and 
growing agricultural sector, relies heavily 
on imports from South Africa and Zambia 
to meet domestic demand, highlighting the 
ongoing efforts to rebuild its agricultural 
capacity.
26.	 The trade patterns across these countries 
highlight 
the 
importance 
of 
regional 
integration and cooperation in enhancing 
agricultural trade, while also pointing to 
potential trade gaps where more trade, or 
new trade relations, might be available, and 
where opportunities exist for value addition. 
An example of such an instance would be 
the case where Egypt is currently sourcing 
the bulk of its maize requirements from 
outside of Africa. There is also the need to 
capture and develop smaller intra-African 
trade volumes, particularly for products 
like sugar and fertiliser, as trade data 
from Zambia and other countries shows 
significant trade flows of these products, and 
the potential to expand these flows further 
within Africa. Findings from Angola, Kenya 

ACF African Competition Forum
367
and Zimbabwe, for example, also indicate 
untapped opportunities in expanding trade 
in agricultural products such as maize, sugar 
and soybeans across regional markets. 
The insights from the study suggest that 
coordinated efforts at various geographic 
levels can boost regional trade, improve 
food security, and promote a more African-
based development of agriculture.
1.2.	 COMMON BARRIERS OR LIMITATIONS TO 
AGRICULTURAL TRADE ACROSS AFRICAN 
COUNTRIES
27.	 The research has shown that there are 
several common barriers or limiting factors 
which impede the efficiency and growth of 
agricultural trade in the continent. 
28.	 One such barrier is infrastructure deficiencies, 
as poor transport networks and inadequate 
transportation 
infrastructure 
significantly 
hinder the movement of agricultural goods, 
leading to increased costs and delays. 
Zimbabwe, Zambia, Kenya, and Angola note 
the challenges regarding underdeveloped 
transport infrastructure, which impede their 
ability to efficiently move goods to markets 
and ports. Insufficient storage facilities, 
including cold storage for perishable goods, 
exacerbate post-harvest losses and reduce 
product quality, with Zimbabwe, Kenya, 
and Angola notably affected by inadequate 
storage infrastructure – which compromises 
the shelf life and marketability of their 
agricultural products. 
29.	 Another known limitation to increased 
agricultural trade relates to regulatory 
and bureaucratic barriers. Lengthy and 
complex procedures increase the time 
and cost of trading across borders. Kenya, 
Zambia, South Africa, and Angola frequently 
encounter bureaucratic hurdles that slow 
down trade processes, which complicate 
cross-border transactions. Stringent Sanitary 
and Phytosanitary (SPS) measures further 
restrict 
market 
access 
for 
agricultural 
exports. Compliance with these standards 
often requires significant investment in 
infrastructure and training, posing substantial 
challenges 
for 
Kenya, 
Zimbabwe, 
and 
Angola, which face difficulties meeting the 
high SPS standards required by international 
markets.
30.	 Tariff policies and tariffs pose a limitation to 
agricultural trade amongst African countries. 
Non-tariff barriers (NTBs), including quotas, 
import 
licenses, 
and 
other 
regulatory 
measures, limit the free flow of agricultural 
goods. All six countries experience various 
NTBs that complicate trade, particularly 
within regional economic communities like 
COMESA and SADC. High import tariffs on 
agricultural inputs, such as fertilisers and 
machinery, increase production costs and 
reduce competitiveness. Zambia, Zimbabwe, 
and Angola are particularly impacted by these 
high tariffs, which hinder their agricultural 
productivity and economic growth. There is 
a pressing need to review policies related 
to trade preferential or discriminatory 
pricing policies. Zambia's experiences with 
discriminatory pricing in the sugar industry 
are a case in point, highlighting the negative 
impacts on both market efficiency, pricing 
and fairness, especially for the trade in sugar, 
which is a key food commodity.
31.	 Market access issues have also come up as 
limiting the expansion of intra-regional trade 
in agriculture. Limited access to finance is a 
pervasive barrier across Kenya, Zimbabwe, 
Zambia, and Angola, constraining farmers 
and agricultural enterprises from investing 
in 
productivity-enhancing 
technologies 
and practices. Furthermore, a lack of timely 
and accurate market information prevents 
farmers from making informed decisions 
about production and marketing. Zimbabwe 
and Zambia, in particular, face significant 
gaps in market information systems, which 
adversely affect trade efficiency and market 
responsiveness.
32.	 Other factors affecting regional trade are 
climatic 
and 
environmental 
challenges. 

ACF African Competition Forum
368
Unpredictable weather patterns, droughts, 
and floods, exacerbated by climate change, 
adversely affect agricultural production, 
leading 
to 
supply 
instability. 
Kenya, 
Zimbabwe, Zambia, and Angola are highly 
susceptible to climate-related disruptions, 
which undermine their agricultural output 
and trade capabilities. Additionally, soil 
degradation and deforestation reduce land 
productivity in Zimbabwe and Zambia, 
further impacting agricultural yields and 
sustainability.
33.	 Competition and market dynamics also 
present a common barrier to enhancing 
agricultural 
trade 
in 
Africa. 
Market 
dominance and anti-competitive practices 
by large agribusinesses or cartels reduce 
competition and negatively affect prices. 
South Africa and Kenya have reported issues 
with market dominance in certain agricultural 
sectors. 
Disparities 
in 
regulations 
and 
standards between neighbouring countries 
can also lead to unfair competition and trade 
disputes, particularly affecting Kenya and 
Zambia within the East African Community 
(EAC) and COMESA trade blocs. The study 
points to a need for further understanding 
in relation to competition concerns, such 
as the protection of historical monopolies. 
For instance, the sugar sector n Kenya and 
Zambia illustrates how market concentration 
and 
preferential 
policies 
could 
limit 
competitive market dynamics.
1.3.	 TRADE LINKAGES FOR AGRICULTURAL 
PRODUCTS ACROSS AFRICAN COUNTRIES
34.	 The trade linkages across the selected 
countries for agricultural products are 
characterised by interdependencies that 
facilitate the flow of goods and services 
within the region. These countries engage in 
both intra-regional and extra-regional trade, 
leveraging 
their 
respective 
agricultural 
strengths to meet domestic and international 
demands. 
35.	 Zimbabwe, predominantly an exporter of tea, 
citrus fruits and sugar, maintains robust trade 
relations with Kenya, another key player in the 
tea sector. Both countries export their high-
quality tea to various international markets, 
including 
Europe 
and 
North America. 
However, 
intra-regional 
trade 
remains 
crucial, with Zimbabwe importing agricultural 
inputs, such as fertilisers from South Africa, 
to bolster its production capacities. This 
exchange illustrates the synergistic trade 
dynamics, where Zimbabwe’s agricultural 
output is supported by imported inputs from 
South Africa.. 
36.	 Angola, with its developing agriculture 
sector, imports a substantial portion of its 
agricultural products from neighbouring 
countries to meet domestic demand. South 
Africa and Zambia are significant suppliers 
of maize and other staple crops to Angola, 
facilitating food security and contributing 
to the country's gradual agricultural revival. 
These 
imports 
are 
critical, 
especially 
given Angola's ongoing efforts to build 
its agricultural infrastructure and reduce 
dependency on external food sources.
37.	 Kenya and Zimbabwe also illustrate strong 
trade linkages through the export of coffee 
and tea to regional markets, including South 
Africa and Mauritius. These trade routes 
are supported by established logistics 
networks and regulatory frameworks under 
regional agreements like COMESA and 
SADC. Moreover, the intra-regional trade 
in agricultural commodities such as maize, 
sugar, and horticultural products is enhanced 
by these countries’ participation in these 
regional economic communities, which aim 
to reduce trade barriers and harmonise 
standards.
38.	 South Africa emerges as a central hub in the 
region’s agricultural trade network, exporting 
a diverse range of products, including maize, 
sugar, and citrus fruits, to its neighbours. 
Zambia, a significant producer of maize, 
regularly exports this staple to Zimbabwe 

ACF African Competition Forum
369
and Kenya, addressing food security needs 
and mitigating production shortfalls in 
these countries. For instance, Kenya, faced 
with declining maize yields due to climate 
variability, relies heavily on maize imports 
from Zambia to stabilise its food supply and 
manage price volatility.
39.	 Similarly, Mauritius, despite its smaller 
agricultural base, plays a pivotal role in the 
regional sugar trade. The country imports 
significant quantities of sugar from South 
Africa and Zambia to satisfy its domestic 
needs, and processes it further for both 
local consumption and re-export. This trade 
linkage underscores Mauritius's strategic 
position within the regional sugar market, 
highlighting its capacity for value addition 
and its reliance on regional partners for raw 
materials. 
40.	 Another key linkage for the participating 
African countries relates to their common 
reliance on, and need for, key agricultural 
inputs such as fertilisers, seeds for sowing 
and oilseed cake or oil-cake. 
40.1.	In terms of fertilisers as a key input, 
the pricing of fertilisers is notably high 
across these countries, driven by global 
supply chain disruptions and local market 
conditions. For instance, in Zimbabwe, 
fertiliser prices have risen from around $497 
per ton in 2017 to $593 in 2021, and were 
influenced by international ammonia prices 
and local production constraints. In relation 
to intra-African trade, the study shows that 
fertiliser markets are oligopolistic across the 
countries, as they are dominated by a few 
large firms, leading to high prices. The intra-
African trade in fertilisers is significant, with 
countries like Zimbabwe importing primarily 
from South Africa. However, regional market 
dynamics and logistical issues often lead 
to higher prices, when compared to global 
averages. 
40.2.	The cost of seeds is another major input 
expense across these countries. Angola’s 
high seed costs are a result of heavy import 
reliance, 
while 
Zimbabwe 
faces 
high 
prices due to the dominance of hybrid 
and genetically modified seeds. South 
Africa’s seed market is heavily influenced 
by 
international 
mergers 
and 
market 
consolidation, leading to increased prices. 
Zambia has a mature seed industry, exporting 
maize seed annually within the SADC region. 
Intra-African trade plays a crucial role, with 
South Africa being a major exporter of seeds 
to the rest of Africa. Notably, Zambia exports 
maize seed to countries like Kenya, Malawi, 
and Zimbabwe, indicating strong regional 
trade relationships despite the higher prices 
compared to global standards.
40.3.	Oilcake (or oilseed cake) serves as an 
agricultural input due to it being rich 
in protein and minerals, and therefore 
commonly being utilised as animal feed. 
In Zambia, oilcake is a significant export 
commodity, with major importers including 
Zimbabwe, Kenya, and Tanzania. Prices for 
oilcake are higher in Zimbabwe compared to 
Kenya and South Africa, reflecting regional 
market dynamics where South Africa might 
benefit from substantial local production 
of oilcake, reducing its dependency on 
imports and therefore influencing pricing. 
Intra-African trade in oilcake is robust, with 
Zambia exporting primarily to neighbouring 
countries. Despite this, prices generally 
remain 
higher 
than 
global 
averages, 
influenced by regional production capacities 
and trade logistics.
40.4.	The high cost of agricultural inputs across 
these countries poses a significant barrier 
to improving agricultural productivity and 
affordability in Africa. Compared to global 
averages, African countries face higher 
prices for fertilisers, seeds for sowing, and 
oilseed cake. This disparity appears to be 
driven by regional market inefficiencies and 
higher transportation and logistical costs. 
40.5.	In relation to the market structure aspects 
of agricultural inputs, these markets are 

ACF African Competition Forum
370
often dominated by a few large firms, which 
can lead to anti-competitive practices and 
higher prices. In the fertiliser market, a few 
large multinational companies dominate, 
resulting in limited competition and higher 
prices. These firms tend to have established 
supply networks, and can access fertilisers 
and raw materials more easily than smaller, 
local companies. This dominance restricts 
market entry for new competitors, and 
can lead to anti-competitive practices. 
The seed market also shows signs of high 
concentration, as seen in South Africa for 
example. The seed market is influenced 
by 
international 
mergers 
and 
market 
consolidation, leading to increased prices. 
The oilseed cake markets in these counties 
also exhibit characteristics of an oligopolistic 
market structure, where the market is 
dominated by a few large firms, as seen in 
Zambia for example.
41.	 The agricultural trade linkages among 
Angola, Kenya, Mauritius, South Africa, 
Zambia, and Zimbabwe are multifaceted, 
reflecting the diverse agricultural landscapes 
and economic strategies of each country, 
as well as common key agricultural inputs 
required for intra-African trade. These 
linkages are essential for addressing food 
security and fostering regional integration. 
The mutual dependencies and cooperative 
trade practices underscore the importance 
of 
regional 
collaboration 
in 
achieving 
sustainable agricultural development and 
economic growth.
1.4.	 COMPETITION ISSUES AND IMPLICATIONS 
FOR ENFORCEMENT IN AGRICULTURE 
AND FOOD MARKETS
42.	 Competition issues in agricultural trade 
and 
agro-processing 
across 
Angola, 
Kenya, Mauritius, South Africa, Zambia, and 
Zimbabwe reveal significant challenges that 
affect market efficiency, pricing, and overall 
economic development. These issues are 
rooted in structural inefficiencies, regulatory 
environments, and market dynamics, which 
collectively hinder competitive practices 
within these countries.
43.	 In South Africa, the agricultural sector is 
characterised by a high degree of market 
concentration, where large agribusinesses 
and cartels have dominated key markets 
such as maize, sugar, and citrus fruits. This 
dominance 
stifles 
competition, 
leading 
to 
price 
manipulation 
and 
reduced 
market access for smaller producers. The 
Competition Commission of South Africa has 
identified such anti-competitive practices, 
and has taken steps towards addressing 
these issues through investigations and 
regulatory and legislative interventions, such 
as amending the Competition Act in 2019 to 
strengthen its powers – aimed at promoting 
fair 
competition, 
addressing 
market 
concentration and protecting consumer 
interests.
44.	 Kenya similarly noted competition challenges 
in its agricultural markets, particularly within 
the coffee and tea sectors. The presence of 
large cooperatives and marketing agencies, 
which control significant portions of the 
supply chain, results in limited market entry 
for smaller farmers and producers. These 
entities often set prices and control market 
access, which can lead to monopolistic 
practices and reduced incentives for quality 
improvements. The Competition Authority 
of Kenya has been active in addressing 
these issues by promoting transparency, 
and advocating for regulatory reforms to 
enhance market competition.
45.	 In Zimbabwe, competition in the agricultural 
sector is impeded by regulatory barriers and 
inefficient market structures. The dominance 
of state-owned enterprises in the marketing 
and distribution of key agricultural inputs and 
outputs limits the opportunities for private 
sector participation and competition. For 
instance, the Grain Marketing Board's control 
over maize marketing creates monopolistic 
conditions that affect pricing and market 
access. Efforts by the Competition and 

ACF African Competition Forum
371
Tariff Commission of Zimbabwe to liberalise 
the market and encourage private sector 
involvement 
are 
crucial 
steps 
toward 
fostering a more competitive environment.
46.	 Zambia faces similar competition issues, 
particularly in the fertiliser and maize sectors. 
The market for agricultural inputs such as 
fertilisers is highly concentrated, with a few 
dominant firms controlling the majority of 
supply. This concentration leads to higher 
prices and reduced availability of inputs 
for 
smallholder 
farmers. The 
Zambian 
Competition 
and 
Consumer 
Protection 
Commission has recognised these challenges 
and is working towards increasing market 
competition through regulatory measures 
(such as cartel investigations and fines, 
representations to revise technical barriers 
to sugar imports, and implementing tax 
incentives to promote local manufacturing 
over imports),  and promoting fair trading 
practices.
47.	 Mauritius also contends with competition 
issues in its sugar industry. The concentration 
of sugar production and processing within 
a few large firms limits market entry and 
competition. This scenario is compounded 
by trade policies that protect domestic 
producers at the expense of market efficiency. 
The Competition Commission of Mauritius 
has been proactive in addressing these 
issues, by investigating anti-competitive 
practices and advocating for policy reforms 
to open up the market.
48.	 In Angola, key competition issues affecting 
the agricultural sector relate mainly to 
market concentration and mergers, the high 
informal market presence, and economic 
policies favouring national production. There 
have been several mergers and acquisitions 
in the agricultural sector, involving national 
and foreign companies, and vertical and 
horizontal 
integrations, 
which 
did 
not 
present immediate harm to competition but 
need ongoing monitoring to prevent the 
formation of monopolies. The prevalence 
of 
informal 
markets 
undermines 
the 
formal agricultural market, leading to price 
distortions and unfair competition for formal 
businesses. There are policies which aim to 
promote national production but can restrict 
competition, where incentives and credit 
facilities favour national producers and can 
create inequalities.
49.	 The competition issues in the agricultural 
markets of these countries highlight the key 
role of competition enforcement in promoting 
fair, efficient, and dynamic agricultural 
markets. Effective competition enforcement 
requires a multifaceted approach, including 
regulatory reforms, market diversification, 
anti-trust interventions, and regional policy 
harmonisation. 
50.	 The scope for enforcement by competition 
authorities 
could 
include 
prioritising 
regular market audits and encouraging the 
entry of new players to diversify markets, 
policy changes that reduce state control 
and open up markets to private sector 
participation, 
targeted 
interventions 
to 
reduce dependency on a few dominant 
suppliers, as well as the promotion of fair-
trading practices and foreign investment 
to stimulate competition and growth. The 
study highlights the necessity for a deeper 
understanding of competition issues by 
competition 
authorities, 
including 
the 
preferential treatment and safeguarding 
of longstanding monopolies. For example, 
the sugar industry in Kenya and Zambia 
demonstrates how market concentration and 
favourable policies may restrict competitive 
market forces.
51.	 There are information platforms, such as 
the African Market Observatory (AMO) 
price tracker which collects and monitors 
prices of selected staple food products in 
East and Southern Africa, as well as early 

ACF African Competition Forum
372
warning systems for food price data,2 that 
could be utilised and expanded within the 
continent, to assist authorities in developing 
market intelligence for informed decision-
making and policy formulation, as well as 
identifying market failures and facilitating 
benchmarking and enhanced trend analysis. 
By addressing these challenges, and utilising 
existing agriculture price information and 
trend sources, competition authorities can 
contribute 
towards 
promoting 
regional 
integration, enhancing market efficiency, 
and promoting agricultural development 
across these countries. Further, considering 
the potential for cross-border cartels and 
mergers, the chances of a wider impact 
from anticompetitive behaviour or mergers 
are significant. Consequently, enhanced 
cooperation 
between 
competition 
authorities in the investigation of mergers 
and conduct is crucial.
1.5.	 OPPORTUNITIES FOR INCREASED 
REGIONAL TRADE IN AFRICA
52.	 The opportunities for increased regional 
trade in agriculture and agro-processing 
across African countries are substantial, and 
can enhance economic integration, food 
security, and sustainable development in 
the region. These opportunities stem from 
complementary 
agricultural 
strengths, 
strategic geographic locations, and existing 
regional frameworks that facilitate trade.
53.	 The study reveals a number of insights 
that could facilitate and boost trade in 
Africa, namely leveraging complementary 
production, enhancing trade facilitation 
and infrastructure, harmonising regulatory 
frameworks, promoting agro-processing and 
value addition, strengthening regional trade 
agreements, improving access to finance, 
adopting 
climate-resilient 
agricultural 
2	
Consumers International, in collaboration with BFAP, developed a tool that acts as an early warning system by 
comparing monthly food price margins (as the difference between wholesale and retail prices) against the previous six 
months (or any other specified period) to detect deviations and can flag margins that are within one or two standard 
deviations relative to the previous specified period.
practices, and improving market information 
systems. These aspects are covered briefly 
below. 
53.1.	 Leveraging 
complementary 
production. 
Countries can capitalise on their unique 
production strengths and complementary 
agricultural outputs to enhance regional 
trade. 
For 
example, 
Zambia's 
surplus 
maize production can be channelled more 
effectively to meet Kenya's and Zimbabwe's 
maize deficits. Similarly, Kenya's robust 
horticultural sector can supply fruits and 
vegetables 
to 
neighbouring 
countries, 
addressing regional food security needs 
and boosting economic interactions.
53.2.	 Enhancing 
trade 
facilitation 
and 
infrastructure. Investing in infrastructure 
improvements and streamlining customs 
procedures can significantly reduce trade 
costs and improve the efficiency of cross-
border trade. Collaborative infrastructure 
projects, such as upgrading transport 
corridors and developing regional storage 
facilities, can facilitate smoother trade 
flows between landlocked countries like 
Zambia and coastal nations like Kenya and 
South Africa. These efforts can ensure more 
reliable and cost-effective transport of 
agricultural products.
53.3.	 Harmonising 
regulatory 
frameworks. 
Standardising sanitary and phytosanitary 
(SPS) measures and reducing non-tariff 
barriers (NTBs) can enhance market access 
for agricultural products. Implementing 
regional standards for agricultural products 
can simplify compliance and reduce delays 
at borders, making it easier for countries like 
Zimbabwe and Zambia to export to South 
Africa and other markets. Harmonised 
regulations can streamline trade processes 
and foster greater market integration.

ACF African Competition Forum
373
53.4.	 Promoting 
agro-processing 
and 
value 
addition. Encouraging investment in agro-
processing facilities can help countries 
move up the value chain, increasing export 
revenues and reducing dependency on raw 
commodity exports. Mauritius's success in 
agro-processing can serve as a model for 
other countries. Investing in processing 
plants for sugar, coffee, and tea in Kenya and 
Zimbabwe can add value to exports, create 
more jobs, and stimulate local economies.
53.5.	 Strengthening regional trade agreements. 
Deepening 
integration 
within 
regional 
trade blocs like COMESA, SADC, and the 
EAC can enhance trade cooperation and 
reduce barriers. Strengthening the African 
Continental Free Trade Area (AfCFTA) can 
open new markets and provide a larger 
platform for regional agricultural exports, 
benefiting 
all 
participating 
countries. 
Effective 
implementation 
of 
these 
agreements can facilitate smoother and 
more extensive trade interactions.
53.6.	 Improving access to finance. Expanding 
access to affordable financing for farmers 
and agribusinesses can boost agricultural 
productivity and trade capacity. Regional 
financial institutions can develop tailored 
credit products for smallholder farmers in 
Zambia and Kenya, facilitating investments 
in improved seeds, fertilisers, and farming 
equipment. Access to finance is crucial 
for enabling farmers to adopt modern 
agricultural 
practices 
and 
enhance 
productivity.
53.7.	 Climate-resilient 
agricultural 
practices. 
Promoting 
climate-resilient 
farming 
techniques can help mitigate the impact of 
climate change on agricultural production, 
ensuring more stable trade flows. Sharing 
best 
practices 
and 
technologies 
for 
drought-resistant crops and efficient water 
management 
can 
enhance 
resilience 
in countries like Zimbabwe and Kenya. 
Regional 
cooperation 
in 
climate-smart 
agriculture can strengthen the capacity 
of farmers to adapt to changing climatic 
conditions.
53.8.	 Enhancing market information systems. 
Developing 
robust 
market 
information 
systems can help farmers and traders 
make 
informed 
decisions, 
improving 
market efficiency and trade outcomes. 
Implementing 
regional 
platforms 
for 
sharing real-time market data, prices, and 
trade opportunities can benefit producers 
and traders in South Africa, Kenya, and other 
countries. Access to accurate and timely 
market information is essential for optimiing 
trade strategies and responding to market 
demands.
54.	 Further, there are also strategic opportunities 
for furthering intra-regional trade in Africa 
that stem or relate more directly to each 
country, and their trade linkages to other 
African countries. Some examples of these 
opportunities are described below.
54.1.	South Africa, for example, has a diversified 
agricultural base that presents significant 
opportunities for regional trade. South 
Africa is a major producer of maize, 
sugar, and citrus fruits, which are in high 
demand across the region. Enhanced trade 
agreements and reduced non-tariff barriers 
could facilitate greater exports of these 
products to neighbouring countries like 
Angola, which imports substantial quantities 
of maize to meet its domestic needs.
54.2.	Kenya, with its robust tea and coffee sectors, 
can capitalise on increased regional trade 
by exporting these high-value crops to 
countries like Zimbabwe and South Africa. 
Kenya's strategic location within the EAC, 
and its well-developed port infrastructure, 
provide a strong foundation for expanding 
trade. 
54.3.	Mauritius has a well-established sugar 
processing industry that offers significant 
regional trade potential. Mauritius can 
import raw sugar from countries like 

ACF African Competition Forum
374
Zambia and Zimbabwe, where sugarcane 
production is substantial, and process it for 
re-export within the region. This not only 
adds value to the raw agricultural products, 
but also strengthens regional trade ties and 
economic integration.
54.4.		Zambia, a key producer of maize and 
other staple crops, can enhance its role as 
a regional food supplier by expanding its 
export markets to neighbouring countries 
experiencing food deficits. Improved trade 
logistics and infrastructure investments are 
crucial to supporting this expansion.
54.5.		Zimbabwe, with its significant agricultural 
potential, particularly in the production of 
tobacco, maize and tea, can benefit from 
increased regional trade by diversifying 
its export destinations. By integrating 
more deeply into regional value chains 
and improving market access through 
harmonised trade policies, Zimbabwe can 
stabilise its agricultural sector and boost 
foreign exchange earnings.
54.6.		Angola's 
agricultural 
sector 
presents 
opportunities for both imports and exports. 
As Angola builds its agricultural capacity, 
it can import essential agricultural inputs 
and technologies from more developed 
regional markets like South Africa and 
Kenya. Concurrently, Angola can export 
surplus 
agricultural 
products 
such 
as 
coffee and tropical fruits to neighbouring 
countries, fostering a mutually beneficial 
trade environment. Additionally, adding 
value through processing local agriculture 
produce can increase export value and 
create jobs.

of the main points and findings across

characteristics, trade opportunities, common 
trends and challenges, as well as key insights 
or potential recommendations for enhancing 
regional agricultural trade.

56.	
The opportunities for increased regional trade 
in agriculture and agro-processing among 
countries like Angola, Kenya, Mauritius, 
South Africa, Zambia, and Zimbabwe are 
significant, and are not limited to only large-
volume trade, as the study has shown there 
are untapped opportunities for expanding 
trade in agricultural products such as maize, 
sugar and soybeans across regional markets. 
By leveraging complementary agricultural 
strengths, strategic geographic positions, 
and existing regional trade frameworks, 
these countries (and Africa more broadly) 
can enhance economic integration, improve 
food security, and promote increased 
agricultural trade.
 

ACF African Competition Forum
377
57.	 The ACF analysis of trade flow patterns and 
regional value chains in the agriculture and 
agro-processing sectors across the selected 
countries, reveals a comprehensive story of 
challenges and opportunities, trade patterns 
and linkages, key findings and competition 
issues which shape the regional trade 
landscape. 
58.	 Agriculture 
continues 
to 
serve 
as 
a 
foundation for many African economies, as it 
is key for aiding food security, employment, 
and economic growth. The sector's potential 
to enhance regional trade is limited by 
challenges such as market access barriers, 
climate 
change, 
and 
anti-competitive 
practices. The study conducted under the 
African Competition Forum (ACF) provides 
insights into trade flows and competition 
issues, emphasising the importance of 
regional and continental value chains for 
sustainable development.
59.	 The 
findings 
illustrate 
the 
need 
for 
leveraging 
complementary 
production 
strengths, enhancing trade facilitation and 
infrastructure, 
harmonising 
regulatory 
frameworks, 
promoting 
agro-processing, 
and improving access to finance. For instance, 
Zambia's surplus maize can address deficits 
in Kenya and Zimbabwe, while Mauritius’s 
agro-processing success serves as a model 
for other countries. The implementation 
of regional trade agreements like AfCFTA 
can further reduce barriers and open new 
markets, enhancing economic integration 
and growth.
60.	 Competition 
issues, 
including 
market 
concentration and the dominance of large 
players, 
hinder 
market 
efficiency 
and 
development, while prices are kept high 
and entry into agriculture markets is limited. 
South Africa and Kenya need to dismantle 
concentrated structures to limit exclusionary 
effects, while Zimbabwe and Zambia require 
regulatory reforms to facilitate market entry 
and private sector participation. Addressing 
these issues through targeted interventions, 
and promoting fair trading practices, is 
essential for fostering more competitive 
markets.
61.	 Common barriers to agricultural trade 
include 
infrastructure 
deficiencies, 
regulatory hurdles, and tariff policies. Poor 
transport networks, inadequate storage 
facilities, and complex customs procedures 
increase costs and delay trade in agriculture 
and agro-processing. Non-tariff barriers and 
high import tariffs further complicate trade, 
particularly within regional communities 
like COMESA and SADC. Climate and 
environmental 
challenges, 
along 
with 
competition and market dynamics, also 
impact agricultural trade.
62.	 Trade linkages among the six countries are 
characterised by interdependencies and 
mutual 
dependencies, 
highlighting 
the 
importance of regional collaboration. South 
Africa's role as a major trading hub, Zambia's 
maize exports, and Mauritius's strategic 
sugar trade illustrate these connections. 
Enhancing these linkages through improved 
infrastructure, 
regulatory 
harmonisation, 
and investment in agro-processing can 
significantly boost regional trade.
63.	 The analysis of agricultural trade patterns 
across the different regions, along with 
regional and product-related trade insights, 
also offers the opportunity for additional, 
future research into certain problematic food 
value chains like fertiliser and agricultural 
inputs, and food value chains where there 
2
CONCLUSION    

ACF African Competition Forum
378
are currently trade gaps (such as with maize), 
or simply those food value chains that 
warrant additional analysis going forward, 
like the sugar value chain – where significant 
trade imbalances and opportunities for 
value addition exist, or the tea value chain 
that holds potential for premium product 
development and market diversification. 
64.	 In 
conclusion, 
addressing 
competition 
issues and utilising trade opportunities 
requires coordinated efforts, regulatory and 
policy reforms, as well as investments in 
infrastructure and technology. By enhancing 
regional 
cooperation 
and 
integrating 
agricultural value chains, the countries in 
this study (and then more broadly in Africa) 
can achieve increased trade across different 
geographical levels, which would support 
sustainable growth, improved food security, 
as well as foster a more integrated and 
prosperous regional trade environment.
